# AVEBURY,
->A<-
# TEMPLE
->OF THE<-
## British DRUIDS,
->With Some Others,<-
### DESCRIBED.
->Wherein is a more detailed account of the first and patriarchal<-
->religion; and of the settlement of the BRITISH ISLANDS.<-
---
->Although slow-moving age hinders me, and many things escape me that I saw in my early years, yet I remember more <note>original Latin: "Quamvis obstet mihi tarda vetustas, Multaque me fugiant primis spectata sub annis, Plura tamen memini" — from Ovid's Metamorphoses, Book 12, lines 182-183. Stukeley uses this quote to reflect on the challenges of recovering ancient history from the distant past.</note> — Ovid, Metamorphoses XII. v. 182.<-
---
->By **WILLIAM STUKELEY, M.D.** <note>William Stukeley (1687–1765) was a pioneering English antiquary who was among the first to perform field surveys of Stonehenge and Avebury. Though his observations were meticulous, his theories—like attributing these sites to the Druids—are now considered historically inaccurate as the sites predate the Druids by thousands of years.</note><-
->Rector <note>A member of the clergy in charge of a parish.</note> of *All-Saints* in Stamford.<-
---
->LONDON:<-
->Printed for the AUTHOR: And Sold by *W. Innys, R. Manby, B. Dod,*<-
->*J. Brindley,* and the Booksellers in London.<-
->1743.<-

<summary>This title page introduces William Stukeley's landmark study of the Avebury stone circle, which he mistakenly attributed to the ancient Druids as part of an original "patriarchal" religion. The work is significant for its early archaeological documentation of British prehistoric sites.</summary>

### To the RIGHT HONOURABLE
# HENRY
## EARL of *PEMBROKE*, etc. etc.

RIGHT HONOURABLE,

In a family that has, throughout history, been a notable friend to the Muses <term>Muses: The nine Greek goddesses of inspiration in literature, science, and the arts; often used to represent the creative arts and scholarship.</term>, I consider myself fortunate to have a personal connection. To you, my Lord, this dedication is passed down by hereditary right. Through your father’s sponsorship <term>original: auspices; meaning protection or support.</term> and encouragement, I began and continued this work. He was always pleased to view my humble <note>original: mean; here meaning modest or of low status, rather than unkind.</note> efforts with favor, and to assist me from the inexhaustible wealth of his own knowledge in all kinds of ancient

...cient learning; and promised to patronize it when published.

But if anything within these pages is acceptable to the public, they are indebted to Your Lordship for its being published sooner than I had intended myself. Out of that innate love of literature <note>original: letters; referring to scholarly learning and the arts.</note> which inspires the PEMBROKES, you thought it fit to prompt and encourage me to print this work. Your Lordship’s judgment will serve as a favorable endorsement <note>original: agreeable prejudice; meaning a positive pre-judgment or bias that helps the author's reputation.</note> in my favor, as you have cultivated your own excellent talents through your own hard work and through everything that can be learned from a careful observation <note>original: curious view; meaning scholarly or inquisitive study.</note> of the antiquities of *Italy*. You are, in every sense, a master of that immense treasure of *Greek* and *Roman* marbles which make *Wilton* <term>Wilton: Wilton House, the country seat of the Earls of Pembroke, famous for its art collection and architecture.</term> a Rome beyond the mountains <note>original: Tramontane Rome; 'Tramontane' refers to the area north of the Alps; here it means a center of classical culture located in England.</note>.

In addition to that learning which is the ornament of the present age, Your Lordship knows how to truly value the antiquities specific to your own country. Even if they lack <note>original: want; here meaning to lack or be without.</note> some of the refinement of the *Augustan* times <term>Augustan times: The era of the Roman Emperor Augustus, considered a golden age of art and literature.</term>, or that of *Alexander the Great*; they still possess their own beauties, and even elegances, which appeal to a taste as exquisite as Your Lordship’s. There is a symmetry and harmony of parts, an amazing grandeur in the design, and the incredible force of the mechanical powers <term>mechanick powers: The physical forces or engineering techniques used to move and place massive stones, such as in ancient monuments.</term> employed in them, the most

It sits amidst what Jones built there in person and what Your Lordship has since added, which so harmonizes with the original as to make the design of that great genius complete.

Your Lordship's knowledge of architecture is so rare and vast <note>original: uncommon and unconfin'd</note> that *Great Britain* now sees a bridge arising, primarily under your direction, that is superior to any produced by the *Roman* Empire at the peak of its power. And the River *Thames*—which so recently rescued the *Danube* from French <note>original: gallic; a reference to the British victory over the French at the Battle of Blenheim near the Danube.</note> tyranny—now boasts a nobler ornament than the bridge that the Emperor *Trajan* <term>Trajan: Roman Emperor (reigned 98–117 AD) who built a famous bridge over the Danube, then the longest in the world.</term> built across that famous river.

That praiseworthy passion <note>original: commendable ardour</note> of mind, which in your younger years led you to study people and customs <note>original: men and manners</note>, places and things in foreign countries, you now employ for the benefit of your own country through the practice of civil and military arts. Your Lordship balances <note>original: tempers</note> that love of...

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface of the work, establishing the author's philosophical and historical framework for studying Druidry as a link to the original "patriarchal" religion of humanity.</meta>

---

# PREFACE.

---

History is political wisdom; philosophy is religious wisdom. The former consists of the knowledge of memorable events and the application of 그 knowledge to the good conduct of life: in embracing the good and avoiding the ill consequences and examples of certain actions. Likewise, the latter teaches us to entertain worthy notions of the Supreme Being and to study how to obtain His favor—which is the ultimate goal of all human and divine wisdom. Religion is the means used to arrive at this purpose. In order to be satisfied as to what constitutes true religion, we must return to the fountainhead as much as possible. The first religion is undoubtedly true, as it came immediately from God.

When I first began these studies regarding Druid antiquities, I plainly discerned that the religion professed in these places was the original, simple, patriarchal religion. This led me to judge it worthwhile to pursue my inquiries <note>original: "prosecute my enquiries"</note> about them, as a matter of the most interesting and important nature. Knowledge is the glory of a human being, and divine knowledge is the glory of a Christian. What I have accomplished in this volume is a further pursuit of the plan <term>scheme</term> <note>In this context, "scheme" refers to a systematic project or intellectual framework rather than a plot.</note> I have laid down for this purpose. The noble person to whom this book is dedicated induced me to hasten its publication, suggesting the shortness of human life and expressing a favorable opinion of the work.

I was willing to seize the first opportunity to communicate to the world the pleasure of contemplating such noble antiquities—which we enjoy on our own island—before it becomes too late to see them. My endeavor in this work is to reveal the times of the first settling <note>original: "planting," meaning the colonization or repopulation of the earth.</note> of the world after the Flood; the spread of true religion alongside mankind; the deviation into idolatry; and the people who built the various kinds

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that studying Druid antiquities reveals the "patriarchal religion"—the original, pure faith given by God to humanity after the Flood before it was corrupted by idolatry.</summary>
<keywords>History, Philosophy, Religion, Druids, Patriarchal Religion, Antiquities, Idolatry, Great Flood</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the original religion of the world was a "pure and simple" patriarchal faith without idols, which was preserved by the Druids in Britain longer than elsewhere.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...of patriarchal temples, such as we see here, and in the more eastern parts of the world. This includes the early settlers of Great Britain in particular and the connection that exists between the East and West in matters of religion. All of this shows that there was only one religion at first: pure and simple.

Pausanias, in his *Description of Corinth*, <note>original: "Pausanias in Corinthiac."</note> writes: "The Phliasians, one of the most ancient colonies in Greece, had a very holy temple in which there was no image, either visible to the public or kept in secret." He mentions the same regarding a grove or temple of Hebe <note>The Greek goddess of youth</note> belonging to that people, and adds, "They give a mystical reason for it." I suspect the mystery is that it followed the original patriarchal manner. The same author says in his book on Argolis <note>original: "argol."</note> "that at Prona is a temple of Vesta with no image, only an altar on which they sacrifice." The ancient Etruscans <note>original: "Hetruscans"</note> ordained by law that there should be no statues in their temples. Lucian, in *On the Syrian Goddess*, <note>original: "Lucian de dea Syr."</note> writes, "The ancient temples in Egypt had no statues." Plutarch, in his *Life of Numa*, and Clement of Alexandria, in the first book of his *Miscellanies*, <note>original: "Clemens Alexan. strom. I."</note> remark "that Numa, the second king of Rome, made express orders against the use of images in the worship of the deity." Plutarch adds "that for the first 170 years after the building of the city, the Romans used no images, but believed the deity to be invisible." Similarly, until the days of Silius Italicus and Philostratus, at the temple of Hercules—the settler of Britain—at Gades <note>Modern-day Cádiz, Spain</note>, the old patriarchal method of religion was observed, as Bishop Cumberland takes notice in his work on *Sanchoniathon* (page 266).

> "But there were no images, nor any known likenesses of the gods." <note>original Latin: "Sed nulla effigies, simulachrave nota deorum."</note> — Silius Italicus, Book III.

And our British Druids had no images. Whatever we find in history that looks like idolatry among them should not be attributed to the original <term>aboriginal</term> Druids, but to the later colonies from mainland Europe <note>original: "the continent"</note>.

Likewise, I have demonstrated a significant connection between the patriarchal family—specifically that of Abraham—and the first settlers of the coasts on the Atlantic Ocean of Spain, Gaul <note>Modern-day France</note>, Germany, and Britain. It is plain that the religion first established here—in what was then an almost inaccessible island—flourished exceedingly and kept to its original system even until the days of Julius Caesar; I mean among the original inhabitants. The newer settlers from the continent, located on the southern and eastern shores of the island, were at least influenced by <note>original: "tinctured"</note> idolatry in later times.

<summary>The author argues that the original, "patriarchal" religion of humanity was characterized by an absence of idols and statues, citing numerous ancient Greek, Roman, and Egyptian sources to show this was the case before later corruption. He asserts that the original Druids in Britain preserved this pure, image-less worship until the Roman era.</summary>
<keywords>Pausanias, Numa, Druids, idolatry, patriarchal religion, Hercules, Gades, Britain, Abraham, ancient temples</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, specifically arguing that Britain's ancient stone monuments are remnants of a pure, original religion practiced by the Druids, which predated the corruption of idolatry seen elsewhere.</meta>

<page-num>iii</page-num>

# PREFACE

...times. While on the European continent, where more frequent changes of inhabitants occur, idolatry polluted the land everywhere. However, in all accounts of the very beginnings of nations, they initially practiced the original religion—until, as happens everywhere, time, wealth, sophistication <note>original: "politeness"</note>, and prosperity brought about corruption in both church and state.

On the continent, we find that idolatry gradually crept in universally. This prompted Providence <note>A term for God’s divine intervention or guidance in human history.</note> to intervene through the *Mosaic* dispensation <note>original: "Mosaick dispensation"; the religious system and laws given by God to Moses.</note>. In doing so, the nature of those temples, which had become entirely polluted, was changed. Nevertheless, we have no reason to doubt that the *Druids* on our island generally maintained the purity of their original and patriarchal institution. This is why all the classical writers—though writing much later than the era we are discussing—represent them as a people whose religion was diametrically opposite to that of the rest of the world, just as the *Jews* were then, or *Christians* were afterward.

Therefore, I believed it was fully worth the effort to devote some labor to these temples of theirs. They are the only remaining monuments we have of the patriarchal religion, especially considering their extraordinary grandeur and magnificence, which equal any of the most famous wonders of the world, as they are commonly called.

I have shown through enough evidence that these kinds of temples were built all over the world in the earliest times. However, it is probable that nothing remains of them today that can compare to those in our own island. We ought, therefore, to try to rescue them from being forgotten <note>original: "oblivion"</note> before it is too late.

I plan to publish only one more volume to complete this argument, as far as I have materials for that purpose. I view what I have done so far as very imperfect; it is merely opening the scene of this very noble subject. Curious readers will find plenty of room to expand, correct, and adorn the plan I have begun. I believe it is well worth the effort, as it provides an excellent view of the scheme of Providence in conducting the affairs of true religion through the various ages of the world. From this, we can discern the great purpose of introducing the *Mosaic* dispensation on the very spot of ground where the bulk of idolatry began and spread over the Western and more sophisticated world. It was over this same world that Providence raised the mighty *Roman* Empire to pave the way for the republication <note>The re-introduction or restoration of the original faith (Christianity).</note> of the patriarchal religion.

<sig>b</sig>

<summary>The author argues that the Druids in Britain preserved a pure "patriarchal religion" while the rest of the world fell into idolatry, and he views British stone temples as essential monuments of this ancient faith. He suggests that history, including the rise of the Roman Empire, was directed by Providence to eventually restore this original religion through Christianity.</summary>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface, shifting from the discussion of Druidical temples to Britain's spiritual destiny and the power of written history to preserve ancient knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>iv</page-num>

## PREFACE.

We may draw this general reflection from the present work: that the true religion has primarily existed in our island since the repopulating of mankind after the flood. It is here that we achieved the best reform from that universal corruption of Christianity, Roman Catholicism <note>original: "popery"</note>. Here, God’s ancient people, the *Jews*, live in the most peaceful circumstances found anywhere on earth, and from this place, they are most likely to encounter the conversion intended for them. If we could only reform ourselves from the abominable public profanation of the Sabbath and the habit of common swearing, we might hope for what many learned men have predicted: that here, the glory of Christ’s kingdom on earth was to be revealed.

I have made it sufficiently clear that the *Apollo* of the ancients was actually *Phut*, the son of *Ham* <note>original: "Cham"; in the Bible, Phut is a son of Ham and grandson of Noah.</note>. I have also shown the reader how he may form a perfect idea of this man’s appearance by looking at the countless monuments of antiquity still to be seen. I have pursued that fascinating topic through many of the ancient patriarchs before and after *Phut*, so as to recover their heroic images <note>original: "effigies"</note>, or at least their likenesses. I hope that I may find an opportunity to publish these at some point.

I shall conclude my preface with a piece of old poetry, consisting of several vigorous <note>original: "nervous"; in this context, it means strong or robust in style.</note> lines written in a rather impressive style. These were written on our subject a hundred years ago by *Samuel Daniel*, a member of the household <note>original: "domestick"</note> of Queen *Anne*, the wife of King *James I*. The curious reader will observe a remarkable delicacy in the sentiments throughout: a struggle between time and the greatness of these ancient works, matched by the effort of written language to recover and preserve the memory of them—which their founders, though well-qualified to do so, neglected.

> O Blessed letters <note>Here, "letters" refers to written language and literature.</note>, that combine in one
> *All ages past; and make one live with all!*
> Make us consult with those who now are gone,
> *And the dead living unto counsel call!*
> *By you the unborn shall have communion*
> *Of what we feel, and what does us befall.*

> Soul of the world, knowledge, without you,
> What has the earth that truly glorious is?
> Why should our pride make such a stir to be,
> To be forgot? What good is like to this:
> To do something worthy of the writing, and to write
> Something worthy of the reading, and the world's delight!

<summary>The author reflects on Britain's religious significance and identifies the Greek god Apollo as the biblical Phut, son of Ham. He concludes with a poem by Samuel Daniel praising the power of writing to preserve history from oblivion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface by Samuel Daniel, illustrating the transience of physical monuments compared to the enduring nature of knowledge and virtue. It follows the author's introductory remarks about Daniel's poetic style.</meta>

<page-num>v</page-num>

# P R E F A C E.

*You mighty lords, who with respected grace,*
*Stand at the helm <note>original: "stern"</note> of good example;*
*And the entire body of this population,*
*Guide with a mere turn of your hand:*
*Keep a steady course, avoid all disgrace,*
*And observe the peak of glory for our land.*

*Hold up disgraced knowledge from the ground,*
*Keep virtue in high demand, and give worth its due.*
*Do not let neglect and crude <note>original: "barbarous"</note> methods destroy*
*So beautiful a good, bringing back the darkness once more.*
*Be not—oh, be not found an accomplice*
*To the death of that which must give life to you.*

*Where will you have your virtuous names safely kept?*
*In gorgeous tombs, or in secure, sacred chambers?*
*Do you not see how those fallen ruins <note>original: "prostrate heaps"</note> betrayed*
*Your ancestors' bones, and could not keep them safe?*
*And will you trust deceitful stones, however beautifully laid,*
*And think they will be more faithful to your honor?*

*No, no; unsparing time will proudly issue*
*A warrant for destruction <note>original: "warrant unto wreck"</note> that, with a single frown,*
*Will tear apart all these mockeries of vanity,*
*And make them as they were before: uncelebrated and unknown.*
*Poor, empty honors that can barely protect*
*Your memories, which cannot even hold onto their own!*

*And what purpose does that wondrous monument <note>original: "trophy"</note> serve now,*
*Which stands on the grand plain near Wilton? <note>The author refers to Stonehenge, located on the Salisbury Plain near the town of Wilton.</note>*
*That huge, silent heap cannot tell us how,*
*Nor what, nor from where it came, nor by whose hands it was built,*
*Nor for whose glory it was set on display—*
*Showing how much our pride mocks the pride of other lands.*

*Upon which, when the gazing traveler <note>original: "passenger"</note>*
*Has looked eagerly with wonder,*
*And would desperately love to know its origin and what it was—*
*How it was erected there, and how long ago—*
*He inquires and asks his fellow traveler*
*What he has heard, and what his opinion is!*

<summary>In this poem, Samuel Daniel argues that physical monuments like Stonehenge are ultimately "dumb heaps" that fail to preserve history, urging the nobility to instead value knowledge and virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Stonehenge, Samuel Daniel, Wilton, transience, virtue, knowledge, monuments, vanity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Samuel Daniel’s poem (likely from his 1599 work *Musophilus*) regarding the mysterious origins of Stonehenge and the failure of physical monuments to preserve human history. The catchword "ABURY" suggests the next page will discuss Avebury.</meta>

<page-num>vi</page-num>

# P R E F A C E.

*He knows nothing; then he turns back again,*
*And looks and sighs, and then admires it all over again,*
*And within himself, he sorrowfully complains*
*About the misery of dark forgetfulness.*
*He is angry with time, because nothing remains*
*To explain the wonder of our greatest wonders.*

*Then ignorance, with mythical <note>original: "fabulous"</note> stories,*
*Robbing beautiful art and skill <note>original: "cunning"</note> of their rightful credit,*
*Tells how those stones were, by the devil's power,*
*Brought from Africa to Ireland in a single night;*
*And from there to Britain, by a magical path,*
*Taken from the hands of giants by Merlin's trickery <note>original: "sleight"</note>.*

*And then placed near Amesbury <note>original: "Ambry"</note> in memory*
*Of all those noble Britons murdered there*
*By Hengist and his treacherous Saxons,*
*Who came to negotiate <note>original: "parle"</note> in peace, catching them off guard.*
*With this old legend, then, gullibility <note>original: "credulity"</note>*
*Is satisfied and stops searching for the truth.*

*And as for you, you huge and mighty structure,*
*Who stands so damaged by the spite of time,*
*And provides no evidence to preserve the fame*
*Of those who placed you there and proved their right;*
*You have become a traitor to their names,*
*Even though they trusted you with everything they had.*

*You shall stand, continually misrepresented <note>original: "belyed"</note> and slandered,*
*Nothing but a curiosity for the ignorant to stare at;*
*And by your failure <note>original: "guilt"</note>, the wise—now warned—*
*Will never again desire to build such massive piles,*
*Nor trust their living glory to the dead,*
*Who cannot speak, but leave their fame to chance.*

*Though time, with all the power of his years, has launched*
*A long assault <note>original: "battery"</note>, backed by the erosion of age,*
*Yet you resist, relying only on your own strength,*
*And wage war against his all-conquering forces;*
*Claiming a heavenly right <note>original: "prescription"</note> to be free,*
*And a permit to last as long as time itself.*

<summary>In this portion of the poem, Samuel Daniel laments how the lack of historical records has turned Stonehenge into a subject of myths and legends. He concludes that while the stones stubbornly survive the passage of time, they have failed their builders by forgetting their names.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page serves as the title and opening chapter of William Stukeley's study of Avebury (Abury). It follows a poetic preface that lamented the loss of history at Stonehenge and transitions into a formal investigation of Druidic temples, connecting British ancient history with Biblical patriarchal traditions.</meta>

# **ABURY**,
### A Temple of the
## **British DRUIDS**,
### With Some Others Described.

---

## CHAPTER 1.

*On the origin of Druid or patriarchal temples, along with public religion and the celebration of the Sabbath. They were made of unhewn <note>original: "rude"</note> stones set upright in the ground, circular in form, and open to the sky. In hot climates, groves were planted around them. Abraham practiced this, and our Druids followed his example. On the nature of evidence in matters of such great antiquity. The patriarchs had a knowledge of the nature of the Deity to be adored, existing in distinct persons <note>original: "personalities"</note>—a concept that can even be reached through human reason. The Druids possessed this same knowledge, as shown by their works. The first public practice of religion was called calling upon the name of Jehovah, the mediator.*

Writers on the study of the past <note>original: "antiquities"</note> generally find it more difficult to organize their material in a way that is engaging for the reader than writers in most other subjects. Being tedious is always a fault, but it is even more so in this field than in others. It is a mistake if we either spend too much time on an overly detailed description of things, or enter into formal and logical arguments beyond what the nature of such accounts can reasonably support. 

Nevertheless, the value of historical knowledge will always ensure enough interest in this very significant branch of learning, as long as any appreciation for culture or education remains in the world. Indeed, we might simply ask: what is all learning but the knowledge of the past? It is a way of bringing back the wisdom and the deeds of former times. However, the best way to write about these subjects, as I see it, is to organize the facts and present them...

<summary>This page introduces Avebury as a Druidic temple and links its origins to the religious practices of Biblical patriarchs like Abraham. The author, William Stukeley, argues that studying the past is the foundation of all learning and aims to present his findings in an engaging, rather than tedious, manner.</summary>

<keywords>Avebury, Abury, Druids, Patriarchs, Abraham, Stonehenge, archaeology, theology, Jehovah, British history</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Stukeley’s introduction, moving from the difficulty of writing about history to his specific plan for the book. He argues that ancient monuments should be described vividly to bring the past to life and begins to link British Druid temples to the Biblical narrative of the patriarchs, specifically the era of Enos.</meta>

# ABURY

...was to present them in such a way, and in such a light, that it wins over the reader's affection and faith as they proceed. This should be done without pointing out every single detail like a child, or using logical proofs or mathematical demonstrations, which are not appropriate for this subject. The study of antiquities must be drawn with such strong lines of probability <note>original: "verisimilitude"</note> and represented in such lively colors that the reader effectively sees them as they were in their earliest ages. This either brings the past down to modern times or raises the reader up through time, as if they lived when these things were first made. Then we may truly say with the poet:

->*Indeed, a beauty comes to ancient things from this: that the one who reads them reads them as if they were new.* <note>original: "Scilicet antiquis proficiscitur inde venustas, / Quod, tanquam nova sint, qui legit illa, legat."</note><-

In trying to follow this rule, I must inform the reader of the general purpose of this volume. It consists of four parts. Three are descriptions of the three kinds of <term>Druid temples</term> (or as we may call them, <term>patriarchal temples</term>) that I have observed in *Britain*. The fourth part will be reflections on their age and origin—specifically, who built them in the early ages of the world and in Eastern <note>original: "oriental"</note> countries. Although my papers grew to an enormous size while writing the descriptive parts of these chapters (which I did on-site and at great leisure)—and this was necessary for my own understanding—I will shorten them significantly for the public. In doing this, I will be helped by the engraved illustrations, which give the reader a better idea of these things at a glance than the most detailed descriptions. 

Likewise, in the part of the work where I reason about these temples and trace the remains of those recorded by ancient authors, I will simply lay out the facts. I will show the relationship between the monuments we see today and the accounts of similar structures found in ancient texts from the oldest times. I will leave the reader to form their own judgment based on this evidence, without trying to force agreement with imagined proofs that rarely hold up when dealing with such a remote age.

After what I wrote in my previous volume on S T O N E H E N G E—which links these antiquities to the very earliest times of the world—I may venture to speak a little from first principles <note>original: "ex priori"</note> concerning the origin of temples in general. This will introduce my goals for the first three parts of this book: the three different types of <term>Druid</term> or <term>patriarchal temples</term> in the *British* Isles. If we wish to know anything about such a remote time, we must (as with all ancient history) turn to the *Bible*. I believe this is mentioned in *Genesis 4:26*: "And to *Seth*, to him also there was born a son; and he called his name *Enos*: then began men to call upon the <term>N A M E of the L O R D</term>."

Regarding this passage, I note that the explanatory note <note>original: "gloss"</note> in our *English Bibles* says "to call themselves by the name of the LORD," which is very incorrect. The word "themselves" is a complete addition <note>original: "interpolation"</note>. If we translated it correctly, it should be "to call in the name of *Jehovah*," or rather, "to invoke in the name of *Jehovah*." *Vatablus* <note>François Vatable, a 16th-century Hebrew scholar</note> translates it as "then men began to profane the Name by calling themselves by it." Our great scholar *Selden* <note>John Selden, a 17th-century polymath and legal antiquary</note> also fell into that opinion.

<summary>Stukeley outlines his four-part plan for the book and argues that the British Druid temples have their origins in the earliest Biblical era of the patriarchs, specifically starting with Enos.</summary>

# ABURY

Messiah. All this shows that the patriarchs had a knowledge of the true nature of God, and that the Christian or mediatorial religion is both the first and the last. And when men had completely strayed from the first, the Mosaic dispensation <note>original: "Mosaic dispensation"; the religious system and laws given to the Israelites through Moses.</note> was only a temporary veil <note>original: "vail"</note> placed over the brilliance and spirituality of true religion for a time, to lead them back to it upon the actual coming of the Messiah. 

3. These altars, as they are called here, were the patriarchal temples like those of our Druids <note>William Stukeley, the author, believed that the ancient Druids of Britain were descendants of biblical people and practiced a "patriarchal" religion that prefigured Christianity.</note>—the places of public worship. "Invoking in the name of Jehovah" <note>The Hebrew name for God, often translated as "The LORD."</note> is a figure of speech meaning public worship on Sabbath days; it is equivalent to our modern expression "to go to church on Sundays." For this reason, Servius <note>original: "Servius on the Æneid III. v. 85"; Maurus Servius Honoratus was a 4th-century Roman grammarian who wrote a famous commentary on Virgil’s Aeneid.</note> writes that in the most ancient form of worship, people only prayed directly to the deity without offering animal sacrifice. 

Thus, I believe we are to understand Herodotus <note>A 5th-century BC Greek historian.</note> (Book 2), where he says the Athenians learned "invoking" from the Pelasgians, who were Phoenicians; they probably learned it from Abraham, who entered the land of Canaan as a religious reformer. Invoking was the standard method of devotion on Sabbath days, while sacrificing was for extraordinary occasions.

It was Abraham's custom to build one of these temples wherever he lived, as he later did in the plain of Mamre, near Hebron (Genesis 13:18). At Beersheba, we are told he planted a grove and there invoked the name of Jehovah, the everlasting God (Genesis 21:33). It cannot be doubted that there was an altar and a structure of stones <note>original: "work of ſtories"; corrected here based on context.</note> at that same place. This was the practice of all his successors, the patriarchs, ever after, as is obvious in scripture right up to the time of Moses. 

Isaac built an altar in Beersheba and invoked the name of Jehovah, who appeared to him personally (Genesis 26:25). Jacob set up the anointed pillar at Bethel (Genesis 33:18) and the temple there (Genesis 35). At Shechem, he built another (Genesis 33:20). At Bethel, he set up a pillar where Jehovah personally appeared to him and blessed him; he anointed it and poured a drink offering, or libation, upon it (Genesis 35:14). 

In Exodus 24:4, we read that Moses rose early in the morning and built an altar at the foot of the hill along with twelve pillars, which we have no reason to doubt were set in a circle. The same was done after they were settled in the land of Canaan until the Temple of Solomon was built. For example, when Samuel lived at Ramah, he built an altar to Jehovah there to celebrate the public duties of religion (1 Samuel 7:17).

From this, we can further gather these three things:

1. They planted groves in patriarchal times to serve as temples for public worship. It appears this was done in those hot countries for comfort during the summer season, and perhaps for the sake of magnificence. We are told that Abraham lived for a long time at Beersheba, where he planted the grove. These groves were like our cathedrals; they were planted around the circular stone structures <note>original: "circular parts of ſtories"; modernized to "stones" based on the architectural context.</note> to serve as porticos <note>A porch or walkway with a roof supported by columns, used here to describe the space for a congregation.</note> for receiving the congregation. Consequently, "groves" and "temples" became synonymous terms in both sacred and pagan writings.

2. These temples, which they called altars, were circles of stones surrounding a central stone that was more specifically named the altar. These circles were larger or smaller, made of more or fewer stones, as the will or convenience of the founder prompted. Moses's temple was a circle of twelve stones, and we have similar circles in England.

3. They were commonly built on open plains and rising ground, making them visible and convenient for large crowds from the entire neighborhood to assemble. This is a logical result of their purpose, as a place of public use must be in the most public and visible location.

4. The patriarchal religion and the Christian religion are one and the same. Hence, in Isaiah 19:19, the prophet—speaking of the restoration of the...

# DESCRIBED.

The patriarchal religion in Egypt, under the gospel dispensation, says, "In that day shall there be an altar to *Jehovah* in the midst of the land of Egypt; and a pillar, at the border thereof, to *Jehovah*." <note>This is a reference to the biblical prophecy in Isaiah 19:19.</note> This is expressly making use of the terms of a patriarchal temple, with a view to that religion being restored, meaning the Christian faith.

These monuments of the piety of the patriarchs in the eastern parts of the world were, in time, desecrated for idolatrous purposes and at length destroyed, even by the people of Israel, for that reason. Temples square in form and covered at the top were introduced during the Mosaic dispensation <note>The "Mosaic dispensation" refers to the period when the Laws of Moses governed religious life, specifically the era of the Tabernacle and the Temple of Solomon.</note> in direct opposition to that idolatry. But before then, that first method passed all over the western world and to Britain, where we see them to this day. By the way, we trace some footsteps of them, but there is always a fable annexed; as is generally the case today in our Druid temples at home. 

Thus, Pausanias in his *Description of Greece: Corinth* informs us that near the river Chemarus is a *septum* or circle of stones. He says they have a report there that this is the place from which Pluto carried away Proserpine. By such a story, we must understand that the mysteries <note>In antiquity, "mysteries" referred to secret religious rituals or initiations.</note> were celebrated there. Pausanias writes that the Thracians used to build their temples round and open at the top, in Boeotia. He speaks of such at Haliartus by the name of a Temple <note>original: *Ναος* (Naos)</note>, equivalent to the Hebrew *Beth* (house), which name Jacob gave to his temple. He speaks of several altars dedicated to Pluto, set in the middle of areas fenced in with stones, and they are called Hermionian. He tells us too that among the Orchomenians is a most ancient temple of the Graces, but they worship them in the form of stones. From the number three, we may easily guess this was a <term>kistvaen</term> <note>original: "Kist vaen"; a Welsh term for a stone chest or dolmen, often used as a tomb or sanctuary.</note>, as our old Britons call it, or <term>Kebla</term> <note>A term used by the author to denote a focal point or sanctuary within a temple, similar to the Qibla in Islamic prayer direction.</note>, like that in our great temple of Abury and elsewhere. Indeed, the stones of these sanctuaries, in time, instead of being a direction in worship, became the object of worship, as Clement of Alexandria affirms.

That our Druids were so eminently celebrated for their use of groves shows them to have a more particular relation to Abraham, and to have derived the usage more immediately from him; a point I indicated to a good extent in the account of STONEHENGE. Hence, the name of Druid imports "priest of the groves"; and their green cathedrals, as we may call them, are celebrated by all ancient writers who speak of this people. We all know the awful and solemn pleasure that strikes one upon entering a grove; a kind of religious dread arises from the gloomy majesty of the place, which was very favorable to the purpose they intended. Servius, commenting upon Virgil's *Aeneid*, Book 3:

->*Before the city in a grove by the water of a false Simois,*<-
<note>original: "Ante urbem in luco falsi Simoëntis ad undam"</note>

observes that Virgil never mentions a grove without a note of religion. Again, in *Aeneid*, Book 9, verse 4, Strabo says the poets call temples by the name of groves. And this is frequently done in the scriptures. But it is natural for our classic writers, when speaking of the Druids and their great attachment to religious rites (so different from what they were acquainted with), to insist much upon their groves. They overlooked our stone monuments, which they would scarcely dignify with the name of temples because they were not covered like their own. Yet if we were to conclude from this, as some do, that groves were the only temples of the Druids—and therefore that Stonehenge and the works we are now discussing were not theirs—we should err as much as if we asserted that Abraham only made use of groves, and not of the other temples erected on plains and open places.

Thus far I have provided a brief introduction to our discourse, showing the origin of temples among mankind; a necessary provision for...

# ABURY

...that duty we owe to our sovereign Author and Benefactor. For unless we can prove ourselves self-sufficient and independent, all nature cries aloud for our acknowledgment of this duty. Private and domestic prayer is our duty as private persons and families—since we have life, subsistence, and the common protection of Providence—but the profession and exercise of public religion is equally necessary because we are a community. We are a part of the public, a parish, a city, a nation, linked together by government for our common safety and protection. We must join together to implore from the hands of God Almighty those general blessings of life that we require in that collective role. Any person who excludes themselves from their share in this duty is a rebel and traitor to the public and is, in effect, separated from the common blessings of heaven. 

But *time* is just as necessary to this public duty as *place*, as everyone's reason must dictate. Therefore, the Sabbath was instituted; it was the very first command of our Maker, given even in the happy seat of *Paradise* and before our fatal transgression <note>original: "fatal transgression"; a reference to the Fall of Man in the Garden of Eden</note>. It is the established ordinance of God and is founded upon the strictest reason. So, if we accept that the patriarchs built these temples in which to assemble for public devotion but reject the Sabbath because the scriptures do not specifically mention that they celebrated it, we think absurdly and err against common sense and reason. The scriptures were given to teach us religion, not to inform us of things already obvious to common sense and reason.

The duty of the Sabbath begins as early as our existence and is included with great propriety in the observation of the divine historian <note>original: "divine historian"; referring to Moses, the traditional author of the Book of Genesis</note> concerning *Adam's* grandson, *Enos*. At that time, it passed from a family ordinance to one where several families united, as was then the case. The specific nature of the expression, *invoking* in the name of *Jehovah* <note>Genesis 4:26: "Then began men to call upon the name of the LORD."</note>, reveals to us the form of their religion. It was founded on the "mediatorial scheme," which taught that the Mediator <note>A "Mediator" in this context refers to a divine figure (the pre-incarnate Christ) who acts as a bridge between God and humanity.</note> was a divine person to be worshiped; through our faith and hope in him, or in his Name, we were to invoke God Almighty for our pardon and protection. Therefore, the same scheme of religion has existed from the beginning to this day. The *Mosaic* system <note>The Law of Moses given to the Israelites.</note> intervened chiefly as a remedy against idolatry until the world was prepared for the great Advent <note>The birth of Jesus Christ.</note>, when the patriarchal religion would be published again under the name of "Christian."

From all this, we must conclude that the ancients knew something of the mysterious nature of the Deity existing in distinct persons, which is more fully revealed to us in the Christian era. All nature, our senses, and common reason assure us of the existence of one supreme and self-originated Being. The second person in the Deity <note>The Son, or Jesus Christ.</note> is discoverable in almost every page of the Old Testament. After his coming, he informs us more fully of the nature of the third person <note>The Holy Spirit.</note>; and that third person is discoverable in almost every page of the New Testament. 

That the ancients had some knowledge of this great truth is demonstrated by the learned *Steuchus Eugubinus* in his work *On Eternal Philosophy* <note>original: "perenni philosoph." refers to "De perenni philosophia" (1540) by Agostino Steuco</note>, using the writings that remain from figures such as *Hermes, Orpheus, Hydaspes, Pythagoras, Plato, the Platonists*, the Sibylline verses, the oracles, and the like. Our own *Cudworth* <note>Ralph Cudworth (1617–1688), a philosopher who argued that ancient pagans were actually monotheists.</note> has very laudably pursued the same track, as have *Kircher* and our *Ramsay* in his history of *Cyrus* <note>original: "history of Cyprus"; likely a printer's error for "The Travels of Cyrus" (1727) by Andrew Michael Ramsay, which explores ancient theology.</note>, and many more, to whom I refer the curious reader who wishes to be convinced of it. I shall only add this: if we assume that an ancient tradition of this knowledge has been handed down from one generation to another to spark our reason, then this doctrine is far from being contrary to reason or above human reason. It can be deduced from reason and is perfectly agreeable to it, as I shall show in Chapter XV. Nor is this a slight matter; for if knowledge is a valuable thing, if it be the...

...the greatest honor and happiness for the human mind; the most divine branch of all knowledge is to understand something of the nature of the Deity. The Druids certainly attempted to achieve this knowledge, and they succeeded, as we conclude from the various types of their temples. Once we have described these structures, we shall take the liberty of returning to this argument and briefly discussing it again, as it is the primary and ultimate purpose of all inquiries into antiquity.

## Chapter II.

*On the specific origin of temples and the meaning of the name. Their design, being round and open. The Tabernacle of Moses as a square, covered temple, designed in opposition to earlier forms that had been corrupted by idolatry. Another reason it was covered with skins was because it served as a symbol <note>original: typical</note> of the Messiah. Similarly, patriarchal or Druid temples were built in shapes that symbolized the Deity and His divine personalities. When these became idolatrous, they were generally dedicated to the sun because of their circular shape. The most ancient symbolic figure of the Deity was the circle, snake, and wings, which we frequently see on Egyptian and other monuments. Patriarchal temples were built to represent these shapes; therefore, they are of three kinds: 1. A circle only. 2. A circle and a snake. 3. A circle and wings. This volume discusses one temple of each of these types in Britain. The temple of Rollright <note>original: ROWLDRICH</note> in Oxfordshire, representing the first type, is described here. The evidence for it being a work of the Druids is organized as a formal example: 1. Its elevated location on an open heath near the headwaters of rivers. 2. An open circle of upright stones, sourced from the local ground surface. 3. The weathered appearance of the stones. 4. Evidence from the name, explaining it through the biblical "Gilgal" <note>a circle of stones mentioned in the Book of Joshua</note>. 5. From the measurements used, specifically the Druid cubit. 6. From the burial mounds <term>barrows</term> surrounding it: a Druid’s court, a king’s burial mound <term>tumulus</term>, and the burial mound of the Archdruid who founded it. 7. From historical accounts regarding these works. 8. Tombs have often served as the foundation for temples throughout history, stemming from a hope for the body's resurrection; this practice also led to the deification of heroes and the introduction of the first forms of idolatry.*

**T**EMPLE is a word derived from the Greek <term>Temenos</term> <note>original: Τεμενος</note>, meaning a place set apart, enclosed, and dedicated to sacred use—whether it be an open area, a circle of stones, a field, or a grove. This concept, like all others, evolved from simple beginnings until it became what we now recognize as a temple. For instance, we read in Book 2 of the *Iliad* about the field of Ceres <note>the Roman goddess of agriculture; original: Iliad II</note>, and in Book 8 of the *Iliad* about the field and altar of Jupiter <note>the king of the gods; original: Iliad VIII</note>.

<summary>This page introduces the symbolic origins of ancient temples, arguing that Druid stone circles were designed based on divine geometric symbols (circles, snakes, and wings) before such forms were corrupted by sun-worship and idolatry.</summary>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's description of ancient circular temples, transitioning from historical precedents in the Middle East (like those of Abraham and Jeroboam) to the specific structures found in the British Isles, which the author attributes to the Druids and a "patriarchal religion."</meta>

## DESCRIBED.

Father Abraham had built a temple before; Jeroboam chose it for his idolatrous temple, called by the *Alexandrian Greeks* in later times "the house of On" <note>original: "οιζος ΩV" (oikos On)</note>, or the temple of *On*. Saint Cyril, in his comments on *Hosea*, writes that *On* is the sun, named for its round form. The pagans had done all they could to corrupt the memory of the true God and turned *Beth-el*—which signifies the "house of EL" or God—into the house of *On*, or the sun. Just as *On* is a word undoubtedly derived from *EL* in *Hebrew*, expressing God's power and sovereignty, it is much like *Elion*, a name for God in Scripture signifying "The Most High" <note>original: "Hypsistus"</note> God (Genesis 14:18; Luke 1:37). In *Arabic*, this is *Allah Ta’ala*, "the Most High God." From this comes *Atlas*, the consecrated name of the *African* hero: *Allah Ta’al*.

When these ancient patriarchal temples in other countries were perverted toward idolatry, they consecrated many of them to the sun, thinking their round form should be referred to his disc, and that these pyramidal stones, set in a circle, imitated his rays. Hence they were called *Aglibelus*, the "Round God" <note>original: "rotundus Deus"</note>, as interpreted by *Bochart*. Among the Eastern peoples, they were known as the "Circular Zeus" <note>original: "עז בעל, ζευς επικυκλιος"</note>, as *Schedius* observes. Had the ancient *Greek* writers seen our temples at *Stonehenge* and the rest, they would have concluded they were dedicated to the sun.

These temples of ours are always of a round form, and there are innumerable examples of them all over the *British* Isles. Nevertheless, they are to be ranked into three kinds; for though they are all circular, there are three manifest variations I have observed regarding that threefold figure by which the ancients (probably even from *Adam*'s time) expressed the great idea of the Deity in writing. This figure is called by *Kircher* "the snake-circle-wing-shape" <note>original: "ophio-cyclo-pteryo-morphus"</note>. It is a circle with wings and a snake proceeding from it. This figure was excellently designed to illustrate the understanding they had—no doubt by divine communication—of the mysterious nature of the Deity. It was the way of the ancients in their religious buildings to copy or represent the form of the Divine Being, as they conceived it, in a symbolic manner. By this means they produced a most effective <term>prophylact</term> <note>a protective charm or structure intended to ward off evil or command divine favor</note>, as they thought, which could not fail to draw down the blessings of divine providence upon that place and country, as if by sympathy and similarity.

I shall therefore make it the subject of the present volume to describe one or two of each sort of temple built upon the plan of these figures, wherein the founders have left an incontestable proof of the knowledge the ancient world had of the divine nature through these durable and magnificent monuments. The remainder of these temples (as many as have come to my knowledge), together with the locations of the sports and games of the ancient *Britons* and the religion of the *Druids*, I shall publish in the succeeding volume.

Names or words are necessary for the understanding of things; therefore:
1. The simple round temples, I call "temples."
2. Those with the form of a snake attached, such as that of Avebury <note>original: "Abury"</note>, I call "serpentine temples," or <term>Dracontia</term> <note>from the Greek 'drakon' (serpent); temples laid out in the shape of a snake</note>, by which name they were known of old.
3. Those with the form of wings attached, I call "alate" or "winged temples."

And these are all the kinds of Druid temples that I know of. We may call these figures the symbols of the patriarchal religion, just as the cross is the symbol of Christianity. Therefore, they built their temples according to those figures.

->D<- ->ROWL<-

<meta>This page transitions from the general history of circular temples to a specific case study of the Rollright Stones in Oxfordshire, which the author uses as a model for Druidic sites.</meta>

# AVEBURY / ROLLRIGHT <note>original: "ABURY / ROWLDRICH"</note>

I will begin with Rollright—or rather Rollrich—and as an example of the requirements sought in these inquiries, I will organize my observations into a specific order. This method may be useful in all research of this kind:

1. **A situation on high ground, open heaths, and near the headwaters of rivers.**
ROLLRIGHT is a Druid temple of the first kind: a circular work that has often been mentioned in print. It lies in the northwest part of Oxfordshire, on high ground where the counties of Oxford, Warwick, and Gloucester meet. It is near the town of Chipping Norton. Two rivers rise here that flow in completely opposite directions. The Evenlode flows toward the southern part of the kingdom; it joins the Isis <note>A traditional name for the Thames near Oxford</note> below Woodstock, visits the great light of Britain—Oxford—and then meets the Thames at Dorchester, the ancient episcopal see <note>The seat of a bishop</note> of the Mercian kingdom. At Dorchester, there are fine remains of Saxon church antiquity, as well as Roman and British ruins. The inquisitive traveler who prefers our own country's antiquities to the vain tour of foreign lands will find much curious amusement there. The other river, the Stour, flows from Rollright directly north to meet the Avon at Stratford, and from there to the Severn Sea. 

Consequently, Rollright must stand on very high ground. To those who attentively consider the place itself, it appears to be a large, sloped <note>original: "cop'd"</note> hill on the summit of an open down. The temple, together with the Archdruid's barrow <note>term: barrow (an ancient burial mound)</note> nearby, stands on the very tip of it, with the land descending in every direction. It offers an extensive prospect, especially into Gloucestershire and Warwickshire. The country hereabouts was originally an open, barren heath with a quarry of a kind of ragstone <note>term: ragstone (a hard, coarse, sedimentary rock)</note> underneath. At present, there are some nearby enclosures that have been plowed up. The majority of our ancient site remains, though many of the stones have been carried away within living memory to build bridges, houses, and other structures.

2. **It is an open temple of a circular form, made of stones set upright in the ground.** 
The stones are rough and unhewn; I believe they were taken from the surface of the ground. I saw stones of significant size and of the same kind as those of our antiquity lying in the fields north of Norton, not far away. There are such stones in other places hereabouts, from which the Druids took them—though for the most part, they have been carried off since then for building and other uses.

3. **We observe the effect of the weather upon these works.** 
The site we are treating stands in a corner of a hedge in an enclosure near the northern summit of the hill. "It is a great monument of antiquity," says the excellent Mr. Camden <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a famous English antiquary</note>, "a number of vastly great stones placed in a circular figure. They are of unequal height and shape, very much ragged, impaired, and decayed by time." Indeed, as we must judge their age from their appearance, we may pronounce them second to none in that respect. They are corroded like worm-eaten wood by the harsh jaws of time—much more so than Stonehenge. This is a significant argument that it is the work of the Druids.

4. **We are led to this conclusion from the name.** 
Mr. Camden calls them the *Rollright stones*. Dr. Holland <note>Philemon Holland (1552–1637), who translated Camden's "Britannia"</note> says in his notes that in a book in the Exchequer (perhaps he means the Domesday Book), the adjacent town—from which the site takes its name—is called *Rollendrich*. If it were written exactly, I suppose it would be *Rholdrwyg*, which means the "Druids' wheel or circle" <note>original: "Rholdrwyg"</note>. Likewise, the word *Rhwyll* in the Bri- <note>The text continues on the next page with "British" or "Britain"</note>

<summary>The author identifies the Rollright Stones as a primary example of a Druidic circular temple, noting its high-altitude location, its construction from local unhewn stone, and its extreme weathering as evidence of its great antiquity.</summary>
<keywords>Rollright Stones, Druids, Oxfordshire, Chipping Norton, William Camden, antiquarianism, barrows, river headwaters, Rholdrwyg, megalithic circles</keywords>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>DESCRIBED.</header>

In British, the word is lattices <note>original: *cancelli*</note>, for these stones are set quite near together, so as almost to become a continuous wall or railing <note>original: *cancellus*</note>. Furthermore, the word *Roilig* in the old Irish language signifies a church; thus, it implies the Druids’ church, chancel, or temple, in the original sense of the word. We may call this place the **Gilgal of Britain**, to speak in the Eastern manner—a word equivalent to the Celtic *Rhol*, a wheel or circle. This gave the name to that famous camp or fortress where the army of Israel first pitched their tents in the land of Canaan after they passed the river Jordan in a miraculous manner, walking across on dry ground <note>original: "dry-shod"</note>, as it is described in the most sublime manner, equal to the dignity of the subject, in Joshua 4. There we also read that Joshua commanded twelve men, one from each tribe, to place twelve stones in the channel of the river Jordan where the Ark stood while the people passed over and the stream was cut off; these were set there as a memorial. They likewise took twelve stones out of the bed of the river, and Joshua set them up in **Gilgal** in a circular form, which gave the place its name, meaning a "roll" or "wheel." And he alludes to this in the next chapter, in a passage that is otherwise difficult to understand; for here Joshua circumcised the people, that rite having been omitted among the younger generation during their travels in the wilderness:

> “And the LORD said unto Joshua, ‘This day have I rolled away the reproach of Egypt from off you’; therefore the name of the place is called **Gilgal** unto this day.”

Commentators who do not understand this often offer many strange explanations because they do not see the connection between the name and the object itself. Some, therefore, suppose it was so called because from there Joshua conquered all his enemies in the surrounding area, and so on. But the truth is, Joshua set the stones in a circular form, like the ancient temples, but placed no altar there because they had no need to use it as a temple since the Tabernacle was present; therefore, he called it simply "the wheel." Similarly, I do not doubt that the altar which Moses built under Mount Sinai, with twelve pillars, was a circular work like our Druid temples (Exodus 24:4). We should think the same of the altar which Moses built and called *Jehovah Nissi* <note>The Lord is my Banner</note>, which the pagans corrupted into *Jupiter Nyseus* or *Dionysus* (Exodus 17:15). The same must be said of all the patriarchal altars of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. These works of ours prove it; they are only slightly later in time and were made in imitation of theirs. Without using a pun or false logic, these matters may be said to prove each other in a circle, where it is absurd to demand any positive proof due to the extreme distance of time and place. I believe nothing more should be expected of us than to gather circumstantial evidence—a gathering of numerous and strong similarities—as is now the case with our study of **Rollright** <note>original: "Rowldrich"</note>.

5. We very justly infer this is a temple of the Druids from the measurements upon which it is built. In a letter from Mr. Roger Gale to me, dated from Worcester, August 19, 1719—he having visited this ancient site at my request—he tells me the diameter of the circle is 35 yards. Likewise, the Bishop of London writes that the distance at Stonehenge from the entrance of the area to the temple itself is 35 yards; so the diameter of Stonehenge is also 35 yards. We suppose this was not measured with mathematical exactness, but when we look into the comparative scale of English feet and cubits, we discern that 60 "Druid cubits" is the measurement being sought. The diameter of the outer circle of Stonehenge and this circle at Rollright are exactly equal.

<margin>TABLE II.</margin>
I have repeated the table of Druid cubits compared with our English feet, which will be of service to us throughout this work (Plate II).

<header>A B U R Y</header>

<margin>Tables III, IV.</margin>
The circle itself is composed of stones of various shapes and dimensions, set quite close together, as may best be seen by the drawings in Tables III and IV. They are somewhat flat, about 16 inches thick. Originally, there seem to have been 60 stones in total; at present, 22 are standing, few exceeding 4 feet in height. However, one stone at the very northern point is much higher than the rest, standing 7 feet high and 5½ feet broad. There was an entrance to the circle from the northeast, as is also the case at *Stonehenge*. Ralph Sheldon, Esquire, excavated in the middle of the circle at *Rowldrich*, but found nothing.

6. Another argument for it being a Druid temple is based on the barrows <note>burial mounds</note> found all around it, following the constant practice seen in these locations. To the northeast is a great <term>tumulus</term> <note>original Latin: *tumulus*, a burial mound</note> or barrow of an elongated shape, which I suppose belonged to an Arch-Druid. Between this barrow and our temple stands a huge upright stone called the <term>kingstone</term>; the stone is 8 feet high and 7 feet broad. It, along with the barrow, can be seen in Tables III and V, though much of the barrow has been dug away. It is now over 60 feet in length, 20 feet in breadth, and flat at the top. I do not know whether more stones originally stood around this barrow, or if this stone served a specific purpose on its own in the administration of religious offices in the temple.

<margin>Table V.</margin>
In the same plate, another barrow may be seen, though this one is circular, located below the road to the left on the side of the hill. Beneath it is a spring-head running eastward toward *Long Compton*. This barrow formerly had stone-work at its eastern end. Upon this same heath toward the east, on the way to *Banbury*, there are many barrows of different shapes within sight of *Rowldrich*. Specifically, near a place called <term>Chapel</term> on the heath, there is a large, flat, and circular <term>tumulus</term>, surrounded by a ditch with a small mound in the center. This is what I call a "Druid’s barrow"; there are many such examples near *Stonehenge*, some of which I have opened. A small circular barrow sits a short distance from it. There are also many circular, dish-like cavities on this heath, similar to those near *Stonehenge*; we may call them "inverted barrows."

Not far from the Druid's barrow, I saw a square earthwork, of the type I call "Druids' courts" or houses. Similar structures exist near *Stonehenge* and *Abury* (Avebury). It is an area 100 cubits square, surrounded by a double ditch. The earth from the ditches was thrown inward between them to raise a terrace that goes entirely around the square. The ditches are too small to have been made for defense. Within the square, there are what seem to be the remains of stone walls. It is within sight of the temple and offers a fine view of the surrounding area, being situated on the highest part of the ridge. A little further on is a small round barrow with stone-work at the eastern end, like the one mentioned earlier near *Rowldrich*; a dry stone wall or fence now runs directly over it across the heath.

<margin>Tables VI, VII.</margin>
Returning closer to the temple, we see a remarkable monument 300 paces directly east of it in the same field, which is often noted. It is what the ancient <term>Britons</term> call a <term>Kist vaen</term> <note>Welsh: *Cistvaen*</note> or <term>stone chest</term>. By Britons, I mean the <term>Welsh</term>, who are the descendants of those invaders from the continent—the <term>Belgae</term>, <term>Gauls</term>, and <term>Cimbrians</term>—who drove the original inhabitants (those who built the works we are discussing) further northward. Consequently, these later people gave the sites names based on their appearance: *Rowldrich*, meaning the <term>wheel or circle of the Druids</term>; *Stonehenge*, which they called <term>choir gaur</term> <note>Middle Welsh: *Côr y Cawr*</note>, the <term>giants' dance</term>; or as our Saxon ancestors called it, *Stonehenge*, the <term>hanging-stones</term> or <term>stone-gallows</term>. Each successive wave of inhabitants was removed further from the true understanding and knowledge of these things.

<margin>Tables VI, VII.</margin>
Our <term>Kist vaen</term> is represented in plates VI and VII. One shows the front side,

side, the other the back; so that there needs but little description of it. It is composed of six stones: one broader for the back part, two narrower ones on each side set square to the first, and above all, as a cover, a still larger stone. The opening faces full west toward the temple, or **Rollright** <note>original: "Rowldrich"</note>. It stands on a round **burial mound** <note>original: "tumulus"</note> and has a fine prospect southwestward down the valley where the head of the river **Evenlode** runs. I am convinced this was merely monumental, erected over the grave of some great person buried there—most probably the king of the country when this temple was built. And if there was any use for the building, it might possibly have been used for some anniversary commemoration of the deceased, involving feasts, games, exercises, or the like, as we read in the classic poets who describe customs older than their own times. It is similar to that **stone chest** <note>original: "Kist vaen"</note> in Cornwall, which I have drawn in plate 37.

Near the arch-druid's barrow, by the one called the **King's Stone** <note>original: "Kingstone"</note>, is a square, oblong plot of land formed on the turf. Here, on a certain day of the year, the young men and maidens customarily meet and make merry with cakes and ale. This seems to be the remnant of the very ancient festival once celebrated here in memory of the person interred, for whom the long barrow and temple were made. This was the burial place of the arch-druid who founded it. At **Enstone**, a little way off between **Neat Enstone** and **Fulwell**, by the side of a bank or burial mound, stands a great stone with other smaller ones. It is half a mile southwest of Enstone church. There is also a famous barrow at **Lineham**, by the banks of the Evenlode.

7. Mr. **Camden** writes further concerning our antiquity that "the country people have a fanciful tradition <note>original: "fond tradition"</note> that they were once men, turned into stones. The highest of all, which lies out of the ring, they call the King. Five larger stones, which are at some distance from the circle and set close together, they pretend were Knights; those in the ring were common soldiers." The country people for some miles around are very fond of this story and take it very poorly if anyone doubts it; indeed, one is in danger of being stoned for their unbelief. They likewise have rhymes and sayings relating to it. Similar reports are to be found at other such works, our Druid temples. They remind one <note>original: "savour"</note> of the most ancient and heroic times—like **Perseus** turning men into stones, or **Cadmus** producing men from serpents' teeth, or **Deucalion** creating people by throwing stones over his head, and other such tales, which we shall have occasion to mention again in chapter 14.

8. We may very reasonably affirm that this temple was built here because of this long barrow. Very often in ancient times, temples owe their foundation to sepulchers, just as they do now. **Clement of Alexandria** in his *Exhortation* <note>original: "Protrepticus"</note> and **Eusebius** both allow this; it is also treated at length by **Schedius** and other authors. It is a common feature among these works of our Druids and an argument that this is a work of theirs. I shall make only two observations from this:
1. That it proceeded from a strong ancient belief in a future state, regarding both bodies and souls; for the temples are thought to be **protective** <note>original: "prophylactic"</note> and to have a power of guarding and preserving the remains of the dead.
2. That it was the occasion for consecrating and idolizing dead heroes—the first type of idolatry. For by degrees, they advanced these heroes into those deities of which these stone figures were symbols, instances of which we shall encounter as this work progresses.

Thus we declare **Rollright** to be a Druid temple, based on a combination of all the evidence to be expected in such a case: from its round form, its situ—

# ABURY

...situated on high ground, near springs, on an extended heath, from the stones taken from the surface of the ground, from the name, from the measurement it is built on, from the wear of the weather, from the barrows <note>ancient burial mounds</note> of various kinds about it, from ancient reports, and from its apparent conformity to those patriarchal temples mentioned in scripture. This is the demonstration to be expected in such antiquities. Nor shall I spend time in examining the notion of its belonging to *Rollo the Dane* <note>original: "Rollo the Dane" – A Viking leader; early antiquarians often incorrectly attributed megalithic sites to later Danish or Saxon invaders</note>, and the like. *Mr. Camden* <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a famous English antiquary</note> had too much judgment to mention it. It is confuted in the annotations to *Britannia*, and in *Selden’s* notes on *Drayton’s Polyolbion*, page 224. And let this suffice for what I can say upon this curious and ancient monument: the first kind, and most common of the Druid temples, a plain circle—of which there are innumerable examples all over the *British* isles—being the original form of all temples until the Mosaic tabernacle.

---

### C H A P. III.

Abury, the most extraordinary work in the world, being a *serpentine temple* <term>serpentine temple: Stukeley's theory that the site was designed in the shape of a snake (dracontium) passing through a circle</term>, or of the second kind, described. Now was the critical time of saving the memory of it. Account of the place. *Natural history*. The gray weathers <note>original: "gray weathers" – sarsen stones, which from a distance look like a flock of sheep</note>, called *Sarsens*, a *Phoenician word* meaning a *rock* <note>original: "Sarsens, a phœnician word" – Stukeley believed the stones' name derived from a Phoenician word for rock, like the city of Tyre (Tsur)</note>. Whence the name of the *city of Tyre*. Their weight and texture. The wear of the weather, more apparent here than at *Stonehenge*, an argument of its being a much older work.

WHEN we contemplate the beauty of this region of *Wiltshire* and the great works of antiquity within it, we may be persuaded that the two Atlantic islands and the "Islands of the Blessed" mentioned by *Plato* and other ancient writers were, in reality, *Britain* and *Ireland*. They who first took possession of this country thought it worthy of their care and built those noble works there which have been the admiration of all ages. *Stonehenge* we have endeavored to describe; and we are no more surprised at the extraordinary magnitude of this work of *Abury* <note>modern: Avebury</note> than that it should have escaped the observation of the curious, being a place on the direct *Bath road* from *London*. 

Passing from *Marlborough* to here, it is the common topic of amusement for travelers to observe the "gray weathers" on the *Marlborough* downs. These are the same kind of stones as those of our antiquity, lying dispersed on the surface of the ground as nature originally laid them. When we come to this village, we see the largest of those stones in great numbers, set upright in the earth in circles, in parallel lines, and other regular figures; a great part of it is enclosed in a vast circular ditch over 1,000 feet in diameter. And what will further excite one's curiosity is that the *vallum* <term>vallum: an earthwork rampart or wall</term> or earth—which is of solid chalk dug out of that ditch—is thrown on the outside. This is quite contrary to the nature of castles and fortifications. The ditch alone, which is wide and deep, was a very great labor, and the rampart is very high, creating the appearance of a huge amphitheatre for an innumerable company of spectators; but it cannot possibly be designed for offense or defense.
