<meta>This page continues the description of the ancient megalithic sites in Wiltshire, specifically comparing the fame of Stonehenge with the relative obscurity and ongoing destruction of the much larger site at Avebury.</meta>

# DESCRIBED.

defense. This is passed twice by all travelers, and its oddness would arrest one's attention even if the stones themselves were missed.

The mighty carcass of *Stonehenge* draws great numbers of people out of their way every day, as if to see a spectacle, and it has occupied the pens of the learned to account for it. But *Abury* <note>original: "Abury"; now known as Avebury</note>, a much greater work and more extensive design, has—by some unkind fate—been altogether overlooked. It was in the utmost danger of perishing because of the habit of the country people, developed lately, of demolishing the stones. Mr. *Camden* <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a famous antiquarian</note>, the great light of *British* antiquities, took the *Kennet* avenue to be mere natural rocks, and believed that the village of *Rockley* took its name from them. It is strange that two parallel lines of great stones, set at equal distances and intervals for a mile together, should be mistaken for rocks in their natural site. As for the town of *Rockley*, it is four miles away and has nothing to do with this antiquity, though it probably took its name from the adjacent "gray weathers" <note>local name for sarsen stones, which from a distance look like a flock of gray sheep</note> from which our stones were drawn.

Dr. *Holland*, his annotator, writes this about it:
> "Within one mile of *Selbury* (by which he means *Silbury Hill*) is *Abury*, a rural <note>original: "uplandish"</note> village built in an old camp, as it seems, but of no large size. It is surrounded with a fair trench and has four gates, in two of which stand huge stones as doorposts <note>original: "jambs"</note>; but they are so rude that they seem natural rather than man-made. There are some others of this sort in the said village."

At the time when this was written, all the circles of these great stones within the village of *Abury* were nearly perfect: two of about 150 feet in diameter, two of 300 feet in diameter, and the great one of over 1000 feet—all of which deserved higher notice. The sheer size of the circles prevented an unobservant spectator from discerning their purpose.

<margin>*TABLE I.*</margin>
I am confident that the intelligent reader, by looking over the plate in the frontispiece showing the village of *Abury*, will see enough to form a grand idea of the place. This is even more true if they realize that the two avenues of *Kennet* and *Bekhampton*, branching off at the bottom to the right and the left, each extend over a mile from the town.

Dr. *Childrey* likewise, in his *Britannia Baconica*, takes these stones around *Kennet* to be mere rocks. Thus, if our minds are not properly inclined toward these inquiries, or if we believe nothing great in art preceded the times of the *Romans*, we may fall into the error of *Sebastian Münster* <note>original: "Munster's error, in cosmograph. iii. 49"</note>, who believed that plain Celtic urns dug up in *Poland* were the work of nature. *Harrington*, in his notes on *Orlando Furioso*, also speaks of *Abury*.

Just before I visited this place to attempt to preserve the memory of it, the inhabitants had fallen into the custom of demolishing the stones, chiefly out of greed for the small area of ground each stood on. First, they dug great pits in the earth and buried them. The expense of digging the "grave" was more than thirty years' purchase <note>a financial term meaning the cost was more than 30 years' worth of the rent or profit the land could produce</note> of the tiny spot they occupied when standing. After this, they discovered the trick of burning them, which has caused miserable havoc to this famous temple. One *Tom Robinson*, the *Herostratus* of *Abury* <note>Herostratus was an arsonist who destroyed the Temple of Artemis to seek fame; the author is calling Robinson a notorious destroyer</note>, is particularly famous for this kind of execution, and he glories in it very much. The method is to dig a pit by the side of the stone until it falls down, then to burn many loads of straw under it. They draw lines of water along the stone when it is heated, and then, with sharp strokes of a great sledgehammer, its prodigious bulk is divided into many smaller parts. But this act of burning <note>original: "Atto de fe," a misspelling of the Portuguese *Auto-da-fé*, literally "act of faith," referring to the public burning of heretics</note> commonly costs thirty shillings in fire and labor, and sometimes twice as much. They admit, too, that it is excessively hard work, for these stones are often 18 feet long, 13 feet broad, and

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The author compares the fame of Stonehenge to the neglected grandeur of Avebury, criticizing earlier historians for misidentifying the stones as natural and detailing the destructive methods used by locals to clear the megaliths from their land.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's account of the systematic destruction of the Avebury stone circles by local villagers and his theories regarding the geological origin and ancient naming of the sarsen stones.</meta>

# ABURY

...and 6 feet thick; their weight crushes the stones placed under them to create a hollow space for burning. To lift them, they use timbers twenty feet long or more, aided by twenty men, though the timbers are often snapped in pieces.

They have sometimes used these stones for building houses, but they say they can get stones more cheaply in more manageable pieces from the **gray weathers** <note>local name for sarsen stones found on the downs, which look like a flock of gray sheep from a distance</note>. One of these large stones will build an ordinary house; however, because the stone is a kind of marble, or rather granite, it is always moist and dewy in winter. This makes the houses damp and unwholesome and rots the furniture. The custom of destroying the monument in this way is so recent that I could easily trace the demise <note>original: "obit"</note> of every stone: who did it, for what purpose, when, by what method, and what house or wall was built from it. Every year that I visited this area, I found several stones missing, though the places where they had been taken from were very apparent. Because of this, I was able twenty years ago to make a perfect ground plan of the whole site and all its parts. It is to be feared that if this work had been deferred until now, it would have been impossible. This stupendous structure <note>original: "fabric"</note>, which for some thousands of years had braved the continual assaults of the weather—and by its nature, if left alone, would have lasted as long as the pyramids of **Egypt**—has instead fallen victim to the wretched ignorance and greed of a little village unluckily placed within it. The scientific interest <note>original: "curiosity"</note> of the site would have been irretrievable.

Such is the modern history of **Abury** <note>modern Avebury</note>, which I thought proper to introduce first to prepare the reader's mind. All of this was recorded in my original memoirs, which I wrote extensively on the spot. Though it was necessary for me to do so then to gain a thorough understanding of the work, I shall publish nothing more here than what I judge absolutely necessary to illustrate it.

Regarding the natural history of the stones, it is the same as that of **Stonehenge**, which is composed of the very same stones fetched from the same **Marlborough Downs**. There, they lie on the surface of the ground in great plenty and in all sizes. This was the reason the Druids took the opportunity to build these immense works in this region. The people call these great stones **sarsens**, and there is a proverb here: *"as hard as a sarsen."* This is a purely **Phoenician** word <note>Stukeley’s own theory; modern etymology suggests "sarsen" comes from "Saracen" (meaning foreign or pagan)</note> that has continued here since the earliest times, signifying a rock. The very name of **Tyre** is derived from this, as discussed extensively and learnedly by **Bochart** in *Canaan II. 10* <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a French Protestant biblical scholar</note>. This whole region is a solid body of chalk covered with a most delicate turf. As this chalky matter hardened at the time of creation, it "spewed out" the most solid bodies of stone, which had a greater specific gravity than the chalk itself. Aided by centrifugal power—the rotation of the globe upon its axis—the earth threw them onto its surface, where they now lie. This is my opinion concerning this phenomenon, which I have often considered attentively.

It is worth the while of a curious observer to go toward the northern end of that great ridge of hills overlooking **Abury** from the east, called the **Hakpen** <note>Hackpen Hill</note>—an oriental name that has also survived from Druid times. A little to the right of the road coming from **Marlborough** to **Abury**, where there are three pretty barrows <note>ancient burial mounds</note> and another dish-shaped barrow, if we look down toward the side of the hill facing **Abury**, we can see many long, straight ridges of natural stone. These are the same as the **gray weathers**, appearing as if they are emerging out of the chalky surface. They are often crossed by others in straight lines, almost at right angles. For in this area, it seems that the chalk, contracting itself and growing closer...

<summary>The author laments the recent destruction of Avebury's stones for local building materials and offers a geological theory that the stones were "spewed out" by the Earth's rotation.</summary>

closer together, as it hardened, thrust the <term>lapidescent matter</term> <note>original: "lapidescent matter"; stone-forming mineral material</note> into these fissures. It is a very beautiful appearance. This is near that part of the downs called *Temple Downs*. There are no quarries, properly speaking, nearer *Avebury* than *Swindon*, and those have not long been dug. In *Calne* they dig up a paltry kind of stone, fit for nothing but mending the highways. But our <term>gray weather stone</term> <note>original: "gray weather stone"; these are sarsen stones, large sandstone blocks found lying on the surface of the Wiltshire downs</note> is of so hard a texture that Mr. Ayloffe of *Wootton Bassett* hewed one of them to make a <term>rape-mill stone</term> <note>original: "rape-mill stone"; a large stone used to crush rapeseed to produce oil</note>, and employed twenty yoke of oxen to carry it away. Yet so great was its weight that it repeatedly broke all his tackle in pieces, and he was forced to leave it. It may be said of many a one of our <term>gray weathers</term>:

->It is a natural mass; the thing gave its name to the place.<-
->Calling it a rock, it is a good part of the mountain. <note>original: "Est moles nativa, loco res nomina fecit. / Appellans saxum, pars bona montis ea est."</note> Ovid.<-

Lord *Pembroke* caused several of these stones to be dug under, and found them loose and detached. My lord computed the general weight of our stones at above fifty tons, and that it required a hundred yoke of oxen to pull one. Dr. *Stephen Hales* <note>An 18th-century clergyman and scientist known for his work in plant physiology</note> estimates the larger kind of them to be seven tons. Mr. *Edward Llwyd*, in his account of the natural history of *Wales* (*Philosophical Transactions Abridged, Vol. V. 2. p. 118*), writes that he found a strange appearance of great stones and loose fragments of rocks on the surface of the earth, not only on wide plains but on the tops of the highest mountains as well. So too are the <term>moor stones</term> <note>original: "moor stones"; typically granitic boulders found on moors</note> on the wastes and hilltops of *Cornwall*, *Derbyshire*, *Devonshire*, *Yorkshire*, and other places, which are of a harder nature than these and much the same as the *Egyptian* granite.

As to the internal texture of this stone, when broken, it looks whitish like marble; it would bear a pretty good polish if not for a large quantity of bluish <term>granules of sand</term> which are soft and give it a grayish or speckled color when smoothed by a machine. It consists, as all other stones do, of a mixture of various substances united by <term>lapidescent juices</term> <note>original: "lapidescent juices"; mineral fluids that harden into stone over time</note> over a sufficient period of time. Sometimes in one stone there shall be two or three colors, sometimes bits of <term>flints</term> kneaded amongst the rest. In one stone fetched from *Beckhampton* avenue, near *Longstone Barrow* (as it is commonly called) and which was broken and made into a wall at the little alehouse above *Beckhampton* on the *Devizes* road, I saw several bones—plainly animal—forming part of the composition of the stone. This I admired very much and concluded it to be <term>antediluvian</term> <note>original: "antediluvian"; dating from the time before the Great Flood mentioned in the Bible</note>. The stone in general is shining, dense, and hard, little inferior to common marble; yet the effect which time and weather has had upon it—far beyond what is visible at *Stonehenge*—must necessarily make us conclude the work to be many hundred years older in date. In some places, I could thrust my cane (a yard long) up to the handle into holes and cavities worn through by age, which must surely indicate a duration of several thousand years.

<summary>The author describes the geological properties and massive weight of the sarsen stones at Avebury, arguing that their extreme weathering proves the site is much older than Stonehenge.</summary>

# AVEBURY
### CHAPTER IV.

*The design of the temple of <term>Avebury</term> <note>original: "Abury"</note> is a circle and a serpent. <term>Hakpen</term>, another Eastern word still preserved here, means "the serpent's head." The <term>topography</term> <note>original: "chorography"; the description or mapping of a particular region</note> of Avebury. A description of the great circle of stones, 1,400 feet in diameter. Of the ditch enclosing it. The <term>rampart</term> <note>original: "vallum"</note> formed on the outside, like an amphitheater to the site. This represents the circle in the symbolic figure. Of the measurements, all referring to the ancient Eastern <term>cubit</term> <note>an ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note> which the <term>Druids</term> used.*

The situation of Avebury is excellently chosen for its intended purpose, being on the more elevated part of a plain from which there is an almost imperceptible descent in every direction. But as the religious work in Avebury, though great in itself, is only a part of the whole (the avenues stretching over a mile from it in each direction), the layout of the entire design is likewise planned with great judgment within a kind of large, separate plain, four or five miles in diameter. Into this you descend on all sides from higher ground. The country north of Avebury, around <term>Berwick Bassett</term> and <term>Broad Hinton</term>, is very high, though it does not appear to be so, and is much above the level of Avebury town. In a field at Broad Hinton, the water flows in two directions—into the <term>Thames</term> and the <term>Severn</term>—and people claim it is the highest ground in <term>England</term>. It is indeed part of that very great ridge of hills coming from <term>Somersetshire</term> and going from here northeast toward <term>White Horse Hill</term>. So the ground to the north and west, though not appearing so, is still very high, with a slope descending in that direction; and while guarded to the east by the <term>Hakpen</term> hill, it may be compared to the <term>Thessalian</term> valley of the same name:

->The Vale of Tempe, stirred by the West Winds. <note>original: "Zephyris agitata Tempe." From Horace, Odes 3.1.24. Tempe was a valley in Greece famous for its beauty.</note><-
->Horace<-

The entire temple of Avebury may be considered as a picture, and it truly is one. Therefore, the founders wisely arranged it so that a spectator would have an excellent view of it as they approached. To give the reader at once a prior knowledge of this great and wonderful work, and the magnificence of the plan upon which it is built, I have illustrated it in a perspective view in **PLATE VIII**. The eye is positioned somewhat higher than on the neighboring hill of <term>Wansdyke</term>, which is its proper vantage point, being south of it.

When I visited this place frequently, as I did for several years together to take an exact account of it, staying a fortnight at a time, I discovered the entire work by degrees. The second time I was here, one avenue was a new discovery. The third year, another. So at length I discovered the secret of it, properly speaking: which was that the whole figure represented a serpent passing through a circle. This is a symbol or hieroglyph of the highest significance and antiquity.

In order to carry out this design, the builders carefully studied their ground; and, to make their representation more natural, they skillfully laid it out over a variety of rises and dips in the land. This, combined with the curve of the avenues, successfully produces the desired effect. To make it even more elegant and like a picture, the head of the serpent is carried up the south-

### DESCRIBED.

The southern promontory of **Hackpen** Hill <note>original: "Hakpen"</note> faces toward the village of **West Kennet**. Indeed, the very name of the hill is derived from this fact, meaning "the head of the snake." Regarding this, we may well quote the poet Lucan (Book IV):

> From here, antiquity—the guardian of the old age, famous and an admirer of its own past—marked the lands with a name; but it gave even greater titles to these hills. <note>original: "Hinc ævi veteris custos, famosa vetustas / Miratrixque sui signavit nomine terras, / Sed majora dedit cognomina collibus istis."</note>

Likewise, the tail of the snake is led to the descending valley below **Beckhampton** <note>original: "Bekamton"</note>.

Thus, this ancient site <note>original: "our antiquity"</note> divides itself into three great parts, which will be our guide in describing the work: the circle at **Avebury** <note>original: "Abury"</note>; the front part of the snake leading toward **Kennet**, which I call **Kennet Avenue**; and the back part of the snake leading toward **Beckhampton**, which I call **Beckhampton Avenue**. These may well be viewed as avenues to the great temple at **Avebury**, which is the part that must be most specifically called "the temple."

This town's name is written as *Aubury, Avebury, Avesbury*, and sometimes *Albury*; it is hard to say which is the true version. The first three names may have their origin from the brook running nearby (*au* or *aux* meaning "water," or *awy* in Welsh; the old German is *aha*). The latter name points to *Aldbury*, or "old work," regarding its situation within the **rampart** <note>original: "vallum"</note>. Nor is it worth while to dwell on its etymology; the Saxon name is of such a recent date compared to what we are writing about that we expect no great use from it—unless **Avebury** refers to *al, hal*, or *healle* (<term>ⱠⱧⱧⱠⱧ</term> in Gothic), meaning a temple or great building.

There are two heads of the river **Kennet** rising near it: one from a little northwest of **Avebury**, at **Monkton**, runs southward to **Silbury Hill**; this affords but little water, except in wet seasons. At **Silbury Hill** it joins the **Swallowhead**, or true fountain of the **Kennet**, which the country people call by the old name, **Cunnit**, and it is quite famous among them. This is a plentiful spring. It descends between East and West **Kennet** by the temple on **Overton Hill**, which is properly the head of the snake; it passes by **Overton**, and so to **Marlborough**, the Roman **Cunetio**, which takes its name from the river.

To better guide the reader through this great work, I must remind them of what I wrote in the account of *Stonehenge* (page 11) concerning the **Druid cubit** or measure, by which they erected all their structures. It is 20 and four-fifths inches by the English standard. For this purpose, I have repeated the plate wherein the English foot and Druid cubit are compared to any lengths, which must necessarily accompany us in this description. <insert>A PLATE II.</insert> A ready way of understanding the relationship between our feet and these cubits is this: 3 feet, 5.5 inches equals 2 cubits. A staff of 10 feet, 4.5 inches (and a little more than half an inch) becomes the measuring rod of these ancient philosophers, being exactly 6 cubits. They used this when they laid out the ground plan of these temples, where we are now to pursue the track of their footsteps which so many ages have passed over.

The whole of this temple, in which the town of **Avebury** is included, I have laid down in **TABLE I** (the frontispiece), based on innumerable measurements. By these means, I fully learned the scheme and purpose of the founders. It is contained within a circular ditch or trench above 1,400 feet in diameter, which makes 800 cubits—equivalent to two **stadia** <note>original: "stadia"; an ancient Greek unit of distance</note> of the ancients. A radius of 400 cubits (one stadium) struck the inner boundary of the ditch in the turf. This was done with sufficient, though not mathematical, exactness.

# AVEBURY

...ness. They were not overly concerned with precise measurements on this large scale, since a perfect circle cannot be perceived by the eye when standing on the ground. The ditch is nearly 80 feet (about 45 cubits) wide and very deep, similar to the moat <note>original: "foss"</note> surrounding an old castle. The massive amount of solid chalk excavated from it was piled on the outside, forming a mighty embankment <note>original: *vallum*</note>—a terrace like an amphitheater—which hides the view of the town as you approach and provides good shelter from the wind. The base of this embankment is the same width as the top of the ditch. On August 16, 1721, Mr. Roger Gale and I measured its outer circumference to be about 4,800 feet.

I observed that the temple's enclosed area, which covers about 22 acres, has a gentle slope from its central north-south line <term>meridian line</term> toward both the east and the west, allowing rainwater to drain in both directions. The northern point is the highest part of the entire site. About 35 feet (20 cubits) inside the edge of this circular ditch sits a great circle of **massive stones**. The description "massive" is well-deserved. These enormous blocks are truly astonishing when you look at a single stone and consider how it was transported here and set upright in the ground, where I have no doubt it has stood for three or four thousand years.

But the wonder is even greater when you realize that one hundred stones make up this mighty circle, which is 1,300 feet in diameter! Although these stones are unshaped <term>unhewn</term>, they are generally about 15, 16, or 17 feet high and nearly as wide. The distance from the center of one stone to the center of the next is consistently about 43 English feet. By looking at the scale of the drawings, we can discern these measurements for the height and width of the stones: 17 feet equals ten cubits; 43 feet—the distance between the centers of the stones—is 25 "Druid cubits." This leaves a 15-cubit gap <note>The author calculates this by subtracting the 10-cubit width of the stone from the 25-cubit central distance.</note> between them.

While this was the standard measurement intended by the founders where the stones were of the largest size, we must understand that, as with all their works, there is some variation. The ancient builders aimed for a sense of grandeur; they sought to create an effect not through a rigid precision—which is impossible to notice in such large works—but by ensuring overall beauty. This is especially true of our Druids, who worked with these irregular masses because their religion forbade them from using tools on the stones. 

Their goal was to make the ratio of the stone's width to the gap between stones roughly two-to-three. They wisely judged that since the dimensions <term>scantlings</term> of such raw materials could not be exact, the proportions had to be adjusted to suit the individual stones, balancing the specific stones with the total distance that needed to be filled. These stones were all gathered from the surface of the downs <note>The Marlborough Downs are high chalk grasslands where "sarsen" stones are found naturally.</note>. They chose the best-shaped and largest stones first; however, when they had to use smaller stones, they placed them closer together. By balancing the solid stone and the empty space <note>original: "vacuity"</note>, they maintained a symmetrical appearance and a sense of regularity throughout the whole structure.

Therefore, while 25 cubits is the standard measurement between the centers of the largest stones, it is not an absolute rule. For instance, if we measure the two stones west of the north entrance (an entrance created for the convenience of the modern town by filling in the ditch with earth from the embankment <note>original: *vallum*</note>), the distance is only about 27 feet. This distance is only 16 Druid cubits; it was used here because these particular stones are of a more moderate size. The following gaps return to the usual 43 feet (25 cubits) where larger stones are used. This area is labeled "Pasture IV" on the site plan.

At first, I was often confused when trying to measure and reconcile these Druidic works—they almost seemed magical—until I mastered...

### [AVEBURY] DESCRIBED.

of their purpose. Therefore, to make it very plain to the reader, I shall repeat what I have delivered in other words concerning this great circle, which is a general rule for all others.

As to the construction of this circle, by diligent observation, I found this to be the art of the Druids. It is not to be thought they would go to the trouble of bringing so many mountains together and placing them in a regular form without seeking how to produce the best effect; and thus they obtained their purpose. As it was necessary that the stones should be rough and natural <note>original: "rude and native"</note>, untouched by any tool, and because it was impossible to procure them in exact dimensions, they considered that the beauty of their appearance must depend on their uniformity, as near as possible, and on the proportion between the solid stone and the empty interval. This ratio <note>original: *ratio*</note> they chose with judgment to be two to three: two parts for the breadth of the stone, and three parts for the interval. They applied this to the whole circle. Thus, they first brought 100 of their choicest stones together and laid them in the destined circle at the intended distances, according to that proportion, and then raised them into their respective places.

Hence I find that where the stones are 15, 16, or 17 feet high above ground, and as broad as they are for the most part—measuring about 43 *English* feet from the center of one stone to the center of another—the square of the solid stone is ten cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger. The author, William Stukeley, believed the Druids used a specific "Druid cubit" of about 20.8 inches.</note>, and the void or interval is 15 cubits. The total distance between centers is 25 cubits. Therefore, the proportion of the solid to the void is two to three.

But before I found out this key to the work, I met with a good deal of difficulty because the central intervals and the voids were different; they proportioned these to the breadths of the stones, as mentioned above. Still, they chose whole numbers of cubits for that proportion; for instance, at the stones of the northern and recent <note>original: "modern"</note> entrance, where the stones are only of a moderate size, you measure only about 27 feet in central distance. This is 16 cubits.

Further, I observed they took care to make a reasonable gradation between larger and smaller stones—not setting a large stone and a small one near each other, but making a gradual decrease in size. By this means, the eye finds no difference in the whole. The proportion of solid and void being the same, the entire circle appears uniform and altogether pleasing.

<margin>Plate <note>original: "Tab." (Tabula)</note> XII.</margin>
I thought it advisable to give an illustration of a very small part of this magnificent circle, showing three stones now standing in their original places <note>original: *in situ*</note>. It is a most majestic sight, from which we may learn something of the appearance of the whole.

I observed further that as these stones generally have a rough and a smoother side, they took care to place the most attractive side of the stone inwards, toward the enclosed area. For this vast circle of stones is to be understood as the portico <note>A porch or walkway with a roof supported by columns, here used metaphorically to describe the outer ring.</note> properly enclosing the temple. Between this circle and the ditch is an esplanade or circular walk going quite around, which was extraordinarily beautiful when in its perfection. It was originally 25 cubits broad, equal to the distances between the centers of the stones. The quickset hedges now on the site sometimes follow the line of the stones and are sometimes set on the edge of the ditch. Further, I observed they set the largest and handsomest stones in the more conspicuous part of the temple, which is toward the south, and around the two entrances of the avenues.

Out of this noble circle of 100 stones, there were more than 40 still visible in the year 1722 when I began to write, of which 17 were standing and 27 were thrown down or reclining. Ten of the remainder, all located next to each other, were destroyed all at once by *Tom Robinson* <note>Tom Robinson was a local stone-breaker notorious for destroying the megaliths at Avebury by heating them with fires and then dousing them with cold water to crack them.</note> in the year 1700, and their...

It measured a full 22 feet long. Reuben Horsall, the parish clerk—an intelligent man and a lover of antiquity—remembers seeing it standing. The original location <note>original: "seat"</note> of many stones is visible by the remaining hollow; others are marked by a mound above where they were buried. For many stones that were then recently carried off, their locations were obvious <note>original: "notorious"</note> because of the nettles and weeds growing there. No doubt many more have disappeared since I left the place. However, because the site plan represents the true state of the town and temple as it was when I frequented it, I will spare the reader’s patience by not being too detailed about the losses.

When this mighty colonnade of 100 stones was complete, there must have been a most pleasant circular walk between them and the ditch. It is almost impossible for us today to imagine the grand and beautiful appearance it must have made at that time.

## CHAPTER 5.

*Of the two great temples included in the area of the great circle of stones. Each consists of two concentric <note>original: "concentrick"</note> circles. One has a central obelisk or ambre (a very high stone in the center). The Egyptians called an obelisk an ambre. The other temple has a "cove" in the center, composed of three stones of a stupendous size, set in a niche-like shape. A short history of the destroyers of this noble work, from only a few years ago.*

The great circle of stones last described, along with the ditch and rampart enclosing everything, may be considered the *precinct* <note>original: "præcinctus"; the outer boundary of a sacred area</note> of the temple, or rather the area containing it, but not the temple itself. Strictly within this large compass are two temples of similar form and dimensions. Each temple consists of two concentric circles. The line that connects their centers runs from northwest to southeast; this line passes through the center of the entire area. Their outer circles each consist of 30 stones of the same size as those in the large outer circle and at the same intervals. The inner circles of both consist of 12 stones each, of the same size and spacing. Therefore, the geometry of these circles, when laid out on paper, shows that the inner circle must be 100 cubits <term>cubit</term> in diameter and the outer circle 240.

<term>A cubit is an ancient unit of measurement. The author, William Stukeley, believed the Druids used a specific "Egyptian" or "Hebrew" cubit of about 20.8 inches to design these monuments.</term>

The centers of these two double circles are 300 cubits apart. Their circumferences (the outward circles) are 50 cubits apart at their nearest point. By this arrangement, they interfere with each other as little as possible and leave the most open space around them within the great circular portico <note>The author compares the outer circle of stones to a colonnade or covered walkway</note> that encloses the whole site, which we described in the previous chapter. There is no other real difference between these two temples that I could discover, except that the southern one has a central obelisk. This was the *kibla* <note>original: "kibla"; the direction toward which a person turns during prayer or ritual</note> toward which they turned their faces during the religious ceremonies performed there. The other temple has that immense work in the center which the ancient Britons call a "cove." This consists of three stones placed at an obtuse angle toward each other, arranged as if on the arc of a circle—much like the large semicircular area at the east end of some old cathedrals, or like the upper end of the "cell" at Stonehenge. It served the same use and intent: it was the *adytum* <note>original: "adytum"; the innermost sanctuary or "holy of holies" of a temple</note> of this temple. I have often admired this structure and been astonished by its extraordinary size and majesty. It stands in the yard belonging to the inn. King Charles II, during his journey through this area, rode into the yard specifically to view it.

This cove of the northern temple was undoubtedly its *kibla*. It opens almost exactly to the northeast, just as at Stonehenge. It measures 34 feet (20 cubits) across from the edge of the outer side-posts, and is half that much in depth. Varro, in the fifth book of his *Divine Things* <note>original: "V. divinorum"</note>, writes that altars were anciently called *handles* <note>original: "ansæ"</note>. Macrobius says the same in *Saturnalia*, book 2, chapter 11 <note>original: "saturn. II. 11."</note>. It seems they are referring to the figure we see here. I suppose this is also what Schedius means in his book *On the German Gods* <note>original: "de dis germ."</note>, chapter 25; speaking of altars among the ancient Germans set in a triangle, he says the Druids understood a mystery by that shape. Perhaps they intended it to be a niche-like, hemispherical figure, intended in some way to represent the heavens. Sextus Pompeius writes that the ancients called...

# ABURY

...called by some the heavens, or the <term>cove</term>. The altar properly lay upon the ground before this superb niche. That stone, no doubt, was carried off long ago, as it was not fixed in the earth; one of the wings is gone as well—the northern one. It fell down in 1713, as marked in the ground-plot.

->*A sound is made as the earth is shaken by a massive weight.* <note>original: "Fit sonus ingenti concussa est pondere tellus." — Virgil, Aeneid</note><-

They told me it was a full seven yards long and of the same shape as the stone opposite it: tall and narrow. We measured the remaining stone at 17 feet above ground (exactly 10 <term>cubits</term>), 7 feet broad, and two and a half feet thick. These were the <term>ansae</term> <note>original: "ansæ"; Latin for "handles" or "wings"</note> or wings of this noble ellipsis. The stone on the back, or in the middle, is much broader, being 15 feet wide, 15 feet high, and 4 feet thick; however, a large piece of one side has broken off due to the natural decay of the <term>stone</term>. We cannot imagine anything bolder than the vision of the people who intended to set up these <term>stones</term>. The common people call them the <term>devil's brand-irons</term> <note>A "brand-iron" or andiron is a large metal support used to hold logs in a fireplace; the name reflects the stones' massive, chimney-like appearance</note>, because of their extravagant bulk and chimney-like form. These <term>coves</term>, as the traveler <term>Maundrell</term> says of the <term>Turkish kiblas</term> <note>A "kibla" or Qibla is the direction of prayer; here it refers to a niche in a wall indicating that direction</note>, show the <term>Druids'</term> aversion to idolatry. They express the reality of the divine presence there while simultaneously acknowledging its invisibility; this was undoubtedly a most ancient and Oriental custom.

Of the exterior circle of this northern temple, only three <term>stones</term> are now left <term>standing</term>. Six more lie on the ground, one of which is in the <term>street</term> by the <term>inn-gate</term>. People still alive remember several <term>standing</term> in the middle of the street; they were burnt <note>Stones were "burnt" by heating them with large fires and then dousing them with cold water to crack them into manageable building blocks</note> for building material in the year 1711. The stone at the corner of the lane leading to the north gate of the town was lying on the ground not many years ago and was used as a <term>stall</term> to lay fish on when a market was held here. The ruins of the rest are noted in the ground-plot, as are the others. However, they told us that about twelve years ago, both circles were <term>standing</term> and almost entire. Those in the enclosures behind the inn were taken up a year ago (this was when I first went there, around 1718); farmer <term>Green</term> chiefly demolished them to build his house and walls at <term>Beckhampton</term>. Of the southern temple, several <term>stones</term> were destroyed by farmer <term>John Fowler</term> twelve years ago; he admitted to us that he burnt five of them. Fourteen are still left, about half of which are <term>standing</term>. Some lie flat in the pastures, two are set into the ground under a barn, and others are under the houses. One lies above ground under the corner of a house across from the inn. One is buried under the earth in a small garden. The hollows left by others are visible, and ash trees have been planted in those spots. All those in the pastures were <term>standing</term> within living memory.

The central <term>obelisk</term> of this temple has a <term>circular base</term> of vast bulk, 21 feet long and 8 feet 9 inches in diameter; when it was <term>standing</term>, it was higher than the rest. This is what the Scripture calls a <term>pillar</term> or <term>standing image</term> in <term>Leviticus 26:1</term>. These works, erected in the land of <term>Canaan</term> by the same people—the <term>Phoenicians</term>—who built ours, were ordered to be demolished by the <term>Israelites</term> because they had been perverted to idolatry at that time. All the <term>stones</term> of our entire temple were called <term>ambres</term> <note>Stukeley connects this to "Ambresbury" (Amesbury) and suggests it means "sacred" or "anointed"</note> even by our <term>Phoenician</term> founders, but this stone in particular was so named. The <term>Egyptians</term> called their <term>obelisks</term> by that name, which the scholar <term>Kircher</term> did not rightly understand, interpreting it to mean <term>sacred books</term>. It actually means <term>ambrosial stones</term> <note>original: "petræ ambrosiæ"</note>, or <term>main ambres</term> in <term>Celtic</term>, meaning an <term>anointed, consecrated stone</term>. <term>Manah</term> was the name of a great <term>stone</term> of this sort which the <term>Arabians</term> worshipped. They were also called <term>gabal</term>, from which the current word <term>kibla</term> or <term>kebla</term> is derived, though now used in a broader sense. The name <term>Elagabalus</term> <note>A Roman emperor who served as a high priest to a sun god represented by a black conical stone</note> is derived from this, from after the time they turned these <term>kiblas</term> into actual deities. It means "the god obelisk."

# ABURY

called the heavens, cove. The altar properly lay upon the ground before this superb niche. That, no doubt, was carried off long ago, as not being fixed in the earth, and one of the wings is gone too—the northern one. It fell down in 1713, as marked in the ground-plot.

->*The earth was shaken and gave forth a sound under the vast weight.* <note>original: "Fit sonus ingenti concussa est pondere tellus." — Virgil</note><-

They told me it was full seven yards long, of the same shape as its opposite, tall and narrow. We measured this at 17 feet above ground (10 whole cubits); 7 feet broad, and two and a half thick. These were the <term>ansae</term> <note>Latin: "handles" or "wings"</note> or wings of this noble ellipsis. That on the back, or in the middle, is much broader, being 15 feet wide, 15 feet high, and 4 feet thick; but a great piece of one side of it has been broken off by the decay of the stone. We cannot conceive anything bolder than the idea of those people who entertained a design of setting up these stones. The common people call them the "devil’s brand-irons" because of their extravagant bulk and chimney-like form. These coves, as Henry <term>Maundrell</term> <note>An English academic and traveler (1665–1701) known for his descriptions of the Levant.</note> says of the <term>Turkish kiblas</term> <note>A Qibla is the direction Muslims face during prayer, usually marked by a niche (mihrab) in a mosque wall.</note>, show the <term>Druids'</term> aversion to idolatry, expressing the reality of the divine presence there and, at the same time, its invisibility; no doubt a most ancient and oriental custom.

Of the exterior circle of this northern temple, only three stones are now standing, and six more are lying on the ground, one of which is in the street by the inn gate. People yet alive remember several standing in the middle of the street; they were broken up using fire <note>original: "burnt for building" — A common 18th-century method of destroying megaliths involved heating them with large fires and then dousing them with water to make them crack.</note> for building materials in the year 1711. The stone at the corner of the lane leading to the north gate of the town, which not many years ago lay on the ground, was used as a stall to lay fish on when they had a kind of market here. The ruin of the rest is noted in the ground-plot, and so are the others. But they told us that about twelve years ago, both circles were standing and almost entire. Those in the enclosures behind the inn were taken up a year ago (this was when I first went there, about 1718); farmer <term>Green</term> chiefly demolished them to build his house and walls at <term>Beckhampton</term>. Of the southern temple, several stones were destroyed by farmer <term>John Fowler</term> twelve years ago; he admitted to us that he destroyed five of them, but fourteen are still left, about half of which are standing. Some lie along the pastures, two are set into the ground under a barn, and others under the houses. One lies above ground under the corner of a house across from the inn. One is buried under the earth in a little garden. The cavities left by several more are visible, in the places where ash trees have been planted. All those in the pastures were standing within living memory.

The central obelisk of this temple is of a circular form at the base and is of a vast bulk, being 21 feet long and 8 feet 9 inches in diameter; when standing, it was higher than the rest. This is what the Scripture calls a "pillar" or "standing image" in *Leviticus 26:1*. These works, erected in the land of <term>Canaan</term> by the same people—the <term>Phoenicians</term>—who erected ours, were ordered to be demolished by the <term>Israelites</term> because at that time they had been perverted to idolatry. All the stones of our whole temple were called <term>ambres</term> <note>Stukeley believed this word related to "ambrosia" or "anointed," suggesting the stones were sacred or consecrated.</note> even by our Phoenician founders, but this one particularly. The <term>Egyptians</term> called their <term>obelisks</term> by that name, which the scholar <term>Kircher</term> did not rightly understand, interpreting it to mean "sacred books." However, it actually means <term>ambrosial stones</term> <note>original: *petræ ambrosiæ*</note>, or "main ambres" in Celtic: anointed, consecrated stones. <term>Manah</term> is the name of a great stone of this sort which the <term>Arabians</term> worshipped. They were likewise called <term>gabal</term>, and the present word <term>kibla</term> or <term>kebla</term> comes from it, but in a larger sense. The name <term>Elagabalus</term> <note>A Roman emperor whose name derived from the Syrian sun-god, worshipped as a conical stone.</note> is derived from this after they turned these <term>kiblas</term> into real deities. It means "the god obelisk."

# DESCRIBED. Tᴀʙ. XV.

...and hence our English words: <term>gable end of a house</term>, <term>javelin</term> or <term>rompite</term> <note>original: "rompite"; likely a reference to a type of broken spear or weapon</note>, and <term>gaveloc</term> <note>original: "gaveloc"; an old English word for a spear or crowbar</note>, a sharp <term>iron bar</term>.

Exactly in the southern end of the line that connects the two centers of these temples—namely, in that pasture marked IX in our ground-plot—is an odd stone standing, not of great size. It has a hole carved into it and probably was designed to fasten the victim in order to slay it. This I call the <term>ring-stone</term>. From this, we may infer a similar use for that stone at <term>Stonehenge</term>, in the avenue near the entrance into the area of the temple. I spoke of it under the name of <term>crwm leche</term> <note>original: "crwm leche"; Welsh for "curved stone" or "crouching stone," often used for megalithic structures</note> on page 33. It has a similar hole in it. These two temples were all that were standing originally in the great area (Tables XIII, XIV, XVI, XVII) within the circular colonnade. Very probably it was the most magnificent <term>patriarchal temple</term> in the world. Now, a whole village of about thirty houses is built within it. This area would hold an immense number of people at their <term>panegyres</term> <note>original: "panegyres"; public assemblies or religious festivals</note> and public festivals; and when the <term>vallum</term> <note>original: "vallum"; the outer earthwork wall or rampart</note> all around was covered with spectators, it formed a most noble amphitheater and had an appearance extremely majestic during the administration of religious offices.

---

->— *The court receives thirty cows,*<-
->*and is wet with blood sprinkled abundantly.*<-
->*<note>original: "ter denas curia vaccas / Accipit, & largo sparsa cruore madet." — Ovid, Fasti, Book IV</note>*<-

Each of these temples is four times as big as <term>Stonehenge</term>.

About 1694, <term>Walter Stretch</term>, father of one of the present inhabitants, found out the way of demolishing these stones by fire. He exercised this at first on one of the stones standing in the street before the inn, belonging to the outer circle of the southern temple. That one stone, containing 20 loads, built the dining-room end of the inn. Since then, <term>Tom Robinson</term>, another <term>Herostratus</term> <note>An ancient Greek who destroyed the Temple of Artemis for fame; here used to mean someone who destroys something valuable for notoriety or profit</note> of the place, made cruel havoc among them. He owned to us that two of them cost eight pounds to destroy. For <term>Green</term> ruined many stones of the southern temple to build his houses and walls at <term>Bekamton</term> <note>Beckhampton</note>. Since then, many others have occasionally practiced this sacrilegious method, and most of the houses, walls, and outhouses in the town are raised from these materials. Sir <term>Robert Holford</term> resented this destruction of them; and <term>Reuben Horsall</term>, the parish clerk, had a due respect for these sacred remains and assisted me with the best information he was able to give. Concerning the purpose of the arrangement and manner of the temples described so far, I shall speak more at length in chapter 10 toward the end, concluding this with an inscription of the <term>Triopian farm</term> consecrated by <term>Herodes Atticus</term>.

*Let it not be lawful for anyone to move a clod of earth or a stone without being punished.*
*For the severe punishments of the Fates are close at hand*
*if anyone commits a crime in the sacred temple.*
*Neighboring farmers and neighbors, all take heed:*
*this land is sacred, and the rights of the gods remain unmoved.*

# DESCRIBED.

...more properly belongs. It serves to correct our notions concerning the <margin>Plate 10</margin> high antiquity of the temple we are writing upon. 1. We discern the skill of the Roman workmen in conducting their road along the precipitous side of this hill, while preserving a straight line as much as possible. 2. We see a part of the famous Wansdyke, or boundary of the <term>Belgic kingdom</term> in Britain, dug under their king Divitiacus, who is mentioned by Caesar in his <term>commentaries</term> <note>original: "commentaries" refers to Julius Caesar's *Commentarii de Bello Gallico*</note>. He built the neighboring town, Devizes, so called from his name, and it was most probably the city of his residence. I treated this matter in my work on *Stonehenge*. 3. We may remark the union of the Roman road and Wansdyke for some distance; this provides proof that Wansdyke was made before this Roman road, because the bank of the dike was thrown in to form the road. Caesar says this Divitiacus, king of the Suessions in Gaul, lived an age before him.

At the bottom of this hill is Heddington, another Roman town called Verlucio. Calne, less than five miles from Abury, was a Roman town too, where many <term>Roman coins</term> are found. I saw several of them myself. Since the Romans were very frequent in this country, it is no wonder their coins are found around Abury. I think I may well be excused from entering into a formal argument to prove that we must not conclude from this that the Romans were the founders of Abury. In my own opinion, having duly considered these affairs, the temple of the <term>Druids</term> here is as much older than the Roman times as the Roman times are older than our own.

Let us return down Runway Hill and contemplate that most agreeable prospect, of which I have given a faint representation in Plate 11. <margin>Plate 11</margin> Here we see the whole course of this Bath Road <note>original: "Via Badonica"</note> from here in a straight line to Marlborough, by Silbury Hill—the great tomb of the founder of Abury. I saw several <term>Roman coins</term> found along this road on Overton Hill, near the White Hart alehouse. On the left hand is the strong <term>Roman camp</term> of Oldbury. Everywhere we behold great numbers of the barrows of the ancient Britons, which face the temple of Abury. On the right hand, we may discern a vast length of the Wansdyke, carried along the northern edge of the high range of hills parting north and south Wiltshire. Below is a pretty work like a Roman camp cut into the fine turf. It would seem to be something belonging to the <term>Druids</term>, which I will discuss later.

Besides some <term>Roman coins</term> accidentally found in and around Abury, I was informed of a square bit of iron taken up from under one of the great stones when it was pulled down. I could not learn specifically what it was, though no doubt it belonged to the <term>British founders</term>. They also found a brass axe-head under an ash tree dug up near the smith's shop by the church. I understood from the description they gave of it that it was one of those Druid axes or instruments called <term>Celts</term> <note>original: "Celts" refers to prehistoric stone or bronze axe heads</note>, with which they cut the <term>mistletoe</term>. They fastened it occasionally to the end of a staff, which they commonly carried in their hands as one of the symbols <note>original: "insignia"</note> of their office, similar to a <term>pastoral staff of bishops</term>.

When Lord Stawell, who owned the manor of Abury, leveled the rampart <note>original: "vallum"</note> on the side of the town next to the church (where the barn now stands), the workmen reached the original surface of the ground. This was easily discernible by a black layer <note>original: "stratum"</note> of mold upon the chalk. Here they found large quantities of <term>bucks' horns</term>, bones, oyster shells, and charcoal <note>original: "wood coals"</note>. The old man who was employed in the work says there was a cart-load's worth of the horns, that they were very rotten, and that there were many burnt bones among them.

<page-num>30</page-num>

# ABURY

In the year 1722, I recorded everything in the form of a survey on large imperial sheets of paper, writing a detailed account of every stone, whether present or missing. However, it would be very tedious to burden the printing press with all of it. I will recount only what I consider most useful and necessary.

Standing at the southern entrance of *Abury* <note>original: Abury; modern: Avebury</note>, the first stone lies on the eastern side (your left hand) close to the ditch. The stone that was opposite it stood where a sycamore tree is planted today. The next stone on the right-hand side is still standing by the turning of the *Bath* road. The next twenty-four stones on both sides have been hauled away. After about twenty intervals while traveling along the road to *Kennet*—which follows the path of the avenue—we descend into a gentle valley and lose sight of *Abury*. In that spot, you can see the curve of the avenue, as many stones remain together on both sides. Here, two stones stand opposite each other. I measured them at nearly 60 feet apart, which equals 34 <term>cubits</term> <note>Stukeley believed the builders used a "Druid cubit" of about 20.8 inches.</note>. Then we ascend a small hillock again, where a significant number of stones remain on both sides.

<margin>PLATE 19 <note>original: TAB. XIX.; abbreviations for "Tabula," meaning a map or illustration.</note></margin>
In an enclosed field <term>original: cloſe</term> on the left side of the avenue (to the east) not far from the town of *Abury*, there is a five-sided stone lying flat on the ground. In the middle of it, a basin has been carved that is always full of water but never overflows. The local people hold it in high regard. The water comes from a spring underneath, and for all I know, it may have been here since our temple was first founded. When leaving *Abury*, you will notice that the line of the avenue points toward *Overton Hill* ahead of you, but you soon find that it turns away and curves slightly to the right. At the 65th stone on each side, you reach a hedge belonging to the enclosed fields of *West Kennet*. In the year 1720, I saw several stones that had just been dug up there and broken for use in building; fragments were still visible, and the spots where they stood had been freshly covered with turf for use as pasture. Wherever grain fields or pastures have encroached upon the sacred ground, the ancient work is generally ruined. Where the heath remains untouched, it is still in good condition; as the great poet Virgil said:

> "Meanwhile, the unplowed land has its own charms." <note>original: *Nec nulla interea est inaratae gratia terrae* from Virgil's *Georgics*.</note>

Thus, the greedy farmer and the rancher have conspired to destroy this most magnificent monument, happening right around the time I was there. *Charles Tucker*, Esq., formerly of *East Kennet*, a sensible gentleman, used to be very angry about the destruction of these stones and prevented it as much as he could.

As for the stones that made up this avenue, they were of various shapes, sizes, and heights, remaining completely unworked <term>original: rude</term>. We measured some that were six feet thick and sixteen feet in circumference. If a stone was flat, its broadest side was placed along the line of the avenue, with the most attractive side facing inward. The builders realized that the only effect required was their massive size and their orderly placement. All the hilltops we pass, especially the *Hakpen* <note>Hackpen Hill</note>, are adorned with burial mounds <term>original: barrows</term>. When the avenue reaches the previously mentioned fields of *West Kennet*, it passes through three of them, crosses a small field lane, and the main road from *Marlborough* to *Bath*, just after the road makes a sharp turn while descending from *Overton Hill*. It should be noted that we have been climbing uphill for quite some time. At this turn, the *Roman* road from *Marlborough* comes down the hill and joins the main road. This is the *Via Badonica* <note>The Roman road leading toward Bath (Aquae Sulis).</note> mentioned earlier.

*John Fowler*, who ran the nearby tavern, destroyed many of these stones by heating them with fire <note>Stukeley describes "burning" elsewhere as a method where stones were heated with fires and then doused with water or struck with hammers to break them into manageable building materials.</note>. The tavern (the *White Hart*) and the walls surrounding it were built using the fragments of just one single stone.

<meta>This page continues the description of the stone avenues leading to Avebury, specifically focusing on the route toward Overton Hill and the destruction of the site known as "the Sanctuary."</meta>

## DESCRIBED.

As mentioned before, the avenue coming out of Avebury <note>original: "Abury"</note> town bent to the right; now it is easily discernible that it makes a mighty curve to the left, the better to imitate the creature for which it was intended <note>Stukeley believed the monument was designed in the shape of a giant serpent (a "dracontia").</note>.

->It becomes stone, and the rock preserves the image of the serpent. <note>original Latin: *Fit lapis, & servat serpentis imagine saxum.* Ovid, Metamorphoses, Book XII.</note><-

Passing the Roman road, it crosses an angle of a pasture and falls into the upper part of the same road again. It marches through two more pastures, all along the side of the quickset hedge <note>a hedge grown from living plant cuttings, usually hawthorn</note>, so that the hedge is planted among the stones. The stones are seen lying in their proper places, both in the pastures and in the road. These stones are all thrown down or reclining, and are very large. We measured one by the stile that was 12 feet long, 6 and a half feet broad, and 3 and a half feet thick.

At the bottom of these pastures on the right runs the virgin stream of the River Kennet, just separated from its source by Silbury Hill. One stone is standing by a little green lane going down to the river. Now our avenue marches directly up the hill across some plowed fields, still by the hedge of the Marlborough road, where another stone belonging to it still stands. Then we are brought to the very summit of the celebrated Overton Hill, properly called the *Hakpen* <note>meaning "head of the snake"</note> or head of the snake, which is 7,000 feet from the rampart <note>original: "vallum"</note> of Avebury town. According to Herodotus (Book II), 400 cubits was the *stadium* <note>an ancient Greek unit of distance</note> of the ancients, our furlong; a distance that Hercules is said to have run in one breath. Had the side-interval of the stones of this avenue been the same throughout—50 cubits—that repeated 100 times (the number of the intervals) would produce 5,000 cubits. But because, as I said, they lessened this interval proportionally as they came to the neck of the snake, it amounts to 4,000 cubits, which is ten *stadia*, an eastern mile in Dr. Arbuthnot’s tables <note>John Arbuthnot published "Tables of Ancient Coins, Weights and Measures" in 1727</note>. This amounts to 7,000 feet, as Mr. Roger Gale and I measured its whole length.

We observe the proportion between the diameter of the great circle of Avebury town, which is 800 cubits (two *stadia*), and the length of the avenue, which is five times the other. Observe further, they carried the avenue up the side of the hill at such a slope as to make the ascent gradual and easy.

<margin>Plate XX.</margin>
The country people have held a high regard for this Overton Hill from time immemorial. It was (alas, it was!) a very few years ago crowned with a most beautiful temple of the Druids. They still call it "the Sanctuary." I do not doubt but that it was an asylum <note>a sacred place of refuge</note> in Druid times, and the veneration for it has been handed down through all successions of times and people, as seen in the name and several other particulars that will be mentioned occasionally. It had suffered a good deal when I took that view <note>original: "prospect"</note> of it with great accuracy <note>original: "fidelity"</note> in the year 1723, which I give the reader in Plate XXI. <margin>Plate XXI.</margin> Then, about sixteen years ago, the aforementioned farmer Green took most of the stones away for his buildings at Beckhampton; and in the year 1724, farmer Griffin plowed half of it up. But the vacancy left by every stone was most obvious, the hollows still left fresh; and that part of the two circles which I have drawn in the plate was exactly as I have represented it. In the winter of that year, the rest were all carried off and the ground was plowed over.

I did not lament the loss of this work alone; all the neighbors (except the person who gained the little dirty profit) were heartily grieved for it. It had a beauty that touched them far more than those much greater circles in Avebury town. The stones here were not large, yet were set pretty close

Stukeley records the tragic destruction of this site by local farmers for building material and agricultural use in the 1720s.</summary>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the stone avenue leading to Overton Hill, focusing on the "Sanctuary" temple at the "head" of the stone serpent and the surrounding landscape.</meta>

# ABURY

Standing close together, the proportions of the stones and the intervals between them, along with the relationship between the two circles—all being taken in at one view—charmed those who saw them. The great stones of the large circles at <term>Avebury</term> <note>original: "Abury"</note> were not easily perceived by the locals to be arranged in circles, nor would they easily be persuaded of it. But they still speak of these stones of the <term>Sanctuary</term> <note>a Bronze Age monument on Overton Hill that was destroyed in the 1720s</note> with great pleasure and regret.

This <term>Overton Hill</term>, where the elegant temple we are speaking of once stood, is a very pleasant place. It is the southern end of the ridge called the <term>Hakpen</term>, which is broken off by the River <term>Kennet</term>. All the water that falls in the plain where the entire work of Avebury stands flows down this way. It is a round knoll with a gentle slope to the east, west, and south. The Kennet, as it were, licks its feet on all those sides. The entire hill takes its name from this southern end.

Our name for <term>Hakpen</term> alludes to the Hebrew word *ochim* (אדם), called "doleful creatures" in our translation of *<term>Isaiah</term>* 13:21, which speaks of the desolation of <term>Babylon</term>: "Wild beasts of the desert shall lie there, and their houses shall be full of *ochim*, and owls shall dwell there, and satyrs shall dance there." St. <term>Jerome</term> <note>the 4th-century priest and scholar who translated the Bible into Latin</note> translates it as "serpents." The <term>Arabians</term> call a serpent *Haie*, and wood-serpents *Hageshin*; and from those comes our <term>Hakpen</term>. *Pen* is the word for "head" in the British language <note>Old Welsh or Brythonic</note>.

<margin>PLATE XXI.</margin> <gloss>serpent</gloss> The word *acan* in <term>Chaldee</term> <note>Aramaic</note> signifies a serpent, and *hak* is nothing other than "snake"; the breath in the pronunciation naturally degenerated into a hissing sound, as is often the case, and especially so with this hissing animal. Thus, the Latin *super* [above] comes from the Greek *hyper* (υπεר), the Latin *sylva* [forest] from the Greek *hyle* (υλνη), and the Latin *sudor* [sweat] from the Greek *hydor* (υδωρ) [water]. Similarly, our word "snap" comes from the French *happer* [to snatch], and a "snacot" fish <note>likely a pipefish or needlefish</note> from the Latin *acus* [needle], or *aculeatus piscis* [spiny fish]. In <term>Yorkshire</term>, they call snakes "hags" and "hag-worms." See *<term>Fuller's Miscellany, Book IV, Chapter 15</term>*.

The temple that stood here was intended to be the head of the snake in the huge landscape picture; and at a distance, when seen in perspective, it represents it very aptly. It consisted of two concentric ovals, which were not much different from circles, with their longest diameter running east and west. By the best information I could obtain from its ruins, the outer circle was between 80 and 90 <term>cubits</term> <note>an ancient unit of measure based on the length of the forearm, about 18–20 inches</note> in diameter, with the average being 85 cubits (146 feet). It consisted of 40 stones, of which 18 remained (left behind by the farmer named *Green*), but only three were still standing. The inner circle was 26 to 30 cubits in diameter, which was equal to the distance between the outer and inner circles.

The stones in the inner circle were 18 in number and somewhat larger than those in the outer circle, but they were all carried off by the aforementioned Mr. *Green*. Everyone here remembers seeing both circles entire and standing, except for two or three that had fallen.

Mr. *Aubrey* <note>John Aubrey (1626–1697), a pioneering English archaeologist</note>, in his manuscript notes printed with <term>Camden's Britannia</term>, mentions it as "a double circle of stones, four or five feet high, though many are now fallen down." He states the diameter of the outer circle was 40 yards and the inner was 15 yards. He speaks of the avenue coming up to it, as well as our previously described avenue from Avebury to <term>West Kennet</term> set with large stones. He says one side is very nearly entire, while the other side is missing a great many stones. He did not see that it is actually only one avenue from Avebury to Overton Hill, as he had no understanding of the double curve it makes. He also erred in saying there was a circular ditch on Overton Hill.

The view from here is extensive and beautiful. Looking down the river eastward, we see <term>Marlborough</term> and the entire course of the <term>Roman</term> road going from here along <term>Clatford Bottom</term>. We see a long way down the road to <term>Amesbury</term> <note>original: "Ambresbury"</note> and the gap in the <term>Wansdyke</term> where we pass through. From there, the Wansdyke skims the edge of all the hilltops to <term>Runway Hill</term>. There we enter upon the view presented in Plate 21. The Roman road runs upon the edge of the hill on the right hand of that plate, between the burial mounds <note>original: "barrows"</note> there. It descends the hill and runs to the left of <term>Silbury</term> Hill, and close by it; and then up Runway—

## DESCRIBED

From Runway Hill, we next see Oldbury Camp overlooking the village of West Kennet. Then we may view the entire length of the avenue from here to Abury and observe the two great curves it makes to imitate the figure of a snake, as drawn in ancient hieroglyphics. Coming from the town of Abury, the path curves to the right (eastward), then winds just as much to the west, until it ascends this Overton Hill, facing directly east.

I observed that the width of the avenue here is narrower than elsewhere, representing the neck of the snake. It is 45 feet or 26 cubits wide, which is equal to the diameter of the inner circle at this location. Because it is narrower, the builders made the side-distance between the stones proportional—about two-thirds of the width. A Mr. Smith, who lives here, informed me that when he was a schoolboy, the Kennet Avenue was entire from end to end. Silbury Hill corresponds directly to the avenue as it enters this temple, sitting due west from here. There are a great number of burial mounds <note>original: "barrows"</note> in sight from this place: two close by, and a little to the northeast, a chain of mounds (shown in the lower part of Plate 29) looking toward Marlborough. Human bones were found while digging a small ditch by the temple across some small mounds and in areas where there were no mounds at all. Mr. Aubrey <note>John Aubrey (1626–1697), a pioneering archaeologist who first recognized Avebury's significance.</note> says that sharp, shaped flints were found among them, which are evidence of great antiquity. These people were likely from the lower class of Britons who could not afford the cost of a proper burial mound <note>original: "tumulus"</note>.

Thus, we have followed one half—the forepart of the snake—in this <term>TAB. XX.</term> mighty work up to Overton Hill, where it rested its bulky head and, not long ago, made a most beautiful appearance. I happened to visit this place at the very last moment when it was still possible to preserve a memory of it. To do so, I have laid out the ground plan in Plate 20 exactly as I found it over three consecutive years before it was demolished. I found that a line drawn between Overton Mill and the entrance of Kennet Avenue in Abury town is the base-line of <term>TAB. XXI.</term> this avenue. From this line, it makes two vast curves in opposite directions to imitate the winding of a snake and the symbolic figures we see on Egyptian and other ancient monuments. From Overton Mill, there is a most glorious prospect, overlooking the whole extent of the Abury temple and the sacred field it stands in, and beyond that into Gloucestershire and Somersetshire.

->From here the earth spreads out into open fields,<-
->the eye barely grasping their scale.<-
->—Lucan, *Pharsalia*, Book IV<-
<note>original: "Explicat hinc tellus campos effusa patentes, Vix oculo prendente modum"</note>

As we descend Overton Hill by the neck of the snake, we can see the main <term>TAB.</term> part of the track of this avenue between here and Abury town, and we may <term>XXII.</term> observe its huge curves in both directions. When we are near the entrance to <term>TAB.</term> Abury town again, upon climbing the hill by the corner of the hedge (about eighteen <term>XVIII.</term> stone-intervals from the rampart <note>original: "vallum"</note>), you see a most advantageous view of the approach to the temple, partly represented in Plate 18. Windmill Hill, with its gentle slope, fronts you directly, while the northern end of Hackpen Hill on the right and Cherhill on the left close the horizon like scenes in a theater.

I observed many of these carefully planned opportunities in this work—using the terrain and views to make the landscape more like a painting. Pausanias, in his book on *Boeotia* <note>A region of ancient Greece described in his "Description of Greece."</note>, writes that on the road from Thebes to Glisas, there is a space fenced around with selected stones which the inhabitants call the "Snake's Head." They tell a silly story about it: that a snake put its head out of a hole there, and Tiresias <note>A famous blind prophet in Greek mythology.</note> struck it with his sword. Right by it, he says...

# ABURY

...is a hill called the supreme, and a temple to <term>Jupiter</term> the supreme, and the brook <term>Thermodon</term> <note>In Greek mythology, a river in Scythia; Stukeley is likely comparing Avebury's geography to classical descriptions of sacred landscapes.</note> runs under it. Can we doubt that this was an ancient temple, like the one we are describing? It was built by <term>Cadmus</term> <note>A legendary Phoenician prince; Stukeley believed the Druids shared a common ancestry with ancient Near Eastern and Mediterranean cultures.</note>, or some of his people, whom we shall discuss more in chapter XIV. 

I conclude this account with a verse from the poet, which I believe was written about a work of the very same nature, as we shall explain shortly.

->*What was formerly a head is now a peak on the highest mountain; the bones become stone.* — <term>Ovid</term>, Metamorphoses, Book 4. <note>original: "Quod caput antè fuit, summo est in monte cacumen, Ossa lapis fiunt"</note><-

### CHAP. VIII.

A description of the other great avenue from <term>Beckhampton</term> <note>original: "Bekamton"</note>, a mile away, which is the tail <note>original: "hinderpart"</note> of the snake extending from the circle. The cove midway along it is called <term>Longstones</term>, or the <term>Devil's Coits</term> <note>original: "coits" or "quoits," a name for large flat stones often associated in folklore with the devil throwing stones.</note>. The avenue ended in a valley. Some animal bones were found in a stone, leading to a conjecture regarding their age. Concerning the number of the stones. <term>Solomon’s Temple</term> compared with ours. The mechanical skills of the <term>Druids</term> were called magic. Concerning the effect of the weather upon the stones.

<margin>PLATE XXIII.</margin>After I had carefully laid out the plan of the <term>Kennet avenue</term> without understanding its full purpose, in the year 1722, I discovered this other one, extending over a mile from the town of <term>Avebury</term> <note>original: "Abury"</note> in another direction. It goes toward the village of <term>Beckhampton</term>; therefore, I call it the <term>Beckhampton avenue</term>. It is actually the tail of the hieroglyphic snake, which the <term>Druids</term> intended to depict here on this most extraordinary scale. 

The previous avenue leaves the town of Avebury at the southeast point; this one leaves due west, at an interval of 25 stones—or one quarter of the great circle—from the <term>Kennet avenue</term>, and proceeds by the south side of the churchyard. Two stones lie by the parsonage gate on the right hand. Those opposite them on the left, in a pasture, were removed in 1702, as marked in the ground-plan of Avebury. <term>Reuben Horsal</term> <note>The parish clerk of Avebury and Stukeley’s local informant.</note> remembers three standing in the pasture. One now lies in the floor of the house in the churchyard. A little further on, one lies at the corner of the next house on the right, by the lane turning off to the right toward the bridge. Another was broken into pieces to build that house in 1714. Two more lie on the left side, opposite. It then passes the brook <note>original: "beck"</note> south of the bridge. 

Most of the stones around here have been used for the bridge and the causeway leading to it. A small spring arises at <term>Horslip</term> to the northwest and runs past this spot toward <term>Silbury Hill</term>, where the true source of the Kennet River is. However, sometimes due to a sudden heavy rain coming from <term>Monkton</term> and <term>Broad Hinton</term>, this stream becomes very deep. The design here mimics reality so closely that this spot could be called the vent <note>The anatomical opening at the end of a serpent's body.</note> of the snake.

Now the avenue passes along a lane to the left of the <term>Calne road</term>, by a stone house called <term>Goldsmiths Farm</term>, and so through Farmer <term>Griffin’s yard</term>, through one barn that stands across the avenue, then by another which stands on its path...
