<meta>This page continues the description of the Beckhampton Avenue leading away from the central circles at Avebury toward the west.</meta>

### DESCRIBED.

Two stones and their opposites still lie in the foundation. Immediately after this, the path enters the open plowed fields, with the Calne road running north of it the entire way. If we look back and observe the position of the Avebury steeple and other landmarks, a discerning eye will find that the avenue makes a large northward curve. After entering the open cornfields, the avenue runs for some time alongside the hedge on the right hand. Once it has crossed the path leading from South Street, we can see remains of it here and there on the way to Longstone Cove. Farmer Griffin broke up nearly 20 stones from this section of the avenue.

This Longstone Cove (commonly called "the Long Stones") is technically a "cove," as it is composed of three stones, much like the magnificent one we described in the center of the northern temple at Avebury, behind the inn. They are arranged in the arc of a circle, facing each other at an obtuse angle. This cove is set on the north side of the avenue, and one of the stones on that side forms the back of the cove. This is the only detail in which this avenue differs from the first one <note>the Kennet Avenue</note>. I believe this was primarily a deliberate attempt at variety and served as a <term>sacellum</term> <note>original: sacellum; a small shrine or chapel</note> or <term>proseucha</term> <note>original: proseucha; a place of prayer or oratory</note> for the local neighborhood on ordinary days of worship—namely, the Sabbath. For if the Druids came here during Abraham’s time and were his disciples, as it seems to me, we cannot doubt their observance of the Sabbath. It stands at the midpoint of the avenue's length, being the fiftieth stone. This cove opens toward the southeast, just as the cove of the northern temple opens toward the northeast. It is placed upon an eminence, the highest ground that the avenue passes over; these are called the Longstone Fields because of it. You have a fine view from here, seeing Avebury (toward which the ground slopes down to the brook), Overton Hill, Silbury Hill, Beckhampton, and the beautiful countryside all around. Many stones along the way are buried just beneath the surface of the earth. Many lie in the boundary ridges and markers <note>original: balks and meres</note>, and many fragments have been moved to serve as field boundaries. However, within living memory, even more whole stones have been burnt to be used as building material. One stone still remains standing near Longstone Cove.

Because it stands in the open fields between the Calne road and the road to Bath, Longstone Cove is discussed more by the local people than the larger and more numerous stones in the town of Avebury. Dr. Musgrave mentions it in his *Belgium Britannicum* (page 44) and on his map. Mr. Aubrey, in his manuscript observations published with Mr. Camden’s *Britannia*, speaks of them by the name of the "Devil's Quoits" <note>original: Devil's coits</note>. At that time, three huge stones were still standing. It was truly a grand and noble work. The stone left standing is 16 feet high, 16 feet broad, and 3.5 feet thick. The back stone has fallen flat on the ground and is of similar dimensions.

-> <term>The passing of years and the decay of time have loosened it</term> <note>original Latin: annis solvit sublapsa vetustas</note> : <-
-> <term>A great mountain is carried headlong into the abyss</term> <note>original Latin: Fertur in abruptum magnus mons</note> — *Virgil, Aeneid, Book 12*. <-

The third stone was carried off by that destroyer Richard Fowler, along with many others, only seven years ago (at the time I was there). The people who saw it broken into pieces by fire assured me that there were perfect flints and bones within its composition. I truly believe I saw a piece of this same stone in a garden wall of the small alehouse down on the Beckhampton Road, which clearly had a bone embedded in it. From this, we may probably conclude that these stones were formed by nature after Noah's flood, and these bones belong to a pre-flood <note>original: antediluvian</note> animal that accidentally fell into the petrifying matter. They...

They told me the stone contained 20 large loads <note>original: "good loads"; a load was a standard measure of what a cart or wagon could carry</note>, that the bones were in the middle of the stone, and were as hard as the stone itself. That stone currently standing was the right-hand or eastern jamb <note>the side post of a doorway or opening</note> of the cove.

A little way from here is a small patch of heath, but the plow will soon have consumed it. Here remains a large barrow <note>an ancient burial mound</note>, called *Longstone long barrow*; and from here we see countless more barrows. The avenue continued its path through the cornfields. Three stones still lie by the field-road coming from *South Street* to the *Calne* road. Mr. *Alexander* told me he remembered several stones standing by the parting of the roads below *Beckhampton*, which were demolished by *Richard Fowler*. Then the path descends by the road to *Cherhill*, until it comes to the *Bath* road, close by the *Roman* road. There, in the low valley, it terminates near a fine group of barrows under *Cherhill Hill*, on the way to *Oldbury Camp*; this is west of the village of *Beckhampton*. This point, facing that group of barrows and looking up the hill, is a most solemn and impressive place. There is a descent all the way from *Longstone* cove, directed toward a further descent much further down the *Bath* road, where no fewer than five valleys meet. And at this very point alone, you can see the temple on *Overton Hill* on the south side of *Silbury Hill*.

Here I am fully satisfied that this avenue terminated, at the same distance from *Avebury* town as *Overton Hill* was in the former avenue: 100 stones on each side, 4,000 cubits in length; ten <term>stadia</term> <note>original: "stadia"; an ancient Greek unit of distance</note> or one eastern mile. Several stones are left scattered on the banks and boundaries <note>original: "meres"; landmarks indicating property lines</note> of the fields. One great stone belonging to this end of the avenue lies buried almost underground in the plowed land between the barrow west of *Longstone long barrow* and the last hedge in the village of *Beckhampton*. *Richard Fowler* showed me the ground here, where he removed several stones and broke them up. I am equally satisfied there was no temple or circle of stones at this end of it. 

1. Because it would be logically inconsistent in the design. The head of the snake was aptly represented by that double circle on *Overton Hill*; but this place, representing the tail of the snake, did not allow for such a thing. I have no doubt that it grew narrower and narrower, as we previously observed with the neck of the snake. 
2. There is not the slightest local report of such a thing among the country people. It would certainly have been well known, because every stone was destroyed within living memory when I was there. I have no doubt that many stones have been lost since, and I have received very distressing accounts of this. I believe this end of the avenue grew narrower in imitation of a snake's tail, and that a single stone stood in the middle of the end as a closing point. I infer this from the design of the end of the Druid temple's avenue at *Callanish* <note>original: "Classerness"; refers to the Callanish Stones on the Isle of Lewis</note>, which I take to be the tail of a snake. I will discuss this later.

For a more mathematical determination of this end of the avenue, see the end of Chapter 10.

The avenue took another circular sweep in the opposite direction as it descended from *Longstone* cove, bending southward.

And it traveled over various elevations and depressions, just like the other avenue at *Kennet*; but while that one ended on a hill, this one ended in a valley. With great judgment, the builders laid out the ground this way to make the whole scene more picturesque.

<meta>The author continues to discuss the religious and historical significance of the prehistoric temple at Avebury (Abury), comparing the scale of its gatherings to those of ancient Israel and Solomon’s temple. He then shifts to the engineering and mechanics behind the monument's construction.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
# A B U R Y

...A sacrifice of a hundred oxen was a <term>hecatomb</term> <note>original: "hecatomb"; an ancient Greek sacrifice of 100 oxen</note>. Twenty-two thousand were offered by Solomon at the dedication, besides other animals. Three times a year the whole nation of Israel assembled there to pay their devotions and perform sacrifices—the original covenant made between God and man to obtain favor and pardon. For all we know, there might have been just as many people here, and for the same reason. I believe their most common times for these extraordinary religious meetings were during the four quarters of the year: the equinoxes and solstices.

We may well wonder how these people could bring together so many of these great stones and set them up so exactly. They did not have to fetch the stones far—only from the other side of the <term>Hakpen</term> <note>The "Hakpen" refers to Hackpen Hill, a ridge overlooking Avebury</note>, from the "gray weathers" <note>local name for sarsen stones scattered on the downs</note>. Their proximity, the fact that they lay on the surface of the ground, and the soil here being solid <term>chalk</term> were the great inducements for the Druids to build this <term>temple</term> in these most early ages. The nature of their mechanics, which was undoubtedly very simple, must be equally surprising. I suspect they brought the stones on strong carriages and drew them by hand; for even in Caesar’s time, there was a vast multitude of people. Their method of raising the stones seems to have involved tall trees used as levers, and no doubt applied very skillfully. 

The method of fixing these enormous blocks of stone was to dig a hole in the solid chalk and ram the foundation in with smaller stones, flints, and "goggles" <note>likely referring to large rounded stones or cobbles</note> very artfully. They are not set in more than two and a half feet deep. And the country being an entire bed of solid chalk was another reason why they chose it for this extraordinary building, just as they did at <term>Stonehenge</term>. Conducting and rightly managing an immense number of workers, and providing for their maintenance, was a matter of wisdom and great authority. The marvelous effect produced might well establish the glory of the <term>Druids</term> of Britain, which echoed across the ocean and greatly supported the opinion mankind had formed about their practice of <term>magic</term>. 

For "magic" is nothing else but the science that teaches us to perform wonderful and surprising things, in the later sense of the word. And for many ages after the Druid times, mankind held the same notion—as common people still do to this day—concerning these works. Most probably, the characterization that Pliny gives of our British <term>Druids</term> practicing magic—and being such experts in it as to equal the <term>Persian and Chaldean magi</term>—sprang from this. "So much so," he says, "that one would even think the Druids had taught it to them" <note>original: Pliny's Natural History, Book 30, where he discusses the Druids' reputation for magic</note>. I judge it much more probable that the Druids learned it from them; at least, they both derive it from the same original source. 

And whatever they might have practiced of real magic, the public's notion of them was strengthened by the name <term>magi</term>, which they might have brought with them from the East. <term>Magus</term> there originally signifies nothing more than a priest or a person who officiates in sacred rites. The word comes from *maaghim meditabundi* <note>original: "maaghim meditabundi"; likely a mix of Hebrew 'ma'hag' (to meditate) and Latin 'meditabundi' (contemplative), meaning "people of a contemplative, retired life"</note>, whom they more commonly called <term>Druids</term> in the West. I am not hesitant in thinking the times we are discussing, when this <term>temple</term> of <term>Abury</term> was built, are of the remotest antiquity—near the time of Abraham. I was often on the spot, equipped with what I thought was a convincing argument based on the weathering: considering what effect the elements had on these stones of a very firm texture, a kind of gray <term>marble</term> <note>the author refers to sarsen stones as a kind of marble</note>. And thus my reasoning was founded.

I had sufficient opportunity to compare the effects of weather on the stones here and on those at <term>Stonehenge</term>. For several years, I went directly from one to the other, staying a fortnight or more at each place...

<summary>This page discusses the logistical and spiritual magnitude of Avebury, comparing its construction and assemblies to Biblical precedents and the reputation of the Druids as master builders and "magicians." The author argues that the monument's antiquity dates back to the era of Abraham, using evidence from geological weathering.</summary>
<keywords>Avebury, Abury, Druids, sarsen stones, megalithic construction, magic, Pliny, Solomon, Stonehenge, antiquity, archaeology</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's comparison between the construction methods of Stonehenge and Avebury, specifically focusing on the use of tools and the relative age of the two monuments.</meta>

DESCRIBED.

<image-desc>A decorative engraving featuring a central oval portrait of a man, labeled "RUBEN HORSALL Clerk of AVEBURY & Antiquarian, July 29 1723" <note>original: Clark of ABURY</note> in a circular inscription. The portrait shows a man with wavy hair and a coat, looking to the right. Surrounding the portrait and within the ornate frame are various archaeological or natural elements, including an urn, a structure resembling a stone monument or building, and trees. A rectangular cartouche near the right of the portrait contains the letters "D • M" <note>original Latin: "Diis Manibus," meaning "To the spirits of the dead."</note>.</image-desc>

This is the place to make my observations. Nothing is more obvious than that the stones of Stonehenge have been chiseled—some all the way around, some on three sides—which is easily distinguished. The stones of Avebury <note>original: Abury</note> are absolutely untouched by any tool. No doubt, at that time, the original patriarchal method used since the foundation of the world was observed: not to allow a tool to be used upon them <note>This refers to the biblical tradition, such as in Exodus 20:25, where altars were to be made of unhewn, natural stones.</note>. Even when Solomon's Temple was built, although the stones were all carved with great art, that work was done before they were brought to the building site; for no axe or hammer was heard there. The same may probably be said of Stonehenge.

It seems likely that when Stonehenge was built, the Druids had received some information from Phoenician traders about the nature of Solomon’s Temple. Therefore, they created their lintel <term>impost: a horizontal stone block resting on top of a pillar</term> work as a kind of advancement toward a covered temple, and they likewise chiseled their stones to comply with that style. By using my best judgment to compare the effects of the weather on Stonehenge and Avebury, I could easily be convinced that Avebury is twice as old. For in some places, there were cavities a yard long corroded by time—even on the sides that originally lay on the ground. Had those stones not been exposed to the weather by being set upright, they would have remained smooth. Several other people of good judgment have shared this same opinion.

->CHAPTER<-

<summary>The author argues that Avebury is significantly older than Stonehenge because its stones are unworked, following ancient religious traditions, whereas Stonehenge shows evidence of tools and influence from Solomon's Temple.</summary>
<keywords>Stonehenge, Avebury, Solomon's Temple, Druids, Phoenicians, stone masonry, Ruben Horsall, archaeological dating</keywords>

<meta>The author continues describing excavations at Silbury Hill, moving from the discovery of human remains to the artifacts found within the burial mound.</meta>

# ABURY

<margin>Plate 36</margin>
Six weeks later, I luckily arrived in time to rescue a great curiosity which the workmen had discovered there: an "iron chain," as they called it, which I bought from John Fowler, one of the laborers. It was actually the bridle buried along with this monarch, though it was now a solid mass of rust. I immersed it in <term>limner's drying oil</term> <note>original: "limner's drying oil"; an oil used by painters to preserve and harden surfaces</note> and dried it carefully, keeping it very dry ever since. It is now as clear and intact as when the workmen first took it up. I have provided a sketch of it in Plate 36. There were also deer horns and an iron knife with a bone handle—all excessively rotten—taken up along with it.

Pausanias, in his book *Elis* <note>original: "in Eliacis"; referring to a section of Pausanias's 2nd-century "Description of Greece"</note>, writes how in his own time, a Roman senator won a victory at the Olympic Games. This senator wished to leave a monument of his victory: a bronze statue with an inscription. While digging for the foundation right by the pillar of Oenomaus, they discovered fragments of a shield, a bridle, and an <term>armilla</term> <note>original: "armilla"; a ceremonial bracelet or arm-band</note>, which Pausanias saw himself.

Our bridle belonged to the harness of a British chariot, and it brings to mind the horses and chariots of Egypt mentioned in the earliest historical records. The Tyrian Hercules—who, I suspect, might have brought the first Eastern colony here—was a king in Egypt. In the Bible, when Joseph was prime minister there, we find chariots frequently mentioned for both civilian and military use. In the time of Joshua (17:16, 18), the Canaanites, the <term>Rephaim</term> <note>original: "Rephaim"; an ancient race of giants mentioned in the Hebrew Bible</note> or giants (*Titans*), and the Perizzites all possessed them. So did the Philistines. Our ancestors, the Britons, coming from both Egypt and Canaan, brought the use of chariots here with them. Chariots remained, in a sense, unique and characteristic of our island until the time the Romans settled it. Indeed, it was fashionable for the Romans in Rome, at the height of their luxury, to own British chariots, just as we today use <term>berlins</term> and <term>landaus</term> <note>original: "berlins, landaus"; types of fashionable four-wheeled carriages used in the 1700s</note>.

->**Stop the British chariot with its engraved yokes.**<-
<note>original Latin: "Esseda cælatis ſiſte Britanna jugis." From the Roman poet Propertius.</note>

<margin>Plate 27</margin>
Philostratus, in his *Lives of the Sophists* (Book 25, *Polemon*), remarks on the enameling and ornamentation of Phrygian and Celtic bridles, noting they were very curiously crafted. Ours is perfectly plain and simple—an argument for its great antiquity. *Silbury* is the name given to the hill by our Saxon ancestors, meaning the "great" or "marvellous" hill. Similarly, *Silchester* (the *Vindoma* of the Romans) means the "great city" or "Great Chester."

The name itself cannot help us identify the monarch buried there. However, when I consider that this hill stands at the source of the River Kennet (*Cunetio*), still called "Cunnet" by the local people, and that the name *Cunedha* is very famous among the most ancient Britons—who speak much of a great king by that name—it tempts one to guess that this is the very man. This theory gains some strength from what my old friend Mr. Baxter writes about *Cunetio* (or Marlborough), the first town the river passes. He thinks it took its name from a famous king, Cunedha, who lived at Marlborough, called *Kunyd Kynūidion*, which we may translate as "Cunedha of Marlborough." This name is mentioned in ancient British genealogies before the grandfather of King Arthur, though we can hardly imagine their genealogies could truly reach back to the original founder we are considering. However, Cyngetorix, a British king who fought Julius Caesar, and Cunobelin <note>The historical figure often associated with Shakespeare's "Cymbeline"</note>, king of the island in the time of Augustus, may be descendants of this man; at least their names are related. In Caesar's *Gallic War* (Book 7), *Conetodunus*, a Gaulish prince, bears the same name.

We may also remember that Merlin the magician, who is said to have built Stonehenge by his magic, is claimed to have been buried at Marlborough. Mr. Camden records this from Alexander Necham. Undoubtedly,

## ABURY

<margin>Table XXXVI</margin>
...from which the whole barrow <note>A barrow is an ancient burial mound.</note> is made. Six weeks after, I luckily arrived to rescue a great curiosity which they excavated there: an iron chain, as they called it, which I bought from John Fowler, one of the workmen. It was the bridle buried along with this monarch, though it was only a solid mass of rust. I immersed it in a painter's <note>original: "limner's"</note> drying oil and dried it carefully, keeping it very dry ever since. It is now as clear and intact as when the workmen first dug it up. I have provided a sketch of it in Table XXXVI. There were also deer horns and an iron knife with a bone handle—all excessively rotten—found along with it.

Pausanias, in his *Description of Greece* (specifically the section on Elis), <note>original: "Paufanias, in Eliacis"</note> writes how in his time, a Roman senator won at the Olympic Games. He wished to leave a monument of his victory, which was a bronze statue with an inscription. While digging for the foundation just by the pillar of Oenomaus, they excavated fragments of a shield, a bridle, and a bracelet <note>original: "armilla"</note> which he saw.

Our bridle belonged to the harness of a British chariot, which brings to mind the horses and chariots of Egypt mentioned in the earliest days. The Tyrian Hercules, who I suppose might have brought the first Eastern colony here, was a king in Egypt. In scripture, when Joseph was prime minister there, we find chariots frequently mentioned for both civil and military use. In Joshua’s time (Joshua 17:16, 18), the Canaanites, Rephaim (or giants, the Titans), and Perizzites had them. So did the Philistines. Our ancestors, the Britons, coming from both Egypt and Canaan, brought the use of chariots here; they remained, in a sense, unique and specific to our island until the time the Romans settled it. It was even fashionable for the Romans in Rome, at the height of their luxury, to own British chariots, just as we now have Berlins or Landaus <note>Types of fashionable horse-drawn carriages used in the 1700s.</note> and the like.

Philostratus, in his *Lives of the Sophists* (Book 25, "Polemon"), remarks on the enameling and ornamentation of Phrygian and Celtic bridles, describing them as being very curiously crafted. Ours is perfectly plain and rustic—an argument for its great antiquity.

<margin>Table XXXVII</margin>
*Silbury* is the name given to the hill by our Saxon ancestors, meaning the "great" or "marvellous" hill. Similarly, *Silchester* (the *Vindoma* of the Romans) means the "Great Chester." This name does not help us identify the monarch buried there. However, when I consider this hill standing at the source of the River Kennet (*Cunetio*), still called the "Cunnet" by the local people, and note that among the most ancient Britons the name *Cunedha* is very famous—and that they speak much of a great king of this name—it tempts me to conjecture that this is the very man.

This conjecture is strengthened by what my old friend Mr. Baxter writes about *Cunetio*, or Marlborough, which the river first reaches. He thinks it took its name from a famous king, Cunedha, who lived at Marlborough and was called *Kynyđ Kynuiđion*, which we may translate as "Cunedha of Marlborough." This name is mentioned in ancient British genealogies before the grandfather of King Arthur, though we can scarcely imagine their genealogies could truly reach back to the original founder we are considering. <summary>The author describes finding an ancient iron bridle in Silbury Hill and argues that the mound is the tomb of a great British king, possibly named Cunedha, linking British chariot culture to ancient Egypt and Canaan.</summary>

<keywords>Silbury Hill, Avebury, archaeology, ancient chariots, Cunedha, Marlborough, Merlin, British history, Tyrian Hercules, Pausanias</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the antiquity of Stonehenge and Avebury (Abury), specifically focusing on the construction and historical context of Silbury Hill.</meta>

Stonehenge, and even more so *Avebury* <note>original: "Abury"</note>, are incomparably older than the time of *Merlin* <note>Merlin is the legendary wizard associated with King Arthur; the author is arguing these sites are prehistoric, not medieval.</note>. However, the oldest reports we can expect to have of these matters must come from the *Britons*, the earliest inhabitants remaining. It is natural for them to attach ancient traditions—stretching far beyond their actual knowledge—to the last famous people they have any record of. Therefore, we may reasonably judge that some truths are generally hidden within these old reports. It is likely our King *Cunedda* <note>original: "Kunedha"</note> lived at *Marlborough*, was buried in *Silbury Hill*, and was the founder of *Avebury*. Additionally, the Archdruid—who was the designer and director of the stupendous work at *Avebury* alongside the king—was buried at *Marlborough*. This is because *Marlborough* is within sight of that part of the temple called the *Hakpen*, or the "snake's head," located on *Overton Hill*.

Strabo writes in Book 12 that there is a *burial mound* <note>original Latin: "tumulus"</note> of King *Marsyas* where he was buried, at the head of the River *Marsyas*. This seems to be an exact parallel to our case, where the river preserves the name of the king to this day. *Pausanias* writes in his description of *Boeotia* <note>original: "Pausanias Bæot."</note> that the tomb of *Asphodicus* is at the spring-head of the River *Oedipodias*. Furthermore, *Tiresias's* sepulchre is by the *Telphussa* fountain. There are many more examples like these.

The person who planned the creation of this vast mound of earth, *Silbury Hill*, had a great mind as well as capable hands; he chose his ground well and planned carefully how to execute his purpose. He settled upon the foot of the chalk hill, by the spring of the River *Kennet*, positioned exactly on the North-South <note>original: "meridian"</note> line of *Avebury*. The bottom of the hill consists of natural earth. Beyond the edge of its base, they dug the earth of the original hill away down to the level of the adjacent meadow to provide materials for the artificial part of the mound, leaving a sort of isthmus, or neck of original land. To further detach this artificial part from the natural landscape, they dug a deep trench on the land-side in the middle of the isthmus, but left two "bridges," or passages, leading up to the hill. By this means, the climb for the multitude of workers employed was made easier, as the natural hill served as a ramp for them.

<margin>Plate XXVIII.</margin>
The diameter of *Silbury Hill* at the top is 105 feet, which is the same as the diameter of *Stonehenge*. At the bottom, it is somewhat more than 500 feet—specifically 300 cubits <note>The author uses a "Druid's cubit" of about 20.8 inches for his measurements.</note>—as is its exact vertical height. Those who have seen the vast area of *Stonehenge* will marvel that such a space could be raised 170 feet high, with a base sufficient to support it. Those who consider the geometry of this burial mound <note>original: "barrow"</note>, as I have drawn it in Plate 28, will be equally pleased with its natural and balanced proportions. However, without actually seeing it, one can scarcely have a full idea of its scale. Its solid volume amounts to 13,558,809 cubic feet. Some people have estimated it would cost £20,000 to build such a hill today.

<margin>Plate VIII.</margin>
Some elderly people remember King *Charles II*, the Duke of *York*, and the Duke of *Monmouth* riding their horses up the hill. The *Roman* road, the *Bath Road* <note>original Latin: "via Badonica"</note>, coming from *Overton Hill* toward *Runway Hill*, should have passed directly through *Silbury Hill*. Consequently, the Romans curved the road a little to the south to avoid it, and it runs close by the neck of the hill, then through the fields of *Beckhampton* <note>original: "Bekamton"</note>. This proves that *Silbury Hill* was older than the *Roman* road. Recently, they have fenced off the *Roman road* (which the locals call the "French Way") in the plowed fields of *Beckhampton*; but you can still see the continuation of it when it reaches the heath, as shown in Plate 9.

<margin>Plate IX.</margin>
It seems a simple matter to identify the time of year when this great prince died—the one who is buried here—which was about the beginning of our present...

## DESCRIBED.

...and near one another. The single barrow next to it, toward the "snake's head" temple <note>The Sanctuary on Overton Hill, which formed the head of Stukeley's hypothesized serpent layout</note>, is large and beautifully shaped, with a ditch around it at a distance, which gives it a bell-like <note>original: "campanule"</note> form.

Mr. Bray of Monkton opened a barrow, among many others, at Avebury <note>original: "Abury"</note>. There was a great stone laid at the top, just under the surface. When it was taken up, they found a body laid in a stone coffin formed by several stones. He says that in another barrow, they found a body with a flat gold ring, which was sold for 30 shillings, and a piece of brass about the size of a pint mug, along with iron spearheads.

A man from Amesbury <note>original: "Ambresbury"</note>, who had lived here, told me of a brass spearhead dug up in a barrow between Monkton and Avebury, found near a body. He also mentioned that under some stones in a barrow south of Silbury, they found a piece of gold (I suppose the covering of a button or something similar, like the one I dug up at Stonehenge) and many sharp pieces of iron.

Mr. Aubrey <note>John Aubrey (1626–1697), the pioneering English antiquarian</note> speaks of a barrow opened in Kennet parish in the year <note>original: "anno"</note> 1643. Two stones eleven feet long were laid side by side with a corpse between them, accompanied by a sword and knife. Another similar stone was laid over the top of it all.

There is a very fine hill north of Avebury, of a round form with a gentle ascent all around; <margin>Plate XVIII</margin> it is called Windmill Hill. The turf is as soft as velvet. It is encompassed by a circular trench that is exceedingly old. Fifteen barrows of a most ancient shape are located there. Many barrows of various shapes are on the top. I opened a small one—very old, flat, and round—and found an entire urn turned upside down in a hole cut into the solid chalk. The bones were very decayed. I have provided a drawing of the urn in Plate XXXVI. <margin>Plate XXXVI</margin> It was red on the outside and black on the inside, 14 inches high and 9 inches in diameter at the opening. It was slightly decorated <note>original: "wrought"</note> both inside and out, as well as on the bottom, which was facing upward.

South of Avebury town is a hill, located between the town and Silbury, also called Windmill Hill <note>Likely referring to Waden Hill</note>. It lies between our two avenues and blocks the view from one to the other. This hill too is crowned with barrows of different sorts and sizes. The Bath Road <note>original: "Via Badonica"</note> runs along its southern edge, going from Overton Hill to Silbury. I noticed a barrow there of the kind I call "Druid" barrows. <margin>Plate VIII</margin> Because it was too near the track of the Roman road, the road goes over part of it. Part of the barrow is filled in, and they have dug away the mound <note>original: "tump"</note> in the middle where the urn lay. This is further proof that these barrows date from a time after our Celtic works here <note>Stukeley is arguing that the prehistoric barrows are older than the Roman roads</note>. This hill is also called Weedon Hill, perhaps named after the Roman way.

At Winterbourne Bassett, a little north of Avebury, in a field northwest of the church on elevated ground, is a double circle of concentric stones sixty cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length; Stukeley used a "Druid's cubit" of about 20.8 inches</note> in diameter. The two circles are close to each other, so that one may walk between them. Many of the stones have recently been carried away. To the west of it stands a single, broad, flat, high stone by itself. About the same distance north of the circle, in a plowed field, is a barrow surrounded by—or rather composed of—large stones. I believe this double circle served as a "family chapel," as we might call it, for an Archdruid living nearby, while Avebury served as his cathedral.

There are also some pear-shaped <note>original: "pyriform"</note> barrows around Avebury, which are longish but broad at one end. Some are composed of earth thrown into a <term>burial mound</term> <note>original: "tumulus"</note>. Of this sort, there is a very long one in the valley from Beckhampton to Roundway Hill <note>original: "Runway-hill"</note>. Another, set with stones, was among the furze bushes south of Silbury, but Farmer Green carried the stones away. Others are made of stones set upright in that shape. Of the latter type, there is a very large one in Monkton Fields, with about twenty stones remaining on one side.

He argues that these structures predate the Roman occupation, citing instances where Roman roads cut through existing barrows.</summary>

### AVEBURY <note>original: "Abury"</note>

It is directly north of Avebury town. There is another such mound south of Silbury Hill. Another is pear-shaped <note>original: "pyriform"</note>, made only of earth, under Roundway Hill <note>original: "Runway-hill"</note>. Another sits on the hill southwest from Beckhampton <note>original: "Bekamton"</note>, cut through by some later boundary ditch <note>original: "division dike"</note>.

I believe these long barrows belong to "archdruids." There are only a few left around Avebury, and only two at Stonehenge. This scarcity <note>original: "paucity"</note> seems to confirm the notion. One very large barrow at East Kennet points toward Avebury, but with its smaller end; it is no less than 200 cubits long, which is 350 feet—a massive body of earth. Another not far off points to the "snake's head" temple <note>The Sanctuary on Overton Hill</note>, standing at a right angle to the first.

By Horslip Gap is another significant long barrow of large size, length, and height. It faces <note>original: "regards"</note> the snake's head temple, though it is not in sight from there.

Near the Beckhampton Cove <note>A group of massive standing stones</note> is another, a vast body of earth as thick as the rampart <note>original: "vallum"</note> of Avebury, which points to the cove nearby; this suggests that the cove served as a chapel. There is another large round barrow near it.

In Monkton, west of the town, is a large and flat long barrow set round 
<margin>Plate 30 <note>original: Tab. XXX</note></margin>
with stones, which I have depicted in Plate 30. It is exactly 120 cubits long and 30 cubits broad at the widest end. It stands due east and west, with the widest end facing eastward. Its width is one-fourth of its length—a most magnificent sepulcher called Milbarrow.

But even this is greatly exceeded by the south long barrow near Silbury Hill, located south of it and upon the bank of the River Kennet. It stands east and west, pointing toward the "dragon's head" <note>The Sanctuary</note> on Overton Hill. It is a very laborious heap <note>original: "operose congeries"</note> of huge stones upon the east end and upon part of its back or ridge, piled one upon another with no little labor. This was undoubtedly done to form a sufficient chamber for the remains of the person buried there, so they would not be easily disturbed. The whole burial mound <note>original: "tumulus"</note> is an excessively large mound of earth 180 cubits long, ridged up like a house. We must conclude that the people who made these durable mausoleums <note>original: "mansolea"</note> had a very strong hope in the resurrection of their bodies as well as their souls, given how they provided against their being disturbed.

Upon the heath south of Silbury Hill was a very large oblong work, like a long barrow, made only of stones pitched in the ground without a mound <note>original: "no tumulus"</note>. The Mr. Smith mentioned before told me his cousin took the stones away fourteen years ago to use as boundary stones <note>original: "mere stones"</note>. I take it to have been an archdruid's grave—humble, yet magnificent, being 350 feet or 200 cubits long.

Pausanias, in his *Description of Greece: Elis*, Book 2 <note>original: "Eliac. II"</note>, writes that upon the bank of the river Cladeus is the burial mound of Oenomaus, made of earth and surrounded by stones. Again, in his book on *Arcadia* <note>original: "Arcadic."</note>, he says that at Pergamus is the monument of Auge, being a barrow of earth surrounded by a circle of stones. In the same book on *Arcadia* (Book 8), he says he carefully studied the burial mound <note>original: "tumulus"</note> of Aepitus because Homer mentions it; he admired it, for he had seen none finer. It was made of earth, was not very large, and was surrounded by a circle of stones. Thus does a genius naturally admire works of antiquity! It seems as if such interest precedes his own existence, as if he had lived in those times long before. He writes again in his book on *Boeotia* <note>original: "Bæot."</note> that at the burial mound of Amphion there are many rough stones which they report were the stones he drew together with his harp. Likewise, there are three rough stones near the tomb of Melanippus, and the antiquarians say Tydeus was buried there.

To go much further back in time, and equal to those we have been describing: Genesis 35:20 says, "Jacob set a pillar upon Rachel's grave."

# ABURY

It is directly north of Avebury <note>original: Abury</note> town. Another such one is south of Silbury Hill. Another is pear-shaped <note>original: pyriform</note>, made only of earth, located under Runway Hill. Another is on the hill southwest from Beckhampton <note>original: Bekamton</note>, cut through by some later boundary ditch <note>original: division dike</note>.

The long barrows are what I call those of the Archdruids. There are only a few left around Avebury, and only two at Stonehenge. This scarcity <note>original: paucity</note> seems to confirm the idea. One very large one at East Kennet points toward Avebury, though with its smaller end; it is no less than 200 cubits in length—which is 350 feet—a massive body of earth. Another nearby points toward the "snake’s head" temple, standing at a right angle to the first one.

Near Horslip-gap is another significant long barrow of large size, length, and height; it faces the "snake’s head" temple, though it is not within sight from there.

Near the Beckhampton Cove is another vast mound of earth, as thick as the rampart <note>original: vallum; the outer bank of the henge</note> of Avebury, and it points toward the nearby cove; this suggests that the cove served as a chapel. There is another large round barrow near it.

In Monkton, west of the town, is a large and flat long barrow surrounded by stones, which I have illustrated in Plate 30. It is exactly 120 cubits long and 30 cubits wide at its broadest end. It is aligned due east and west, with the widest end toward the east. Its width is one-fourth of its length; it is a most magnificent sepulcher called **Milbarrow**.

But even this is greatly exceeded by the "South Long Barrow" near Silbury Hill, located to its south on the bank of the River Kennet. It lies east and west, pointing toward the "dragon’s head" on Overton Hill. A very elaborate <note>original: operose; involving great labor</note> pile of huge stones sits upon the east end and upon part of its ridge, piled one upon another with great effort. This was undoubtedly done to create a sufficient chamber for the remains of the person buried there, so they would not be easily disturbed. The entire burial mound <note>original: tumulus</note> is an exceptionally large mound of earth, 180 cubits long, ridged up like the roof of a house. We must conclude that the people who built these durable mausoleums <note>original: mausolea</note> had a very strong hope in the resurrection of their bodies as well as their souls, since they provided so thoroughly against their being disturbed.

On the heath south of Silbury Hill, there was once a very large oblong structure, like a long barrow, made only of stones set in the ground without an earthen mound <note>original: no tumulus</note>. The previously mentioned Mr. Smith told me that his cousin removed the stones fourteen years ago to use as boundary stones <note>original: mere stones</note>. I believe it was an Archdruid’s tomb—humble, yet magnificent—being 350 feet or 200 cubits long.

Pausanias, in his *Description of Greece: Elis* <note>original: Eliac. II</note>, writes that on the bank of the River Cladeus is the burial mound of Oenomaus, made of earth and surrounded by stones. Again, in his book on *Arcadia* <note>original: Arcadic.</note>, he says that at Pergamum is the monument of Auge, which is a mound of earth encircled by a ring of stones. In the same book on *Arcadia* (Book 8), he says he carefully studied the burial mound <note>original: tumulus</note> of Aepitus because Homer mentions it. He admired it, having seen nothing finer. It was made of earth, was not very large, and was surrounded by a circle of stones. In this way, a person of genius naturally admires works of antiquity! By doing so, he seems to extend his own existence into the past, as if he had lived in those ancient times. He writes again in his book on *Boeotia* <note>original: Bæot.</note> that at the burial mound of Amphion there are many rough stones, which they say were the stones he drew together with the music of his harp. Likewise, there are three rough stones near the tomb of Melanippus, and historians say that Tydeus was buried there.

To go much further back in time, to an era equal to those we have been describing: *Genesis 35:20. Jacob set a pillar upon Rachel’s grave.*

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>AVEBURY</header>

...probably gave the name of "Old Chapel" to the place; the barrow likewise was once surrounded by large stones.

In the second stone structure, one stone lies flat on the ground along the middle line of the barrow. On each side, a flat stone stands upright, and two more flat stones stand upright at right angles, acting like wings to them. I suspect other stones were piled on top of these to form a <term>kist-vaen</term> <note>original: "kist-vaen"; a Celtic word for a stone-lined burial chamber or "stone chest"</note>. The body of the deceased probably lies here. The stones are generally very large, about ten feet long.

I believe the entire site was the palace and burial place of an arch-druid, as well as his tribunal or seat of justice. It is positioned precisely enough between the southeast and northwest. The learned Mr. Rowland, who wrote the history of the Isle of Mona <note>the Roman name for the Isle of Anglesey, a historical stronghold of the Druids</note>, describes similar structures in that location and calls them houses of the Druids.

This site stands near a large cavity called Balmore Pond, which seems to be related to this structure. It is a pear-shaped <note>original: "pyriform"</note> hollow lined with stones on the inside. It aligns exactly with the entrance of the "Old Chapel," and the local people report that there is a vault beneath it. One might be tempted to think it was a prison and that the pond served as a place of execution, as it is shaped like a theater. Alternatively, it might have been a place for sports and spectacles. It is 150 cubits <note>An ancient unit of measurement; Stukeley's "Druid's cubit" was approximately 20.8 inches</note> wide and 180 long, shaped like an Amazonian shield.

In a valley between here and Rockley, there are nine round barrows of various sizes. And on all the highest ground in the area, there is an infinite number of immense stones—known as sarsens or "grey weathers"—some as large as those at Avebury and lying as thick as fallen leaves in autumn. Some are on the very surface of the ground, some are half-buried, and many deep holes are visible where stones have been removed.

If we descend Hackpen Hill westward from here toward Winterbourne Bassett, there is another "Druid's house" on the slope of the hill, also called "Old Chapel." It is a square with two small ditches; in the middle is a wide, rectangular bank. In front of it is a kind of courtyard, nearly as large as the other site. Local people say they have found a lot of old iron and pewter nearby. It seems to have once been surrounded by stones.

<margin>Table 19</margin> There is another of these sites in a lovely circular hollow under Hackpen Hill on the west side, overlooking the Kennet Avenue. It is exactly 180 cubits square. It sits on a northern slope, presumably for coolness. The entrance is in the middle of the lower side. Toward the upper side is another, smaller rectangle, which we would call a <term>praetorium</term> <note>original: "prætorium"; the headquarters or general's tent in a Roman camp</note> in a Roman camp. This section had a separate entrance to the south. It is aligned exactly north and south.

In the town of Beckhampton, near the end of the Beckhampton Avenue (or "the snake's tail"), is another such place called "Old Chapel" or "Chapel Field." It is full of large stones, many of which are buried underground. Richard Fowler—that great destroyer of monuments <note>original: "depopulator"</note>—told me he demolished one stone that stood near the pasture hedge. Nearby, a large stone supposedly lies over the mouth of an old well; however, no one remembers it ever being open, and they only know of it through tradition. This field still belongs to the church.

<margin>Table 11</margin> There is another very attractive site of this kind (for all I know) between the Wansdyke and the <term>Via Badonica</term> <note>original: "Via Badonica"; the Roman road leading to Bath</note>, running up Runway Hill. It is a charming, pleasant hollow—a rectangle containing another smaller one, like a headquarters <note>original: "prætorium"</note> inside. In the <term>vallum</term> <note>an earthwork rampart or bank</note> are many gaps at equal intervals. You can see a large part of it in Plate 11, where it is called the "model of a camp." It would be a waste of our time to be overly detailed in describing or investigating these matters...

# AVEBURY

...probably gave the name of the **Old Chapel** to the place; the burial mound <term>barrow</term> likewise has been set entirely around with great stones.

In the second stone-work, one stone lies flat on the ground along the middle line of the burial mound. On each side, a flat stone stands upright, and two flat stones stand upright at right angles, acting as "wings" to them. Upon them, I suppose other stones were piled to form a stone-built chest <note>original: "kist-vaen"; a tomb made of stone slabs</note>. Here probably lies the body of the person interred. The stones are generally very large, about ten feet long.

I take the whole structure to have been the palace and burial place of an arch-druid <note>a high-ranking priest of the ancient Celts</note>, as well as his tribunal or seat of justice. It is positioned accurately enough southeast and northwest. The learned Mr. Rowland, who wrote the history of the **Isle of Mona** <note>Anglesey</note>, describes structures just like this in that place and calls them houses of the **Druids**.

This place stands near a great cavity called **Balmore-pond**, which seems to have some connection to this work. It is a pear-shaped hollow <note>original: "pyriform concavity"</note> set with stones on the inside. It aligns exactly with the **Old Chapel entrance**, and the local people report that there is a vault under it. One would be tempted to think it was a prison and that the pond was the place of executions, being shaped like a theater. Otherwise, it might be a place for sports and spectacles. It is 150 cubits wide and 180 long, shaped like an **Amazonian shield** <note>a crescent-shaped shield used in classical art</note>.

In a valley between here and **Rockley**, there are nine round burial mounds of different sizes. Upon all the highest ground thereabouts, there is an infinite quantity of immense stones, known as **sarsens** or **gray weathers** <note>large sandstone blocks found on the Marlborough Downs</note>. Some are as large in dimension as any at **Avebury** and lie as thick as leaves in autumn. Some are on the very surface of the ground, some half-sunk in, and many deep holes are visible where stones have been removed.

If we descend **Hackpen Hill** westward from here toward **Winterbourne Bassett**, upon the slope of the **Hackpen**, is another **Druid’s house**. In the middle is a broad, oblong, square bank. Before it is a sort of court, nearly as big as the other. Near it, they say, they have found much old iron and pewter. It seems to have been set around with stones.

<margin>TABLE XIX.</margin> There is another of these places in a delightful circular hollow under **Hackpen Hill**, on the west side, hanging over the **Kennet Avenue**. It is exactly 180 cubits square. It lies on a northern slope, for coolness, as one might judge. The entrance is in the middle of the lowest side. However, toward the upper side is another smaller oblong square, what we would call a headquarters <note>original: "prætorium"; the commander's tent or area in a Roman camp</note> in a **Roman camp**. Near it, a great stone lies upon the mouth of an old well, so they say; but they never remember it being open and only speak of it by tradition. This field still belongs to the church.

...about which it is hardly possible to reach any certainty today. The pleasure arising from them comes from being on the spot and walking upon the pleasant, soft grass, which is crowded with these antiquities; here, health for the body and amusement for the mind are effectively combined.

<margin>PLATE XXXVII.</margin>
In Monkton Fields, directly northeast of Avebury, is a monument of four stones, which is probably a <term>kistvaen</term> <note>original: "kist-vaen"; a stone-lined burial chamber from the Neolithic or Bronze Age</note>. I have provided an illustration of it in Plate 37. These seem to be what Mr. Edward Llwyd calls <term>cromlechs</term> <note>original: "Krom-lechon"; a Welsh term for "bowing stones," typically referring to megalithic tombs</note>. I believe it was a funeral monument set upon a burial mound, though it is now mostly plowed up, and the great covering stone has been displaced.

<margin>PLATES XXXII. XXXIII. XXXIV. XXXV.</margin>
Plates 32, 33, and 34 are views of another significant work of this kind in Clatford Bottom between Avebury and Marlborough, which requires no further description.

Plate 35 shows two ancient British urns found at Sunbury by the Thames, which were shown at the Society of Antiquaries some years ago. It also shows the inscription on the monument of Chyndonax, an Archdruid among the Gauls, of which a lengthy account was published in French. Father Montfaucon questions the authenticity of it, but I think his objections are insignificant.

<margin>PLATE XXXVI.</margin>
In Plate 36, I have etched the piece of the "king's bridle" found in Silbury Hill—the founder of Avebury—which is in my possession. Underneath is the British urn that I dug up in a burial mound on Windmill Hill, north of Avebury. This plate is dedicated to the memory of Sir Robert Halford, Knight, and Charles Tucker, Esq., who were very diligent in preserving these noble antiquities.

I have given the reader as plain and as brief a description of these works around Avebury as I possibly could. We cannot help but make this general reflection upon the whole:

1. This temple, with the things belonging to it, when it was intact, must have been the work of a very great and learned people. Its style, manner, and underlying concept show its extreme antiquity. When we view the ruins of Rome, Greece, Egypt, Syria, Persia, or the like, we readily understand the wisdom and prosperity of the people who created them. We must do the same for these British Druids. These very works justify the high praise given to them by classical authors. If we try to contradict them by citing other reports from the same authors concerning the primitive and uncivilized nature of the ancient Britons, the answer is obvious: they are speaking of different times, or perhaps of different people—later waves of migration from mainland Europe who drove the former inhabitants (who created these works) further north and west.

The works themselves are evidence of the genius of their founders. Learning commonly reaches its height within a short period of time. These works here have a remarkable grandeur of style, a sensible plan, a clear symmetry, and sufficient precision in their execution. They are very extensive in scale, and magnificent and pleasing in their effect. We cannot sufficiently admire the boldness of the imagination involved. When this entire area, which is about four miles square, was entirely sacred ground under the care and custody of the Druids—one of their great centers of learning—everywhere covered in fine grass and an infinite variety of burial mounds, it was a most beautiful scene, and truly like a picture.

When one travels across this ground, an intelligent person will discern many remarkable beauties in the manner and arrangement of the temple. The wise Druids knew the internal meaning and purpose of this great symbol of the fertility of the deity, first exerted in producing the

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that the Druids possessed a sophisticated theology, specifically a belief in a divine Trinity, where the "second person" (the Son or the Divine Mind) was the creator of the physical world.</meta>

<center># AVEBURY</center>

...the second person represented thereby, who with them was the creator of all things. From the supreme being proceeded the divine essences equal to himself; but the son of the supreme formed the material worlds, which is why he was called the *mind*, the creator, and the *wisdom of the father*, both by the Druids and us Christians. And never since the creation has so magnificent an idea been formed in mortal minds as this <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>original: "hieroglyphic"; here referring to a symbolic image or "picture-writing" made out of the landscape</note> here before us made in stone-work. This snake of ours may be nearly three of our common miles in length, accurately laid out with its proportions adapted to nature. Its winding shape is well represented in huge curves running in opposite directions, led over several elevations and depressions of the ground. Two hills, one on each side of the stream running from Avebury to Silbury, hide the view of the avenues from each other. So it is probable that the <term>vulgar</term> <note>original: "vulgar"; the common people or the uneducated public</note> then did not know the true figure of the whole, any more than they do now. But those who approached this place for a religious purpose, and who understood its mystical meaning, must have been extremely moved by it; it is, without doubt, the greatest picture on the face of the earth, naturally exciting in their minds the proper disposition for those religious approaches!

2. I observe that Avebury, even now, lays claim to all its old appendages: the bounds of the parish take in mostly everything the snake reaches and its surroundings, such as Southstreet, West Kennet, and Beckhampton, and part of Winterbourne Bassett and Stanmore south of Winterbourne Bassett (they say it used to be a town), as well as Overton Hill, South Downs, West Downs, and Cherhill, almost to Oldbury Castle.

3. I remark that though the people know nothing of the figure of a snake made by the two avenues, yet a notion has been handed down from ancient times that gives an obscure hint of the thing, and of the <term>prophylactic</term> <note>original: "prophylactic"; protective or warding off evil</note> virtue in this figure of the snake. For they say that in all this tract of ground, which we may call the *sacred field*, there never was a snake seen; and if a snake should be brought here, it would not live. Nevertheless, snakes abound in all the country round, even as far as Clatford, between Marlborough and here, but they never come higher up. Whether this notion is justly founded, I do not know, but it is deeply rooted in the minds of the inhabitants. <term>Pliny</term> <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author who wrote extensively about the Druids in his *Natural History*</note> has a great deal to say about the Druids' fondness for snakes, but it is a little unintelligible, as we find most of what authors have said concerning them. And we must be content at this time to mark out some obscure traces of things that seem relevant to our purpose regarding this affair of theirs, which shall be the subject of the next chapters.

4. When we contemplate the manner and arrangement of our temple, regarding its parts in the circle at Avebury and its position upon the cardinal points, some questions arise in our minds for which we desire a resolution. Concerning this, I believe the following hints will give us some satisfaction. Ever since the world began, those building temples or places of religious assembly have been careful to set them according to the quarters of the heavens. For they considered the world as the general temple or house of God, and believed that all particular temples should have a proper regard for it. The east naturally claims a priority, as it is where the sun and all the planets and stars rise; this, therefore, they accounted as the face and front of the world, or the universal temple. The north then was considered as the right hand and great power of the world, and the south as the left hand or lesser power. For when the sun approaches the northern region, passing over the <term>vernal equinoctial</term> <note>original: "vernal equinoctial"; the spring equinox, around March 21st</note>, he brings plenty and the fullness of his fruit-bearing influence; when he returns to the south, the face of nature...

<meta>catchword: nature</meta>

<meta>The author continues his discussion on the symbolic orientation of ancient temples, specifically focusing on the significance of the North and East in Druidic architecture.</meta>

-># DESCRIBED.<-

...nature languishes in its winter attire. Therefore, they thought the polar region was not only the highest point, but the one of most eminence and influence.

Thus, *Orpheus* wrote: "You who hold the scepter of the pole, venerable for many reasons, the throne of the world in the north."

*Psellus* says, "the all-containing <note>original: *Pandochean*</note> power of the world reigns in the north."

For this reason, *Plutarch* writes, "That *Xenophon* says of the *Egyptians*, they thought that the part where the sun rises was the face of the world; the north was its right hand, and the south (where the *Nile* rises) was its left." This helps us to explain several *Egyptian* antiquities.

But to apply this to our purpose: we cannot help but observe that the whole of the *Abury* temple, or *Mausoleum*, when regarded as a picture, has its upper part to the north and its face (if we may speak this way) toward the east. The serpent moves in that direction. The cove of the northern temple opens that way; the cove of the *Bekamton* avenue opens that way; and the face of the *Stonehenge* temple looks that way. So, the Druids appear to have held the same notions as the other wise men of the ancient East.

This shows the reason why they set their temples fronting the east in all antiquity, and why the coves of our local works look that way. As for the two temples at *Abury*—the northern and southern ones included in the great circle—it seems that the northern one had the preeminence and was the more sacred of the two. Just as the cove was the <term>adytum</term> <note>The innermost sanctuary of a temple, where only priests were allowed.</note> of that temple, the entire northern temple may be esteemed as the <term>adytum</term> of the whole work, with the southern temple serving as the body of it.

We know that *Solomon's* temple consisted of three parts: the <term>adytum</term> (or *Holy of Holies*), the *Holy Place* (or *sanctuary*), and the *porch*. In this way, there is a similarity between it and *Abury*, and also to *Stonehenge*, which has an elliptical <term>adytum</term>, a circular or outer part, and the *area*. Doubtless, the different orders of priests and different religious offices occupied these different parts. If we may offer an opinion, it is natural to think that because the ring-stone is by the southern temple, sacrifices were offered and administered there by the lesser orders of priests around the <term>ambre</term> <note>original: "ambre"; likely referring to a sacred monument or "ambrosial stone."</note> or central pyramid-shaped stone. The highest part of religion was to be performed by the Archdruid and the upper order of priests before the magnificent cove of the northern temple, accompanied by hymns, incense, music, and the like.

5. In my account of *Stonehenge*, I suggested a hunch that the Druids used a compass or magnetic instrument when laying out these works of theirs. I founded a conjecture concerning the time of building that temple by observing the magnetic variation with a <term>theodolite</term> <note>A precision instrument for measuring angles in the horizontal and vertical planes.</note>. As the variation in all the works about *Stonehenge* is between six and seven degrees to the east of north, I found it at *Abury* to be almost precisely ten degrees in the same direction. This necessarily excites one's attention, as there is little reason to suppose it is accidental. The whole work was manifestly designed to be aligned with the cardinal points of the heavens, but they all vary in the same direction by exactly the same amount. It is impossible to account for <margin>TAB. VIII.</margin> this in any way other than that they used a magnetic instrument. This is the reason that the neck of the snake on *Overton Hill* crosses the *Roman* road (which runs east and west) which would otherwise have been the baseline of this work.

Thus, *Kennet* avenue enters the town of *Abury* ten degrees north of the north-west point, even though the north-west point was the Druids' intended goal. The neck of the snake going down from *Overton Hill* points precisely toward *Silbury*, and their intent was for it to be due west, but it is actually ten degrees north of west. The meridian line of the whole work passes from *Silbury Hill* to the...

<meta>This page continues the author's chronological argument for the antiquity of Avebury (Abury), comparing it to Stonehenge and aligning its construction with biblical and ancient historical figures.</meta>

### DESCRIBED.

Dr. Halley <note>Edmond Halley (1656–1742), the English astronomer famous for calculating the orbit of the comet that bears his name.</note> suggests that the entire period is completed in about the span of 700 years. I am fully convinced—by considering the different effects of weathering between *Abury* <note>The author's spelling of Avebury, a Neolithic henge monument in Wiltshire.</note> and *Stonehenge*, the great diversity in the style of the works, and some other considerations—that *Abury* must be more than 700 years older than *Stonehenge*. 

However, if we take two entire revolutions of 700 years (totaling 1,400 years) and add them to 460 BC—the supposed time of the building of *Stonehenge*—it brings us, according to *Ussher's* chronology <note>The biblical timeline created by Archbishop James Ussher (1581–1656), which famously dated the Creation to 4004 BC.</note> (which I consider to be the best), to the year of the death of *Sarah*, *Abraham's* wife. This occurred in the summer of 1859 BC. This was shortly before the time of *Inachus* <note>In Greek mythology, the first king of Argos.</note>.

Based on the best information I can obtain, I judge our *Tyrian Hercules* <note>A mythological figure often identified by antiquarians like Stukeley as a leader of Phoenician explorers.</note> made his expedition into the ocean around the end of *Abraham's* life. It is most likely that *Abury* was the first great temple of *Britain*, built by the first *Phoenician* colony that arrived here. They established it in this place because of the <term>gray-weathers</term> <note>original: "gray-weathers"; local Wiltshire name for sarsen stones, the sandstone blocks used to build Avebury and Stonehenge.</note>, which were so convenient for their purpose.

*Ussher* dates this departure of the *Hyesi* <note>original: "Hyesi"; referring to the Hyksos, the "shepherd kings" who ruled a portion of Lower Egypt.</note>, or royal pastors, out of *Egypt* (which was led by our *Hercules*) to be 34 years after that date. However, my calculations make it somewhat later.

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the patriarchal temple at Avebury, focusing on the symbolic significance of the serpent (Dracontia) in ancient religious architecture and its origins in the early post-flood world.</meta>

### DESCRIBED.

This patriarchal temple, of which Avebury <note>original: "Abury"</note> is one, can be traced down to later times, even while the thing itself and the manner of building it were disused and forgotten.

<margin>Servius on the second *Aeneid*.</margin> Servius, in his commentary on the second book of Virgil's *Aeneid*, writes: "*Anguis* <note>Latin: snake</note> is the proper name for a water-snake; *serpens* <note>Latin: serpent</note> for those on land; and *draco* <note>Latin: dragon/serpent</note> for those belonging to temples." By this last term, he must ultimately be referring to our type of structures, though the author probably did not understand this, as he had no personal acquaintance with our specific kind of ancient works. However, it unavoidably brings to mind the temples of the ancients that were "guarded by dragons," which we so frequently encounter in classical history. We may well presume they mean such temples as this one at Avebury, which are called *Dracontia* <note>Latin: Serpent Temples</note>.

<margin>Maximus of Tyre, *Dissertation 38*.</margin> "The serpent," says Maximus of Tyre in his 38th Dissertation, "was the great symbol of the deity to most nations, and as such was worshiped by the Indians." The temples of old made in the form of a serpent were called, for that reason, *Dracontia*. The universality of this regard for serpents shows the high antiquity of the symbol, and suggests that it dates back to before the Great Flood <note>original: "antediluvian"</note>.

To shed light on this matter, it will first be convenient to discuss a little concerning the nature of the serpent, and why mankind should make it a symbol of divinity. It seems a little strange that such a high regard should be paid to it, considering that our first mother, Eve, was seduced from her innocence by the devil in this form.

The earliest learning in the world consisted chiefly of symbols. The wisdom of the Chaldeans, Phoenicians, Egyptians, and Jews—as well as that of Zoroaster, Sanchoniathon, Pherecydes of Syros, Pythagoras, Socrates, and Plato—indeed, all the wisdom of the ancients that has come down to us, is symbolic. 

<margin>Serranus on Plato’s *Symposium*.</margin> "It was the custom," says Jean de Serres <note>original: "Serranus"</note> in his commentary on Plato’s *Symposium*, "of the ancient philosophers to represent truth through certain symbols and hidden images. It leads us gradually, sweetly, yet most effectively toward the contemplation of the First Being, which is the end of all philosophy and theology." We may add that it was the method of ancient theologians too, from the beginning until the time of our Savior. No one utilized this method more than Christ; in all his sermons and discourses, which were moving, well-crafted, lively, appropriate, and entertaining in the highest degree. Some of them were complete dramas in themselves. In general, we must conclude that symbolism gives a beautiful polish and an amiable face to the truth.

That the Druids studied in this enigmatic and symbolic way is apparent from the very subject we are writing upon; Diogenes Laertius, in his preface, affirms this of them. He ranks them alongside the Magi, the Chaldeans, and the Gymnosophists <note>literally "naked philosophers," referring to ancient Indian ascetics</note>. He provides some of their doctrines and suggests they are even older than the Egyptians—meaning the learned class among the Egyptians. He says, "the Gymnosophists are descended from the Magi, and some affirm the Jews are as well." By this, he means the ancestors of the Jews, specifically Abraham. I believe that the Druids, Chaldeans, Gymnosophists, and Egyptians were all descended from, or rather disciples of, the Magi, who were the first patriarchal priests after the Flood. Sanchoniathon calls Shem (as I understand it) by the name of *Magus*, as the prince of their order. He says the Egyptians veil their doctrines under the figures of beetles, snakes, birds, and other animals. This appears to be the origin of animal worship in Egypt.

<margin>Gale in his *Court of the Gentiles*, Part I, p. 64; again Part II, p. 35.</margin> Thus Theophilus Gale, in his *Court of the Gentiles*, Part I, p. 64 and Part II, p. 35, states: "the ancient mode of expressing things worthy of memory through hieroglyphic forms, notes, and symbols was very common among the ancients, especially in the East, among both poets and philosophers. It was exceedingly proper for that early state of the world, wherein knowledge was so imperfect and unrefined. We need not doubt that this symbolic kind of discourse, or language, had its origin from the divine system <note>original: "œconomy"</note> which God prescribed for his infant church, con—"

<summary>This page discusses the symbolic use of serpents in ancient temple architecture (Dracontia) and argues that the Druids, like other ancient learned groups, used symbols to convey divine truths. The author traces this tradition back to the patriarchal period and the "Magi" following the Great Flood.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the symbolic nature of the serpent in ancient religions, explaining how visual signs were used to convey spiritual truths before the widespread use of alphabets.</meta>

# AVEBURY

“...consisting of many earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> images and tangible <note>original: "sensible"</note> forms, symbols, and types, for illustrating <note>original: "shadowing forth"</note> the highest contemplations and heavenly mysteries. This way of conveying and preserving knowledge is not only helpful to the memory and pleasing to the imagination and judgment, but also very effective for moving the emotions.”

A symbol is an arbitrary, tangible sign of an intellectual idea. I believe that the art of writing was originally nothing more than the creation of symbols, pictures, or marks for the things people wanted to express. In this way, every letter was the picture of an idea. This was the first and "antediluvian" <note>pre-Flood</note> method of writing, used before the alphabet was invented. In my opinion, alphabet writing was an invention that came after the Great Flood. I shall provide my reasons for this on another, more immediate occasion.

Servius, in his commentary on Book 5 of Virgil’s *Aeneid* regarding the phrase "seven huge coils" <note>original Latin: "septem ingens gyros"</note>, writes about the snake encircling the tomb of Anchises. He notes that this symbolic method existed before alphabet writing. I believe the Chinese method of writing is the original pre-Flood one, and perhaps the same can be said of Egyptian hieroglyphics. The Egyptians had the good sense to embrace alphabet writing when it was shared with them, though the Chinese will not. Still, the Egyptians maintained a particular reverence for their former method and dedicated it entirely to sacred uses.

This symbol of the snake and circle—which is the layout <note>original: "picture"</note> of the temple of Avebury <note>original: "Abury"</note>—can be seen on countless Egyptian monuments. It always holds the uppermost, first, and chief position, which shows its high importance. In his 1629 work on the *Arundel Marbles* (p. 132), Mr. Selden says, “this figure in shorthand writing among the Greeks signifies *Daemon* <note>Greek: Δαίμων</note>, the deity.” And Athanasius Kircher, in his third volume, affirms the same regarding the Brahmans <note>original: "Brachmans"</note> of the East Indies.

I cannot admit that this was an Egyptian invention. The Egyptians took this symbol, and hieroglyphic writing in general, from the common ancestors of all mankind. This is sufficiently proven by how universal the symbol is, reaching from China in the east to Britain in the west, and even into America.

Nothing is held in higher regard among the Chinese than the representation of dragons and serpents, as we see in all their pictures and tools—even on the stamps used for their ink. It is the national <note>original: "genial"</note> banner of their empire and represents everything they consider sacred. In Baron Vischer’s elegant book of ancient architecture (Plate 15), there is a picture of a Chinese triumphal arch, many of which exist in the city of Beijing <note>original: "Pekin"</note>. Twice upon a tablet on its front is pictured a circle and two snakes, just as on Egyptian works. They adorn their temples, houses, clothing, and everything else with this figure as a common protection <note>original: "prophylaxis"</note>. I believe it was a sacred, amulet-like character from the very beginning.

It is carved several times on the cornices of the temple at Persepolis, as seen in the works of Sir John Chardin, Cornelis de Bruijn <note>original: "Le Brun"</note>, and Engelbert Kaempfer <note>original: "Kæmfer"</note>. Dragons were the banners of the Parthians, from whom the Romans later adopted them, and our Saxon ancestors took them from the Romans. There is a well-known verse by the satirist Persius:
*Paint two snakes, for the place is sacred.* <note>original Latin: "Pinge duos angues, sacer est locus."</note>

The Druids had no less a veneration for it, as we find from the temple at Avebury and by their fondness for "snake-stone" beads and similar items. Pliny calls these "snakes' eggs" and discusses them at length in relation to our Druids.

Here

<summary>This page argues that the serpent-and-circle symbol is a universal, pre-alphabetic sign for the deity, appearing in cultures from China and Egypt to the Druids of Britain. The author identifies this symbol as the foundational design of the Avebury temple complex.</summary>
