<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that ancient myths about people being transformed into stones or animals are actually allegories for the construction and layout of megalithic temples. He draws a direct parallel between Greek mythology and the local folklore surrounding British stone circles like Rollright and Stanton Drew.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>

<header>DESCRIBED.</header>

We just read that the Sidonian women, the mourners for Melcarthus <note>A Phoenician god often identified with Hercules</note> and his mother, were turned—some into stones, others into birds.

->*Some of the Ismenian women were turned into birds, taking flight on new wings.*<-
<note>original: Pars volucres factæ, sumptis Ismenides alis. This quote is from Ovid’s Metamorphoses.</note>

I would suppose the internal meaning of this story refers to the creation of an <term>alate temple</term> <note>A temple designed in the shape of wings</note>, which we will discuss further in chapter 16.

In his 31st story, Antoninus Liberalis <note>A Greek grammarian who recorded myths of transformations</note> tells a very old tale of the first inhabitants of Italy before the time of Hercules. He mentions a place among the Messapians called the "Sacred Stones," where the nymphs known as the Epimelides had a sanctuary surrounded by trees—trees that were said to have formerly been men. This must be understood in the same way as the previous story.

In this way, we see how the ancient Greeks wrapped everything in fables. Yet every fable has some historical foundation, and we must endeavor to find it by connecting these elements properly, until the hidden truth reveals itself.

The learned Dr. Bogan, in his letter prefixed to *Delphi Phoenicians*—drawing from Aeschylus and others <note>The original text cites "Izet. á.", likely referring to the commentator Tzetzes</note>—shows that the ancients often called men "snakes" in an allegorical way. As for the reports of Cadmus and his wife, or the Sidonian women and others, being turned into snakes, stones, birds, or trees—in the sense we are explaining—it is no different from what we daily see and hear at this time regarding these very Druid temples on our own island. The people who live in Chipping Norton and all the countryside around our first described temple, Rollright <note>original: Rowldrich</note>, constantly affirm and firmly believe that the stones forming this work are a king, his nobles, and his commoners turned into stone. They quote an ancient proverb about that tall stone called the King Stone:

->*If Long Compton you can see,*<-
->*Then King of England shall you be.*<-

And as Mr. Roger Gale once wrote to me from that place: "It is the creed of all that country, and whoever dares to contradict it is looked upon as the most audacious free-thinker."

The very same legend persists at the Druid temple of Stanton Drew in Somerset, which I shall describe in my next volume. This noble monument is popularly called "The Weddings." They say it is a group of people who attended a wedding ceremony and were thus turned to stone. In an orchard near the church is a <term>cove</term> <note>A small shrine-like structure made of three large standing stones</note> consisting of three stones, like those of the northern circle in Avebury <note>original: Abury</note> or the Longstones. They call these "the parson, the bride, and the bridegroom." Other circles are said to be the wedding guests dancing, and a separate group of stones standing a little distance from the rest are called "the fiddlers," or the band of musicians.

Similarly, that vast circle of stones in Cumberland, which was a Druid temple, is called "Long Meg and her Daughters," and is truly believed to have been human beings turned into stone.

Thus, we see an exact consistency between the fables of the ancient Greeks and our own people today. The Greeks found these types of patriarchal temples built by their first heroes and settlers; admiring the vastness of the works, they attached these marvelous stories to them and retain them as firmly as our common folk do now. This is the nature of ancient mythology: by finding the end of the thread, we can unravel it to reveal useful truths.

<summary>The author argues that folklore about people turning into stone—found both in Greek myth and British legends like Rollright and Long Meg—is actually a traditional way of explaining the origins of massive ancient stone circles.</summary>

<meta>This page begins Chapter 15, transitioning from a description of specific ancient serpentine temples and their builders to a metaphysical discussion of the religious philosophy held by the Druids, specifically regarding their understanding of the nature of God.</meta>

## DESCRIBED.

### CHAPTER XV.

*A metaphysical investigation concerning the nature of the Deity, showing how the Druids, through the power of reason, might have reached the knowledge of a divine emanation or "person" originating from the supreme First Cause—whom we call the Son of God—and the necessity of accepting such an emanation. All the philosophers and priests of antiquity held this concept, as we read in the works of Plato and many others.*

I HAVE provided the reader with an account of three distinguished builders of these <term>Dracontia</term> <note>original: "Dracontia"; serpent-shaped temples</note>, or serpentine temples, in the earliest times after the flood and in the eastern parts of the world, as well as described one of those works on our own island. There are many more such builders and buildings, which will be easily discovered by those who are familiar with ancient learning. This figure of the circle and snake upon which they are founded had gained a very sacred status, as it expressed the most eminent and illustrious act of the Deity: the "multiplication of his own nature," as the <term>Zoroastrians</term> <note>followers of the ancient Persian prophet Zoroaster</note> and <term>Platonists</term> <note>followers of the philosophy of Plato</note> say. It served as a symbol of that divine person who was the result of it.

We should not wonder that the Druids had a perception of this great truth when we consider that it was known, as far as was necessary, to all the philosophical and religious sects of antiquity, as shown at length by several learned writers. My opinion is that it was originally communicated to mankind by God himself. It is the highest point of wisdom the human mind can reach to understand something of the nature of the Deity; the studious, pious, and thinking people of the world would not fail to improve this knowledge through reflection and <term>ratiocination</term> <note>the process of logical reasoning</note>.

Although my task is to speak more fully of the religion of the Druids in the next volume, I judge it very relevant to the present subject to anticipate that intention. I wish to show how far they might have advanced toward that knowledge by the force of reason, in order to further explain the works in which they recorded their notions of this sort in the largest characters ever made—monuments remaining to this day, such as those I have been describing. These may lead us to have the same sentiment concerning them as <term>Pere Gauloïs</term> <note>a French scholar of the period</note>, even though he knew nothing of our local antiquities; he wrote "that the Druids worshiped the true God, and that their ideas of religion were truly grand, sublime, and magnificent."

We may therefore very justly say of them that in their serious contemplations in this place concerning the nature of the Deity—which, as <term>Julius Caesar</term> tells us, was one part of their academic inquiries—they would have reasoned in their own minds in the following way:

A contemplative person, viewing and considering the world around him, is enchanted with the harmony and beauty, the fitness of things within it, the uses and connection of all its parts, and the infinite agreement shining throughout the whole. He would have to doubt all his senses to question whether it was composed by a being of infinite power, wisdom, and goodness, whom we call God. But among all the most glorious attributes of divinity, goodness is preeminent. For this beautiful fabric of the world displays through every...
Z

The author argues that the Druids' religious views were sophisticated and aligned with other ancient philosophical traditions like those of Plato and Zoroaster.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 15, which explores the metaphysical philosophy of the Druids. The author argues that because God is essentially good, He could not have remained inactive or solitary for eternity before creation, leading to the logical necessity of divine emanations.</meta>

->ABURY<-

...atom of it, such an amazing scene of the goodness and beneficence of its author. To such contemplative minds, it appears that His infinite power and wisdom were merely like two hands employed by the *goodness* of the Sovereign Architect.

Goodness was the beginning, the middle, and the end of creation. To explain, prove, or illustrate this topic would be an insult to the common understanding of mankind. The sum of what we can know of Him is that He is good—essentially good. We are no more certain of the existence of the First Being than we are that He is good: *the Good*, goodness itself, in its highest form. He is God because He is good, which is the meaning of the word in English and in many other languages. For God Almighty, this is the attribute above all attributes, the perfection of His all-perfect nature. He made and maintains those creatures—which He multiplied to an infinite degree—as the objects of His care and beneficence. These are the great characteristics of supreme love that make Him deservedly adorable.

All possible perfections, both moral and natural, must necessarily be inherent in this first and supreme Being, because they can flow from Him alone. This is, in one comprehensive word, what we call "good." But goodness that is unexercised, unemployed, or uncommunicated is no goodness at all; it implies a contradiction when said of the All-good Being. Therefore, it undeniably follows that there never was a time—and never can be—when God was <term>useless</term> <note>original: "useless"; in this context, the author means "inactive" or "not exercising His divine attributes"</note> and did not share His goodness.

Yet there was a time before creation—before this beautiful fabric of the world was made, before even <term>chaos</term> <note>original: "chaos"; the formless state of matter before the universe was ordered</note> itself, or the production of the raw matter from which the world was formed. And this time must be acknowledged not only regarding the material creation but also regarding the creation of angels and spiritual beings. No matter how many ages or myriads of ages we count for the beginning of creation, it certainly had a start, and there was a time before that beginning. By definition, creation is bringing into being that which did not exist before. There must have been a time before it.

Here, then, occurs the difficulty of filling that infinite gap before creation. Consider the supreme First Being sitting in the center of a universal solitude, surrounded by the abyss of infinite nothingness—a chasm of immense emptiness! What words can describe the greatness of such a <term>solecism</term> <note>original: "solecism"; here meaning a logical inconsistency or an absurdity</note>? What mind does not shrink from the horror of such an absurdity, especially when imagining that this state lasted for infinite ages?

It is useless to claim that a being of all perfections can be happy in Himself, or in the awareness of those perfections, while He does no good for anything; or that He could be happy in the inward thought of possessing all excellence while He does not exercise even a tiny bit of it. This is the picture of a being completely inconsistent with the nature of the All-good. Just as the Druids would easily judge that there must be one, only, self-originating First Being as the source of all things, they would also see the necessity of admitting one or more eternal beings, or "emanations" from that First Being, in a manner quite distinct from creation.

That there was always one eternal, self-existent, and unoriginated being is the very first and most necessary truth that the human mind can possibly obtain through contemplation and <term>ratiocination</term> <note>original: "ratiocination"; the process of logical reasoning</note>. Yet, by considering the matter deeply, they would find it impossible to conceive that there should ever be a time when there was only one being in the universe—the one we call the First and self-originating Being—possessing all possible perfections within Himself while remaining for endless myriads of ages sluggish, inactive, and solitary.

<summary>This page presents a philosophical argument that God's essential nature is goodness, which necessitates constant action and sharing of that goodness. The author posits that the Druids used this logic to conclude that divine "emanations" (spiritual beings or "persons") must have existed eternally alongside the First Being, even before the creation of the material world.</summary>

<keywords>Sovereign Architect, Druids, Metaphysics, Divine Goodness, Creation, Emanation, Ratiocination, Theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that God’s essential goodness necessitates an eternal object for that goodness. Having established that creation itself is not eternal, the author now argues for the "filiation" or begetting of a divine Son as the eternal expression of God's nature, a concept he attributes to the Druids.</meta>

### DESCRIBED.

useless <note>original: "useless"; in this context, the author means "inactive" or "not exercising His divine attributes"</note>. This is a notion so abhorrent to reason, so contrary to the nature of goodness, and so absolutely absurd, that we might as well imagine this great Being to be altogether absent, and that there was no existence at all.

All the philosophers were aware of this, for goodness that is unexercised—that always lies dormant and was never put into action—is no goodness at all. It might as well be supposed absent, and even that there was no God. To imagine that God could be asleep all this while shocks the mind; therefore, the mind casts about for a way to resolve this great paradox.

Now, it cannot be said of any part of creation, or of the whole, that God always did good to any created being or beings; for these are not, and cannot be, equal in time <note>original: "commensurate in time"</note> with His own existence. No matter how far back we count for the beginning of things, there was still a time prior to this beginning. For eternal creation is as much an absurdity as the eternal absence of any being. Where no part is necessary, to affirm that the whole is a necessarily and self-existing being is a mere distortion of reason <note>original: "portent of reason"</note>.

So we see, from every perspective, an absolute necessity of admitting a being or beings coeval <term>coeval</term> <note>meaning existing at the same time or of the same age</note> with the supreme and self-originated Being, distinct from any creation. This being must necessarily flow from the first Being, who is the cause of all existence. For two self-originated beings is as much an absurdity as any of the previous ideas.

But, as it is impossible that the act of creation should be coeval with the first Being, what other act of goodness can there be? For that Being which is essentially good must ever have been actively and actually so. To answer this great question, we must ask as the prophet *Isaiah* does in the person of God, in his last chapter, when summing up the work of his prophetic office:

> “Shall I bring to the birth, and not beget, says
> “*Jehovah*: shall I cause to bring forth, and be myself barren, says your
> “God?”

He is speaking there of the birth of the Son of God in human form; but we may apply it in a more eminent degree to the Son of God in His divine nature, just as the Druids may well be supposed to have done. The highest act of goodness possible, even for the Supreme Being, is the *production* of His like: the act of filiation <term>filiation</term> <note>the process of being a son; the act of begetting</note>, the begetting of His Son. *Proverbs* 8:22:

> “The LORD begat me *from eternity*, before his works of
> “old;” (so it ought to be read); verse 30. “then I was by him, as one brought
> “up with him (*amoun* <note>original Hebrew: *amun*, meaning a master craftsman, ward, or confidant</note> in the original) and I was daily his delight, rejoicing
> “always before him.”

This is the internal divine fecundity <term>fecundity</term> <note>fruitfulness or the ability to produce offspring</note> of the fruitful Cause of all things. Creation is external fecundity. The Druids would naturally apply the term "generation" to this act of producing this person, or divine emanation from the Supreme, which we are obliged to admit, and to affirm him coeval with the Supreme. The difficulty of priority in time between father and son would easily be removed by considering the difference between divine and human generation—the production of necessary versus contingent beings.

If an artist produces an admirable and curious piece of mechanism, he is said to *make* it; if he produces a person or being altogether like himself, he is rightly said to *generate* that person; he begets a son, which is an act of filiation. So we must affirm the same of the Supreme Being generating another being, with whom alone He could communicate His goodness from all eternity and without any beginning; or, in scriptural language, *in whom he always had complacency*. This is what Plato means by saying “love is older than all the gods,” and that the kingdom of love is prior to the kingdom...

<summary>The author argues that God's eternal goodness requires an eternal object, leading to the logical necessity of the "Son of God" existing coevally with the Father. He suggests the Druids shared this metaphysical understanding of divine generation and filiation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical defense of the eternal nature of the Son of God, arguing that if God is eternally good, He must have an eternal object for that goodness—the Logos—rather than a creation that had a beginning in time.</meta>

# ABURY

“kingdom of necessity.” And this Son must be a self-existent, all-perfect being, just as much as the Father, with the only exception being self-origination, which the necessary relationship or economy <term>economy</term> <note>original: "oeconomy"; in theology, this refers to the arrangement or relationship between the persons of the Trinity</note> between them forbids. If He is a Son, He is like Himself; if He is like Himself, He is God; if He is God, an eternity of existence is one necessary part of His divine nature and perfection.

If the Son is of the same substance and nature as the Father, an eternity of being is one part of His nature; therefore, no time can be assigned for this divine generation <note>original: "geniture"</note>, and it must be what we call eternal. Or perhaps we may express it just as well by saying it was "before eternity," or that He is coeval <term>coeval</term> <note>existing at the same time or of the same age</note> with the almighty Father. In this same sense, Proclus, in *On the Patriarchs* <note>original: "Proclus de patriarch."</note>, uses the words "of equal age" and "pre-eternal" <note>original: "æquivoos, præternus"</note>. For though it is impossible that creation—whether of material or immaterial beings—should be of the same age as God, yet, if the Son is of the same nature as the Father (which must be granted), then it is impossible for it to be otherwise than that the Son of God should be coeval with the Father.

If goodness is, as it were, the essence of God, then He can have no happiness except in the exercise of that goodness. We must not say, as many are inclined to do, that He was always and infinitely happy by reflecting upon His own being and infinite perfections in the idea of Himself. This is no exercise of goodness, unless we allow this "idea of Himself" which He produces to be a being outside of Him, or distinct from Himself; and that is granting exactly what we are arguing for. A true and exact idea of Himself is the *Logos* <term>Logos</term> <note>Greek for "Word" or "Reason," referring here to the second person of the Trinity</note> of the ancients, the first-born of the First Cause. And this is the meaning of what the Eastern and all other philosophers assert: “that it was necessary for Unity to unfold itself and multiply; it was necessary for Good to communicate itself. There could be no time before then, for then He would be an imperfect unity, and might as well be termed a cipher <note>a zero or nothing</note>, which of itself can never produce anything.” Agreeable to this doctrine, Philo, in *On Monarchy, Book 2* <note>original: "Philo in II. de monarchiis"</note>, writes, “the *Logos* is the express image of God, and the one by whom the whole world was made.” It would be senseless to think here that he meant only the wisdom of the Supreme—the reason or the skill <term>cunning</term> <note>original: "cunning"; in this period, the word meant knowledge, intelligence, or technical skill rather than deceit</note> of God—as a mere quality rather than a personality.

The difficulty that exists here arises merely from the weakness of our understanding and from our being familiar only with ordinary biological generation. A son of ours is of the same nature as his father. His father was begotten in time, and therefore the son is the same. It is not so in divine generation. But as the Father is from eternity, so is the Son. There is only this difference, or rather distinction: the Father is self-existent and unoriginated; the Son is from the Father.

Further, in this kind of reasoning, we must remove all the imperfections of different sexes, as well as time, which exist in human generations; we must remove all such gross ideas that are incompatible with the most pure and perfect divine nature. All of our reasoning further confirms that the Son exists by necessity. It was necessary for God to be actively good always, and begetting His Son was the greatest act of divine goodness, and necessarily the first. But the word "first" is absurd, betraying the imperfection of our own speech and ideas when we treat these matters; for there could be no "first" where there is no beginning. And the very names of Father and Son are only relative and economical <term>economical</term> <note>referring to the roles or "economy" of the Trinity in relation to one another and to creation</note>; they are useful only so that we may be able to hold some tolerable notion of these things that are so far above our understanding.

But though it be infinitely above our understanding, we still reach far enough to see the necessity of it. And we can no other way cure that immense vacuum <note>original: "vacuo"</note> that...

He cites ancient philosophers like Proclus and Philo to support the idea of the Logos as a distinct divine personality rather than a mere attribute.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that the Druids and other ancient civilizations understood the concept of a Trinity within the Deity through reason and ancient tradition, rather than just through later Christian revelation.</meta>

# DESCRIBED.

...that greatest of all absurdities: the idea that the Supreme Being was idle or useless before creation. The Druids likely arrived at all of this through reasoning <term>ratiocination</term> <note>original: "ratiocination"; the process of logical reasoning</note>. We should have no difficulty accepting this if we simply assume that obscure notions about the nature of the Deity were handed down from the beginning of the world. For instance, in the *Alexandrian Chronicle* <note>original: "Chronicon Alexandrinum"; a 7th-century Byzantine Christian chronicle of the world</note>, the works of John Malalas <note>original: "Malala"; a Byzantine chronicler</note>, and other authors, we read that in the earliest times among the **Egyptians**, there reigned a man from the family of **Mizraim** <note>original: "Misraim"; in the Bible, the son of Ham and the progenitor of the Egyptians</note> named **Sesosiris**. He was a descendant of **Osiris** and was highly respected for his wisdom. He taught that there were three great energies or persons in the Deity, which were nonetheless one. This man was **Lud**, or **Thoth**, the son of **Mizraim** or **Osiris**; for this reason, when idolatry began, he was consecrated under the name **Hermes**, representing one of those divine energies which we call the Holy Spirit.

This is a brief and simple account of the knowledge the ancients had concerning the nature of the Deity. It was deduced through reason by contemplative minds and was certainly known to the whole world from the beginning, rightly called a mystery. While our reason is strong enough to see the necessity of accepting this doctrine, it is not strong enough to understand the "how" of it. The manner of an "eternal generation" <note>the theological concept of the Son being eternally "begotten" by the Father</note> can only be understood by the Deity itself.

The Druids would have taken this line of reasoning a step further. Although we must admit the necessity of an eternal generation, the person generated—who is all-perfect God—does not multiply the Deity itself, even though He is a person distinct from His Father. This is because addition or subtraction implies imperfection, which cannot be attributed to the nature of God. Therefore, they would say that although the Father and the Son are different divine personalities, they cannot be called two Gods or two Godheads. To do so would be to divide <term>discerping</term> <note>original: "discerping"; tearing apart or dividing into parts</note> the Deity or Godhead, which is both absurd and wicked.

It is well known that mankind used to reason in this way, so I do not need to formally prove it. It is beyond dispute; many authors have already proven it thoroughly. Since such ideas existed in the world, our Druids—who were famous for their theological studies—would have cultivated them in the manner I have described, using only the strength of natural reason. I cannot say whether they arrived at this through prior reasoning <note>original: "ex priori"</note>, but the massive <term>operose</term> <note>original: "operoſe"; involving great labor or effort</note> work of **Avebury** <note>original: "Abury"</note> before us is the strongest argument that they did. Nothing else could have driven men to create such a physical mark—such a "picture" of their own concepts—as this stupendous production of labor and art.

Just as our Western philosophers [the Druids] created a massive "picture" of their idea in a work three miles long, partially hidden by various hills, the Eastern sages—who were more willing to write—chose to express it in obscure and enigmatic ways. **Pythagoras**, for instance, claimed that the origin of all things came from unity and an infinite duality (as noted in Plutarch’s *On the Opinions of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Plutarc. de plac. philos."</note>). **Plato** identifies three divine authors of all things: the first or supreme being he calls the "King" or "the Good." Beside him, he names the "Cause," who descends from the first; and between them, he names the "Leader" <note>original: "dux"</note> or, at other times, the "Mind." In exactly the same way, the **Egyptians** called them "Father," "Mind," and "Power." Therefore, in his sixth letter, writing to **Hermias** and his friends to instruct them on taking a solemn oath, **Plato** directs it to be made before "God, the leader or prince of all things that are and shall be, and before the Lord, the Father of that leader and cause." All of these, he says, we...

<summary>This page argues that ancient civilizations, specifically the Druids and Egyptians, possessed an early understanding of the Trinity through logic and ancient tradition. The author cites the massive earthworks at Avebury as physical proof of these complex theological beliefs held by the Druids.</summary>
<keywords>Druids, Trinity, Avebury, Thoth, Hermes, Plato, Pythagoras, Mizraim, Osiris, natural religion, theological archaeology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that the Druids and other ancient civilizations understood the concept of a Trinity within the Deity through reason and ancient tradition, rather than just through later Christian revelation.</meta>

-># A B U R Y<-

“We shall know clearly, if we philosophize correctly, as far as the powers of good men will carry us.” And in *Timaeus* <note>original: "Timæus"; one of Plato's dialogues, which speculates on the nature of the physical world and human beings</note> he makes **MIND** to be the son of **GOOD**, and to be the more immediate architect of the world. And in *Epinomis* <note>original: "Epinomis"; a dialogue traditionally attributed to Plato, serving as an appendix to his "Laws"</note> he writes, “the most divine **LOGOS** <note>original: "LOGOS"; a Greek term meaning "word," "reason," or "plan," often used in theology to refer to the second person of the Trinity</note> or **WORD** made the world;” which is similar to what **Philo** <note>original: "Philo"; Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize Greek philosophy with Jewish scripture</note> wrote, and is clearly a Christian truth.

It is not to be wondered at that the ancients wrapped up this doctrine in an obscure and symbolic way of speaking and writing, and in hieroglyphic characters and works, as we have seen. It was communicated to them in the same manner; they did not, and could not, comprehend it any more than we can, but they held it as a precious deposit <term>depositum</term> <note>original: "depositum"; something entrusted to a person's care for safekeeping</note> of sacred wisdom.

We may therefore make this deduction from what has been said: that the Christian doctrine of distinct personalities in the Deity is so far from being contrary to reason (as some would have it), or beyond human reason (as others claim), that it is evidently deducible from it—or at least highly consistent with it—when seriously presented to our reason. And since the ancients undoubtedly held such a notion even from the time of creation, those minds that were of a contemplative nature would embrace it and cultivate it as being the most exalted knowledge we are capable of. Our **Druids** were of such a nature, as all historical accounts agree.

## C H A P. XVI.

*Regarding the third type of patriarchal temples, formed in the shape of a circle with wings. A description of one of this sort on the banks of the Humber River in Lincolnshire. A very remarkable type of burial mounds <term>barrows</term> found there, shaped like beds. This figure of the winged <term>alate</term> circle was called by the Egyptians by the name of **CNEPH**; authors are mistaken in telling us it was the name of God. It is indeed the symbol of the third divine emanation from the Supreme, called the world-soul <note>original: "anima mundi"</note>. **CNEPH** is an oriental word, from *canaph*, meaning "to fly" <note>original: כנף (kanaph)</note>. The entire symbol—circle, snake, and wings—was called **CNEPHPTHA**. **Ptha** more particularly meant the serpent, or symbol of the second divine person. The ancients held the Supreme to be ineffable as well as invisible; therefore, they symbolized him by the circle. The **Neptune** of the Greeks derived from **CNEPH**; a circle <note>original: דניא (dunia)</note> added to **CNEPH** signifies a winged circle <note>original: "circulus alatus"</note>. He was the ruler of the waters, based on Genesis 1:2: "and the Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters." This explains why this temple was set on the edge of the Humber. Regarding the Egyptian **Canopus**. Another of these winged temples on Navestock Common in Essex. The word *ganaph* is preserved in the name of the town. *Knave*, *gnavus*, and *knap* (a Teutonic word) all derive from Hebrew. Mr. **Toland** mentions a winged temple of the Druids in the Hebrides islands, but does not entirely understand it. Regarding **Abaris** the Hyperborean Druid, a friend of **Pythagoras**. Evidence that the directional property of the magnetic needle was known in ancient times. An explanation of the "bed-style" burial mounds on the banks of the Humber. A metaphysical discussion concerning the Druids' knowledge of a third emanation or divine person from the Supreme—a truth agreeable to reason. This was the **Mercury** of the ancients as well as **Neptune**. The names which the Druids gave to the three divine persons. Conclusion: they were, in effect, Christians.*

When I wrote my *Itinerary* <note>original: "Itinerary"; Stukeley's "Itinerarium Curiosum," an account of his travels through Britain</note>, I traveled a great deal along the *Hermine Street* road and the *Fosse Way* road, having Mr. **Samuel Buck** <note>Samuel Buck (1696–1779) was an English engraver known for his "Views" of ruins and towns</note> in my company. At that time, I encouraged him to take up the work which he has so praiseworthily pursued, and he has saved the memory of countless antiquities...

I have contributed to the knowledge of the antiquities in our island through that collection of elegant prints which I have published. When we were on the banks of the **Humber**, the name of **Barrow** invited my curiosity. It was fully rewarded by finding that most noble antiquity of the old Druids upon the marsh, called **Humber's Castle**.

<margin>Table 38</margin> A small stream <term>rivulet</term> rises near the town of **Barrow**. When it flows down from the high ground and enters the level marshes on the **Humber** shore, it turns a mill. Right there, upon the edge of the marsh on a gentle rise <term>eminence</term> that is nearly overflowed by high spring tides, and situated between the salt and fresh water, is the work we are to speak of. It is made of great banks of earth thrown up in an unusual manner, which gives it the name of "castle." I observed all around it, and in the adjacent marshes, many long burial mounds <term>tumuli</term> of different sizes, but all of a particular shape that I had never seen elsewhere, being formed like a bed. I immediately set to work digging into several of them, and we found burnt bones, ashes, bits of urns, and similar materials, all extremely rotten and decayed. These were the exact same appearances as I had so often seen when digging the burial mounds <term>barrows</term> around **Stonehenge** and **Abury**.

This satisfied me that the work must belong to the most ancient inhabitants of the island, despite its unusual form. When I attentively considered those banks and made a plan of them, I was very agreeably surprised to discover their purpose and meaning: they were intended to represent the winged circle <note>original: "circulus alatus"</note>. This is an ancient hieroglyph well known to those particularly familiar with **Egyptian** monuments, and which they rightly call the symbol of the world-soul <note>original: "anima mundi"</note>, or the spirit pervading the universe—in truth, the divine spirit.

I had no hesitation in identifying this as a temple of the Druids. Every imaginable reason agreed. Although they made this work with mounds of earth instead of stones—I suppose for lack of stones lying on the surface of the ground—it constitutes the third kind of Druid temple which I proposed to describe. Its main axis <term>vertical line</term> runs northeast and southwest, with the upper part facing directly northeast. The burial mounds <term>barrows</term> generally follow this line, being either on it or at right angles to it; the head of the burial mound is sometimes facing one way, sometimes the other.

The circle was 120 cubits in diameter. The wings were 100 cubits broad and 150 long, but the eastern wing was more extended than the other. This is because the design is somewhat in perspective, as is sometimes seen on **Egyptian antiquities**.

This very extraordinary work, which I could not admire enough, has often occupied my thoughts. We see a consistency in human nature throughout all ages. We build our churches, especially cathedrals, in the shape of a cross—the symbol or emblem of Christianity. The first builders of churches did so using the symbol of the deity, which was depicted with great judgment, and that (most likely) from the beginning of the world.

The circle and wings were the image of the deity, which the old **Egyptian** priests <term>hierophants</term> called **CNEPH**. Since there were three varieties of this figure, they had more than one name for it—meaning the whole figure of the circle, serpent, and wings. Sometimes they used one word, sometimes another, and sometimes combined them. **Eusebius**, in his *Preparation for the Gospel* <note>original: "pr. ev." (Praeparatio Evangelica)</note>, Book 3, Chapter 3, writes "that the **Egyptians** painted God, whom they called **Kneph**, like a man in a blue garment, holding a circle and serpent" (not a scepter, for no such figure...

<summary>Stukeley describes his discovery of an ancient earthwork temple at Barrow-on-Humber, which he identifies as a Druidic "winged circle" representing the divine spirit (Cneph), similar to Egyptian iconography.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "hierogramma"—the sacred symbol of the circle, snake, and wings—by identifying it with the Egyptian deity Cneph (or Cneptha). The author explores the etymology of these names and their connection to other ancient mythologies, including Persian, Greek, and Roman traditions, to argue that these diverse cultures shared a common religious origin.</meta>

# DESCRIBED.

“...figure ever appears) and on his head, feathers or wings.” Now this very figure is seen on the portals of the <term>Persian</term> temple of <term>Chilminar</term> <note>original: Chilminar; modern: Persepolis, the ancient ceremonial capital of the Persian Empire</note>. Authors are not sufficiently accurate in these matters, for want of a more perfect knowledge of them. <term>Cnepth</term> is properly the winged <term>alate</term> circle <note>original: alate circle; "alate" means having wings</note>; yet sometimes they call the whole figure by that name. Similarly, a feather or two, or wings, are often placed on the heads of the <term>Egyptian</term> deities; but the picture mentioned above at <term>Chilminar</term> has the wings annexed to the circle, as is more common.

<term>Phtha</term> was another name for one of these figures, which they sometimes joined to the preceding one to create the word <term>Cneptha</term>. <term>Kircher</term> <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit scholar and polymath who published extensive, though often speculative, works on Egyptology</note> erroneously calls it <term>Hemptha</term>; for before him, <term>Iamblichus</term> <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD)</note> erred in calling <term>Cnepth</term>, <term>Emepth</term>. <term>Strabo</term> calls <term>Cnepth</term> by the name <term>Cnuphis</term> and says his temple was at <term>Syene</term> in book 17. Undoubtedly, it was a temple shaped somewhat like this form. <term>Athenagoras</term>, in *Erotics VI*, calls him <term>Cnepheus</term> <note>original: Κνεφαιοσ</note> and says, “he cannot be seen by our eyes, nor comprehended by our mind.” <term>Hesychius</term>, and the etymologist <term>Suidas</term>, under the word for darkness <note>original: νεφας (nephas)</note>, interpret the word as obscure, hidden, and not to be seen or understood. <term>Iamblichus</term> and <term>Proclus</term> say the same, making <term>Amûn</term> and <term>Phtha</term> the same, as in <term>Proverbs 8:30</term>. The truth is, the word <term>Cnepth</term> comes from the <term>Hebrew</term> word <term>ganaph</term> <note>original: ענף (ganaph)</note>, meaning "to fly" <note>original: volare</note>, and <term>kanaph</term> <note>original: קנף (kanaph)</note>, meaning "a wing," as in <term>Psalm 18:11</term>: *He rode upon the <term>cherubim</term>, and did fly.*

<term>Phtha</term>, called <term>Phthas</term> <note>original: Φθας</note> in <term>Suidas</term>, is derived—on the authority of <term>Kircher</term> and <term>Huetius</term>—from the <term>Hebrew</term> <term>chabat</term> <note>original: חבת</note>, which is the same as the <term>Greek</term> word <term>peitha</term> <note>original: πειθα</note>, meaning "to persuade" <note>original: suade</note>, or <term>suada</term> in <term>Latin</term>. It refers more particularly to the serpent, which is the emblem of eloquence and the divine **WORD**. *In <term>Arabic</term>, it signifies the* **son**. Thus, <term>Cneptha</term> signifies the entire figure: the circle, snake, and wings. The *Supreme Being had no name*. They held him to be ineffable as well as invisible. For this reason, they called the <term>Jehovah</term> of the <term>Jews</term> an uncertain or unknown deity, or the deity without a name. <term>Herodotus</term>, in *Euterpe*, writes that "he heard from the priests of <term>Dodona</term> that the ancient <term>Pelasgians</term> made their prayers and sacrifices to the deity without any name or surname, for at that time they knew none." <term>Iamblichus</term>'s interpretation of <term>Phtha</term> is very little different. He says, “It signifies him who performs all things in truth, and without lying.” The <term>Egyptians</term> called this <term>Phtha</term> by the name <term>Vulcan</term> and say he was the son of the supreme God. <term>Cicero</term> identifies him as the guardian god of <term>Egypt</term> and the author of all their philosophy, according to <term>Diogenes Laertius</term> in his preface. This is that most ancient deity of the <term>Egyptians</term> who was particularly represented by the serpent. The <term>Greeks</term> make <term>Vulcan</term> the only son of <term>Juno</term>, born without the help of her husband. Again, they describe <term>Pallas</term> <note>Athena</note> as being produced out of <term>Jupiter</term>’s brain; she wore the <term>Aegis</term>, or snaky breastplate, which originally was nothing other than our great **prophylactic hierogramma** <note>a "protective sacred symbol"—the author argues the circle and snake symbol was used for spiritual protection</note>, the circle and snake, used by the most ancient warriors as a sacred preservative. <term>Medusa</term>’s head is exactly the same: a circle, wings, and snakes. However, the refined <term>Greeks</term> redesigned it, transforming the circle into a beautiful face that better suited their artistic tastes. The legend of it turning men into stone actually refers to the construction of our serpentine temples in that form by the first heroes, who bore this symbol <term>cognizance</term> on their shields.

But to return to **CNEPH**, the deity to whom these winged temples are dedicated. It became the chief and most famous name. For this reason, <term>Porphyry</term>, in <term>Eusebius</term>'s *Preparation for the Gospel* (III. 11), calls this <term>Cnepth</term> the creator. <term>Plutarch</term>, in *On Isis and Osiris*, testifies: “the inhabitants of <term>Thebais</term>, or the remotest part of <term>Egypt</term>, worshipped only the eternal God <term>Cnepth</term>, and paid nothing toward...”

<summary>This page analyzes the Egyptian deity Cneph (or Cneptha), identifying him as the creator god symbolized by the winged circle and serpent. The author links this "hierogramma" to various ancient cultures, interpreting the Aegis of Athena and Medusa's head as stylized versions of these serpentine temple designs.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that ancient, remote regions like Britain and the Egyptian Thebaid preserved a pure, original form of religion—a "patriarchal tradition"—that predates later pagan idolatry. Stukeley links the Egyptian deity Cneph (the "winged circle") to the Greek Neptune and finds evidence for this connection in the layout of the Druid temple at Barrow on the Humber.</meta>

# ABURY

“...the charge of idolatrous worship in the other parts of that kingdom.”

Thus we see that those countries farthest separated from the busy part of the world, such as **Thebais** <note>The region around Thebes in ancient Egypt</note> and **Britain**, retained the pure and ancient religion. Bishop **Cumberland** also asserts this in *Sanchoniatho*, page 15, regarding **Thebais** before **Abraham’s** time. **Strabo** <note>An ancient Greek geographer and historian</note> says, “there was a temple of **Cnuphis** (as he writes it) at **Syene**, the farthest part of **Thebais**.” This must be understood as one of our winged temples originally, though probably it was later built upon, covered, and became a site of idolatry. “Hence the **Ethiopians**, neighbors to those of **Thebais**, still living in the upper regions of **Egypt**,” says **Strabo**, “worship two gods: one the immortal creator, and the other a mortal who has no name, nor is easily understood.” Here we find they have a notion of the Supreme Being and his son. 

Their opposite neighbors across the Red Sea worshipped only two gods: **Zeus and Dionysus** <note>original: τον Διον και τον Διονυσον; ton Dion kai ton Dionysion</note>, or **Jupiter and Jupiter of Nysa** <note>original: Jovem & Jovem Nysæum</note>—that is, **God and the God of Nysa**. This is what is meant by the two principles of **Pythagoras**, mentioned by **Plutarch** in *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*: **unity** and **indefinite duality**, the sacred **Dyad** of **Plato**. Consequently, **Diodorus Siculus** writes in his first book, “that the **Egyptians** declared there were two first eternal Gods.” These they expressed by the names of **unity** and **duality**. 

I do not believe that they found this out by their own understanding and reasoning, but received it from **patriarchal tradition**. Then, their own reasoning would confirm it, for it is altogether agreeable to reason when arguing from the productivity of the First Cause. The **Greeks** turned **Cneph** into **Neptune**, the sovereign of the waters, based on what the **Hebrew legislator** <note>Moses</note> writes in the beginning of his **cosmogony** <note>the story of the origin of the universe; here, the Book of Genesis</note>: “and the spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters.” The word **Neptune** comes from **Cneph** and **Dunia** <note>original: דוניא; a Hebrew/Semitic term for world, orb, or circle</note>, meaning **the winged circle**. 

This probably gives us some insight into why we find our winged temple of **Barrow** upon the banks of that noble estuary, the **Humber**. I wondered indeed how it happened that the **Druids** should so studiously place this work under the edge of the high land and upon the brink of the salt marsh, so that every high tide washes or overflows the edges of it, while the freshwater brook runs close beside it. At the time of its construction, it must have presented them with the agreeable picture of the sacred **hieroglyphic** <note>The symbol of the winged circle/snake</note> hovering over both fresh and salt water.

I observed a line, or a little bank and ditch, cast up above our figure, which I judged was intended to keep off the flooding of the ocean during times of sacrifice. These sacrifices seem to have been performed within that enclosed area where I have placed the figure of the compass in the engraved view. Likewise, just outside that line to the east, I noticed three little square plots, which perhaps were the habitations of the **Druids** who were the keepers of the temple.

It is relevant to take notice of one of the greatest fixed stars in the heavens, at the bottom of the constellation called the **Ship** <note>Argo Navis</note>, which has the name **Canopus**. This is none other than our word **Cneph**. This star was given this name by the **Egyptians** because it appeared to them just above the edge of the southern horizon. In their celestial spheres, we may very well presume they painted it as a winged circle, because it always appeared as if it were hovering over the horizon or the great ocean.

->*O deity of the waters,*<- <note>original: O numen aquarum</note>
->*To whom the power next to heaven fell, Neptune.*<- <note>original: Proxima cui cælo cessit, Neptune, potestas. Ovid, Metamorphoses Book IV</note>

So that originally the ancients understood the spirit or soul of the universe,

He connects these ideas to Pythagorean philosophy, Egyptian astronomy (the star Canopus), and patriarchal traditions preserved in remote lands like Britain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Stukeley's argument that ancient "winged" and "serpentine" temples were built as monuments to great heroes of the post-Flood era. He links these structures to classical mythology, suggesting that myths about figures like Orpheus and Memnon turning into serpents or birds were actually metaphorical descriptions of the shape of their burial monuments.</meta>

## DESCRIBED.

...or more properly the divine spirit, by this figure which they call [the winged circle], which the European nations called **Neptune**, sovereign of the [sea]. He was often called by the poets the *Earth-shaker* <note>original: Ένωχιχθων, Ένοσιχθων; Enōchichthōn, Enosichthōn</note>; for the "waters" in the writings of **Moses** refer to the <term>Hyle</term> <note>original: Hyle; the "moist matter" or primordial substance of chaos from which the universe was created</note>, or the moist matter of chaos from which the universe was made.

Two of the quarterly solemnities or general sacrifices of the **Druids** were held on the two equinoxes, which are the times of the highest tides. A curious observer staying at these locations for several years together at these times might possibly make some notable discovery concerning the change in the sea level over the last 5,000 or 6,000 years. For I am convinced this temple was made by the very first inhabitants of the island, not long after the Great Flood, to serve as the burial place of some great hero who advanced so far in peopling the country. And if our reasoning and evidence so far are at all close to the truth, we may identify the types of many of these most ancient temples built at the burial sites of heroes mentioned in the writings of those times. For instance, we infer a serpentine temple was built at the <term>tumulus</term> <note>burial mound</note> of **Orpheus**, based on the fable of a serpent attempting to devour his head, which was then turned into stone.

> Here a wild serpent attacks the head exposed on the foreign sands, and licks the hair dripping with dew, and gapes to tear the face that used to sing hymns. At last **Apollo** <note>original: Phoebus</note> appears, and as the serpent prepares to bite, he freezes it, hardening its wide jaws just as they were. <note>original: *Hic ferus expositum peregrinis anguibus arenis / Os petit, & sparsos stillanti rore capillos / Lambit, & hymniferos inhiat divellere vultus. / Tandem Phoebus adest, morsusque inferre parentem / Congelat, & patulos, ut erant, indurat hiatus.* (Ovid, Metamorphoses, 11.56–60)</note>

Again, we may reasonably suppose that an alate <note>winged</note> temple was built by the tomb of **Memnon**, who was said to be buried in **Phrygia**. He was supposedly turned into a bird on his funeral pyre at the request of his mother, **Aurora** <note>the goddess of the dawn</note>. We see hints of this even in **Ovid**'s telling of the story. This was done at the request of **Aurora**, who petitions **Jupiter** for this favor for her son, though she desires none for herself. Thus she begins:

> Inferior to all the goddesses whom the golden sky supports (for I have very few temples throughout the whole world), yet I come as a goddess: not so that you might give me shrines, or days of sacrifice, or altars to be warmed by fires, etc. <note>original: *Omnibus inferior, quas sustinet aureus æther / (Nam mihi sunt totum rarissima templa per orbem,) / Diva tamen venio: non ut delubra, diesque / Des mihi sacrificos, caliturasque ignibus aras, &c.* (Ovid, Metamorphoses, 13.591–594)</note>

He was turned into a bird, and a flock of the same birds, called **Memnonian birds** <note>original: *Aves Memnoniæ*</note>, arose from the same funeral pyre. They immediately divided into two companies and fought until they destroyed each other. It is said that a similar flight of the same birds came from **Ethiopia** on the same day every year, circled his monument three times, and then divided and fought in honor of their ancestor.

What can we understand by this except an assembly of his people and descendants gathering to celebrate his anniversary, as was the ancient custom for great men? The story is entirely consistent with the one told about **Cadmus** and must be interpreted in the same way.

In this sense, we should understand authors when they tell us that **Cadmus** built a temple to **Neptune** on the island of **Rhodes**. This was not a covered temple with elegant pillars, nor an idolatrous one (which were developments of later times), but one of our winged temples. **Phut** <note>The biblical grandson of Noah, often associated with the settlement of North Africa and the Mediterranean</note> had built a <term>Dracontium</term> <note>a temple in the shape of a serpent</note> there previously.

->**Antoninus**<-

<summary>Stukeley argues that ancient myths about heroes turning into animals were actually descriptions of the architectural forms—serpentine or winged—of their burial monuments. He suggests these "alate" and "dracontic" temples, like those at Avebury, represent the original, pure religion of the first post-Flood settlers.</summary>
<keywords>Abury, Avebury, Druids, Neptune, Orpheus, Memnon, Cadmus, Phut, alate temple, Dracontium, tumulus, Ovid, mythology, biblical archaeology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Stukeley’s exploration of "winged" temples (Cneph), connecting British megalithic sites with ancient Egyptian, Greek, and Italian traditions. He argues that the "winged" design is a universal ancient symbol for the divine spirit or creator, found across various cultures.</meta>

# ABURY

Antoninus Liberalis (in Chapter 12) speaks of Lake <term>Canopus</term>, which I believe was named after a <term>Cneph</term> or winged temple near it. It was built by the hero <term>Cygnus</term>, the son of <term>Phut</term>, "who, the legend says, was turned into a bird there," and the burial monument of <term>Phylius</term> was nearby. In this same sense, <term>Strabo</term> (Book II) mentions <term>Hercules</term> being called <term>Canopus</term> because he built such a temple. We can now understand that previously mysterious <term>Egyptian</term> artifact called a <term>Canopus</term>—a lidded vase they used for preserving water in their temples and homes. To ensure a heavenly blessing upon this essential element, they often marked it with the sacred protective symbol of the <term>Kneph</term> or "winged circle" <note>original: circulus alatus</note>, which is the equivalent of the Greek <term>Neptune</term>, the "ruler of the waters" <note>original: dominator aquarum</note>. Many of these vases still remain in the collections of antiquarians; one such example is illustrated by <term>Kircher</term>.

Incidentally, I should mention that some of these vases are decorated with a <term>scarabeus</term> (beetle) with spread wings, which carries the exact same meaning as the winged circle. However, this is not the place to discuss these matters at length.

I suspect the names <term>Geneva</term> and <term>Geneffa</term> come from such temples, as does <term>Gnaphthalus</term>, a bird mentioned by <term>Aristotle</term>. <term>Simias</term> of <term>Rhodes</term> celebrates our <term>Cneph</term> in his poem written in the shape of wings, portraying him as the source of motion and creation. This is where we get the word <term>Nebula</term> (cloud <note>original: νέφέλη; nephele</note>) and perhaps <term>Nebulo</term> <note>original: nebulo; a Latin term for a "shadowy person" or scoundrel</note>.

In 1725—the year after I discovered the <term>Humber</term> temple and the final year of my travels—I found another of these winged temples on <term>Navestock Common</term> in <term>Essex</term>. It seems to be from a later date than the others, perhaps from a time when the original doctrine concerning these theological ideas was somewhat forgotten. This is because the temple is located on a dry common, nowhere near water, yet the shape is exactly the same. What is extremely remarkable about this noble antiquity on <term>Navestock Common</term> is that the name has survived to this day, confirming everything we said before regarding their name and meaning. <term>Navestock</term> must have been named after some ancient and notable tree (likely an oak) standing upon or beside the <term>C N E P H</term>, or winged temple; hence "Knave-stock." Our English word <term>knave</term>, which originally carried no negative meaning, signifies the same thing: "winged" or "diligent" <note>original: alatus, impiger</note>. The Latin word <term>gnavus</term> means the same, as does the <term>Germanic</term> word <term>knap</term>. All these derive from the original Hebrew.

I have no doubt that there are more such temples in the <term>British</term> Isles called "<term>Knaves-castles</term>" or similar names. I recall seeing one on a large, heathy common near the <term>Roman Watling Street</term> in <term>Staffordshire</term>. Also, <term>Mr. Toland</term> notes a winged temple of our <term>Druids</term> in the <term>Hebrides</term> or "Hyperborean" islands of <term>Shetland</term>. <term>Abaris</term>, a <term>Druid</term> from this region who was hungry for knowledge, traveled to <term>Greece</term> where philosophy flourished. After that, he went to <term>Pythagoras</term> in <term>Italy</term> and became his favorite disciple. <term>Pythagoras</term> shared his greatest philosophical ideas with him—which the writers of those times, in their cryptic way, perhaps describe as "showing him his golden thigh." <term>Abaris</term>, in return, gave <term>Pythagoras</term> "<term>Apollo’s arrow</term>," which he had brought from his own country where it had been kept in a winged temple. They further claim that <term>Abaris</term> flew on this arrow through the air to <term>Greece</term>. This story undoubtedly comes from the belief people held that the <term>Druids</term> practiced magic.

After what I wrote in *Stonehenge* concerning the magnetic needle, I cannot help but think that this "arrow of <term>Apollo</term>" used by <term>Abaris</term> on his journey from <term>Shetland</term> to <term>Greece</term> was actually a magnetic instrument of this sort, which

<summary>Stukeley identifies "winged temples" (Cneph) across the ancient world, linking British sites like Navestock Common to Egyptian Canopus vases and the legend of the Druid Abaris. He suggests that Abaris’s mythical flying arrow was actually a precursor to the magnetic compass.</summary>
<keywords>Cneph, Canopus, Navestock Common, Abaris the Druid, Pythagoras, Apollo's Arrow, magnetism, etymology of Knave, winged temples, Druidry</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>The author continues discussing the "arrow of Apollo" and its potential connection to the magnet or compass, linking ancient mythology to the Druidic and biblical past.</meta>

### DESCRIBED.

...which the *Hyperborean* <note>a mythical people of the far north</note> sage gave to *Pythagoras*. Because the *Druids* possessed such a secret as this, they would naturally create and favor the notion that they were *practicing magic*. Calling it *Apollo's* arrow seems to trace the possession of it back to *Phut*, the most famous navigator whom we discussed previously. Indeed, it seems we may trace it even higher, even to *Noah* himself. *Sanchoniathon*, the *Phoenician* writer, tells us—among other remarkable things concerning *Ouranus*, who is certainly *Noah*—"that he devised *Baetylia* <note>original: "Batulia"; sacred stones, often meteorites, believed to be divine</note>, or contrived stones that moved as if they had life."

Besides the interpretation we may naturally give to these "anointed stones" or *main ambres* <note>original: "main ambres"; likely referring to 'maen' (stone) and 'ambres' (sacred or rocking stones)</note>, we may well judge that the knowledge of the magnet is understood here. They would first place it in a little boat in a vessel of water, and then it would move itself until it pointed to the quarters of the heavens. *Athenaeus* in *The Banquet of the Learned* <note>original: "Deipnosoph."</note> affirms that *Hercules* borrowed the golden cup with which he sailed over the ocean from *Nereus*. *Nereus* is *Japheth*, the eldest son of *Noah*, and the golden cup was, in all probability, a compass box.

Among the ancient constellations pictured on the celestial globe is the Arrow. It is said by *Eratosthenes*, the most ancient writer we have on the *Catasterisms* <note>original: "Catoæterisms"; a work describing how mythological figures became stars</note>, to be the arrow of *Apollo*, which was kept in the winged temple among the *Hyperboreans*. *Diodorus Siculus*, citing *Hecataeus* and other older writers, shows that the *Hyperborean* island was in the ocean beyond *Gaul* <note>modern-day France</note>, to the north, under the Great Bear constellation, where the people lived a most simple and happy life. *Orpheus* places them near the *Cronian* sea; a word that is purely *Irish*, as Mr. *Toland* shows, for *Croin* signifies "frozen." He shows further, and at great length, that the Hebrides islands—*Skye*, *Lewis*, *Harris*, and *Shetland*—are the true *Hyperborean* islands of the ancients. Among them, therefore, was the winged temple; whether it was made of mounds of earth like other Druid temples, such as the one on *Navestock Common*, or made of stones like other Druid temples.

There are other Druid temples in those islands made of stones, and I shall provide an illustration of one in my next volume. Furthermore, there is a famous one in *Cornwall* commonly called the *Hurlers*, which I believe was one of our "state temples," made of upright stones.

The learned *Bayer*, in his fine designs of the celestial constellations, represents the arrow of *Apollo* mentioned before as a magnetic needle, based on a very ancient book of drawings. I also observe that the Isle of *Skye*, in the language of the natives, is called *Scianach*, which signifies "winged." In that place, probably, was the winged temple we are speaking of, which gave the island its name.

We mentioned before that *Phut* married *Rhoda*, from whom the Isle of *Rhodes* took its name. *Rod* in the *Psalms* and the *Prophets* signifies a snake. Indeed, *Pliny*, in Book VII, Chapter 56 of his *Natural History*, asserts that the island was originally called *Ophiusa* <note>from the Greek for "Snake Island"</note>, a word with an equivalent meaning. It is most likely they built a serpentine temple there, which gave it that name. So too the Isle of *Tenos*, which *Bochart* shows means a "serpent" in the oriental language, was also called *Hydrusa* and *Ophiusa*. The Isle of *Cyprus* was called *Ophiodia* by *Nicenetus*. So was *Hydra*, an island just off *Carthage*, which was first built by *Cadmus*. There are also the *Ophiades Islands* on the *Arabian* coast of the *Red Sea*. *Pausanias* mentions a place called *Opheos Cephale* <note>original: "Opheos Cephale"; Greek for "Serpent's Head"</note>, or the serpent's head, which is the same as our *Hakpen* on *Overton Hill* in *Avebury*.

In the Isle of *Chios* is a famous mountain higher than the rest called *Pelineus*, which undoubtedly had one of our great *Dracontian* <note>original: "Dracontian"; referring to temples laid out in the shape of a serpent</note> temples.

<summary>The author argues that ancient myths regarding "Apollo's arrow" and "living stones" actually describe the early discovery of the magnetic compass. He identifies the British Hebrides as the mythical Hyperborea and links various island names to "serpent" temples similar to those found in England.</summary>

# AVEBURY

The learned Samuel Bochart <note>original: "Bochart I. 9."; a 17th-century French scholar of biblical geography</note> shows that its name signifies the "enormous serpent," and a story of that nature is attached to it. Indeed, this famous temple gave its name to the whole island of Britain. He demonstrates that it is a Syrian word, חויא *Chivia* <note>Aramaic for "serpent"</note>, meaning a serpent; thus, the isle of Chios is the "serpent's isle." The word is the same as "Hivite." It is probable that Cadmus <note>the legendary Phoenician prince who introduced the alphabet to the Greeks</note> or some of his people built it. Hesychius and Phavorinus mention Jupiter *Pelineus* as the name of the deity worshipped there.

In the second book of the *Aeneid*, Virgil describes the two serpents that destroyed Laocoön coming from the island of Tenedos.

<margin>
PLATE
XXXIX.
</margin>

I described the burial mounds <term>original: "barrows"</term> around Humber's Castle as being shaped like beds. They are all "long barrows" of very different lengths, higher at the head than at the feet (if we may put it that way), and featuring a cavity along their entire length that tapers off at the feet to the level of the turf. Consequently, they represent the impression of a person who has lain on a very soft, downy couch. One that I excavated near the temple was 60 cubits long; the other two near it were 40 cubits each (see Plate 39). The sight of them naturally brought to my mind the *eune* <note>original: "ευνη"; Greek for "couch" or "bed"</note> or couch of Typhon or Phut, which Homer says was in Arima <note>a legendary mountain region</note>. It is natural for us to imagine that Homer means exactly such a *tumulus* <note>burial mound</note> of a hero as these we are discussing.

Phut was a great Arch-Druid or patriarchal high priest, being the head of his family. According to my view of the matter, these long barrows all belong to some of the higher order of the Druids. Eustathius interprets Homer’s word as *taphos* <note>original: "ταφος"; Greek for "tomb"</note>. Stephanus, the commentator on Hesiod’s *Theogony*, identifies Arima as a mountain in Cilicia or Lydia where Typhon's *koite* <note>original: "κοιτη"; Greek for "resting place" or "bed"</note> is located. (See Oppian, Lucan, Apollonius, Strabo, Mela). Pausanias, in his *Description of Attica*, tells us of the burial mound of Hippolyta the Amazon, noting that it was made in the shape of an Amazonian *pelta* <note>a crescent-shaped shield</note>; perhaps it was somewhat like our own burial mounds.

In the early days of idolatry, people also consecrated Hermes the Egyptian as the god Mercury. However, the Egyptians viewed Mercury in a different light than the Canaanites did: the Egyptians made him the god of divine wisdom, whereas the Canaanites, who were immersed in trade and traffic, made him the god of profit and gain in the person of their ancestor, Canaan. Nevertheless, they knew of the Holy Spirit prior to the rise of idolatry; many believe that Mercury was not a mortal man (as Saint Augustine notes in *The City of God*, Book 8, Chapter 26). Orpheus, in his hymn to him, declares him to be of the race of Dionysus, by whom "Jehovah" is understood.

I suppose that when Canaan died, he had a "winged" <term>original: "alate"; referring to the serpent-and-wings layout of the temple</term> temple built around his place of burial. In later times, this led his descendants to deify him under the name of Mercury. Similarly, I suppose the same was done over the burial mound of the patriarch Tarshish <note>original: "TARSIS"; a grandson of Noah associated with the sea</note>. In idolatrous times, this provided a pretext to consecrate him as the Neptune of the heathens. In effect, Neptune is the same as Mercury, except that because they were worshipped by people of different characters and temperaments, they divided one god into two.

Thus, we have sailed through a wide ocean of antiquities, and not without a compass. We have placed ancient matters transmitted to us in writing in parallel with what we can now see here at home, in such a way that clearly shows them to be the same.

He argues that pagan gods like Mercury and Neptune were actually deified versions of biblical ancestors like Canaan and Tarshish.</summary>

<meta>The author continues his theological interpretation of the temple at Avebury, connecting the Druidic concept of a "winged" serpent temple to ancient ideas of the Trinity and the "soul of the world."</meta>

# DESCRIBED.

I shall conclude with what we may very well imagine to have been the reasoning <note>original: "ratiocination"</note> of the Druids among one another. In their theological contemplations concerning this last kind of their works—these winged temples—their speculations would be of such a sort during their serious scrutiny into the nature of the deity.

We observed that the Druids, in their theological studies, must have found (along with other Eastern wise men) two ways that the supreme being exerts his almighty power: by multiplying himself—as the *Zoroastrians*, the *Pythagoreans*, and the *Platonists* call it, or "divine generation" <term>original: "geniture"; the process of the Son being begotten by the Father</term>—and by creation. The first was necessary and therefore done before time began; the second was by his own will <note>original: "arbitrary"</note> and therefore done within time. Nevertheless, this second act was fit and proper to be done, and therefore necessarily performed. For whatever is fitting for an all-perfect being, we may pronounce as a necessity for him.

The Druids would advance still further in their contemplations this way and conclude that it was fitting for the supreme—and therefore necessary—for him to exert his power in all possible ways and modes of acting. They would believe he was not content with producing a single divine person or emanation from himself from the infinite fund of his own fruitfulness; rather, he was pleased to proceed to that other mode of acting which we call "divine procession" <term>the theological concept of the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father and the Son</term>, or a third divine person proceeding from the first and second. The ancients had knowledge of this person and styled him the *soul of the world* <note>original: "anima mundi"</note>, "that spirit of the Lord which fills the world" (*Wisdom* 1:7). They made him a distinct person from God, the supreme, but more immediately, he was the author of life to all living things. And this spirit he spread throughout the whole macrocosm <term>the entire universe or "great world"</term>. I need only quote *Virgil*, among many others, from his fine poem, the *Georgics*, book 4:

> They have said that bees possess a share of the divine mind
> And breathe ethereal drafts. For God, they say, passes through all 
> The lands, the expanses of the sea, and the deep heavens. 
> From here the flocks, the herds, men, and every race of wild beasts—
> Each at birth draws their thin, fleeting lives;
> Surely, all things are then returned to him and, being dissolved,
> Are brought back there.
> <note>original Latin: "Esse apibus partem divinae mentis & haustus / Aethereos dixere. Deum namque ire per omnes / Terrasque tractusque maris, coelumque profundum. / Hinc pecudes, armenta, viros, genus omne ferarum, / Quemque sibi tenues nascentem arcessere vitas; / Scilicet huc reddi deinde & resoluta referri, / Omnia."</note>

---

<margin>PLATE XL.</margin>

The ancients pictured this divine mind, or *soul of the world*, using the symbol of a circle and wings. In their symbolic language, this meant the holy spirit, or the spirit proceeding from the fountain of divinity. We see this symbol countless times on *Egyptian* and other ancient monuments. *Plutarch*, in his "Platonic Questions," asks: "Why should Plato in his *Phaedrus* say that the nature of a wing, which lifts heavy things upward, is the primary participant in those things that surround the body of the deity?"

But the Druids would reason in this way: there are three modes of divine origin and existence quite different from creation. They are these: the self-existent, unoriginated first cause; divine generation; and divine procession. All are equal in nature (excepting self-origination) and all are equally necessarily existent. When the supreme being produces his likeness, it must be divine sonship <note>original: "filiation"</note>; thus, the Son of God is produced. Divine procession must come from both of them, but it cannot possibly be another sonship. For besides the fact that in these acts of divinity we must discard all ideas similar to human reproduction, it would be absurd to call this "generation." This is because, as it is done prior to all notions of time or eternity itself, it would make the Son to be both Son and

<summary>This page explores the Druidic theology behind Avebury's "winged" serpent design, arguing that the Druids shared the ancient belief in a Trinity consisting of a First Cause, a generated Son, and a proceeding Holy Spirit (the "soul of the world").</summary>

<keywords>Druid theology, Trinity, soul of the world, anima mundi, Virgil, Georgics, Plato, Plutarch, divine procession, divine generation, symbolism, winged circle</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>The author continues his theological interpretation of the Druids, arguing that their understanding of the Trinity involved a "procession" of a third divine person from the Father and the Son, mirroring Christian doctrine.</meta>

# ABURY

...the Father in the same act. Therefore, no other term remains for this than "procession" from the Father and the Son.

Whether these abstract and metaphysical ideas would occur to a mind completely unfamiliar with any doctrine of this sort is doubtful. However, when presented to a serious and thoughtful mind, they would be embraced and refined as being consistent with reason and as a step toward the most sublime and useful knowledge of all: the nature of God.

2. The very learned Schedius, in his treatise *On the Customs of the Germans* <note>original: "de mor. germ."</note> (Chapter 24), discusses the Druids and strongly confirms everything we have said on this subject. He writes that they carefully search for a large and handsome oak tree that grows with two main branches forming the shape of a cross alongside the upright trunk. If the two horizontal branches are not naturally shaped this way, they fasten a crossbeam to it. They consecrate the tree in this manner: On the right branch, they carve the word **HESUS** into the bark in clear characters; on the middle or upright trunk, the word **TARAMIS**; and on the left branch, **BELENUS**. Above this, where the branches extend, they carve the name of God, **THAU**, and they repeat the same name, **THAU**, at the bottom of the tree.

We must understand that by doing this, they intended to show the unity of the divine nature; for every word signifies "God" emphatically in their general meaning, especially *Thau*. The other three words each have a more specific meaning regarding the "economy" <term>the internal roles and relationships within the Trinity</term> of the Godhead. This is also Schedius’s opinion.

This inscribed tree served as their *kebla* <term>the direction faced during prayer; usually used in reference to Mecca in Islam, but here meaning a focal point</term> within their sacred groves, "cathedrals," or open-air churches. They directed their faces toward it during religious services, just as they did toward the "Ambre" stone or the "Cove" in the temples of **Avebury** <note>original: "Abury"</note> described earlier—similar to how Christians look toward a symbol or painting over an altar. Because of this, later writers got the idea that they worshiped trees, and that these names belonged to many different gods, a concept that poets enjoy expanding upon. However, if we examine their origins, these names can easily be reconciled with orthodox belief.

The word *Hesus* means the supreme God in the Celtic language, just as *ESAR* did among the Etruscans <note>original: "Hetruscans"</note> (as noted by Suetonius in his *Life of Augustus*). It was pronounced "Eisar," just as the Germans pronounce "Caesar" as "Keisar." It comes from the Hebrew <term>אל (El - God)</term>, <term>אד (Ad - Lord)</term>, and <term>שר (Sar - Prince)</term>. The letter <term>ה (He)</term> is used emphatically for the name of the divinity, as in <term>השם (HaShem)</term> or the Greek phrase *the name* <note>original: "το ονομα"</note>, meaning the name **Jehovah** (Leviticus 24:11, 16). This explains the letter <term>ה (He)</term> or *El* inscribed over the door of the temple at Delphi, which Plutarch wrote about. It was the custom of Babylonian monarchs to assume divine names, such as *Esarhaddon*, which means nothing less than "God the Lord." Hesychius says *Esi* means God. In Arabic, it signifies the "Creator," according to Dickenson's *Delphi Phoenicizing*. But these authors do not reach the root of the matter, for it comes from *AS* or *AT*, signifying "God the Father." *Ata* or *Atta* is "Father" in Greek. The Armenians call him *Ad*, the Egyptians *Ot*, and those in Sarmatia and Slavonia *Os*, according to the learned Baxter in his *Glossary of Roman Antiquities* (under "Ascania") <note>original: "gloss. ant. Rom."</note>, where he provides much ancient learning on the subject. This is also the *Atys* of the Phrygians.

*Belenus* is the same as the *Baal* mentioned in Scripture. Originally, this word was used to refer to the true God, **Jehovah**, until it was adopted for idolatry. He is also the *Belus* of the Assyrians. If we examine the word fundamentally, it means "God the Son." In the Babylonian language, <term>בנל (Ben-El)</term> is "the son," and *Belis* <note>original: "Βηλις"</note> is "the daughter." He is the same as the *Apollo* of the Romans.

<summary>The author uses a description of a cross-shaped oak tree inscribed with divine names (Hesus, Taramis, Belenus) to argue that the Druids held a Trinitarian view of God. He attempts to prove this by linking these Celtic names to Hebrew, Greek, and Near Eastern titles for God the Father and God the Son.</summary>
<keywords>Druids, Trinity, Hesus, Taramis, Belenus, Schedius, Avebury, etymology, oak tree, monotheism, cross symbol, Baal</keywords>
